Listening paths

  • The Full Arc — Thirty records, ragtime to now
  • Into the Deep End — Fifteen records that get progressively harder

Ragtime 1895–1919

Written-out piano music where the left hand marches and the right hand refuses to land on the beat.

Sedalia / St. Louis / Missouri river towns, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Rag, Classic ragtime

Put your foot on the floor with the left hand — steady, even, oom-pah, like a march. Now listen to what the right hand does over the top: it keeps arriving a half-beat early, tugging against that floor. That tug is the whole idea, and 'ragged time' is where the name comes from. Ragtime is composed music, published as sheet music and sold by the ton between 1899 and 1917, mostly in multi-strain march form: four separate 16-bar tunes played in sequence, each repeated, often with a key change for the third. It is not improvised, which is why some writers refuse to call it jazz at all. But every early jazz pianist learned it, the New Orleans repertoire was built out of it, and the syncopation habit it drilled into American ears is the precondition for everything downstream. Scott Joplin wanted it treated as concert music and wrote an opera to prove the point; the market wanted novelty numbers and cakewalks. Both things happened.

What to listen for

  • A steady oom-pah left hand that never varies while the melody keeps landing between the beats
  • Four distinctly different tunes inside one piece, each played twice before moving on
  • A key change roughly halfway through, usually up a fourth, when the 'trio' section arrives
  • No drums, no bass, no horns — one piano doing all of it

How it works

harmony
Straightforward European parlour harmony — tonic, dominant, secondary dominants, brisk circle-of-fifths runs. Blues notes are rare; this is not blues-derived music.
rhythm
An unvarying duple left hand under a right hand full of tied-over, off-beat accents. The syncopation is written, not felt in the moment.
form
Multi-strain march form: AABBACCDD or similar, 16 bars per strain, with the C strain ('the trio') usually in the subdominant.
instrumentation
Solo piano first and foremost; also banjo, brass band, and the ragtime orchestras that played from stock arrangements.
improvisation
Essentially none in the classic repertoire. Performers were expected to play the printed notes, and Joplin famously instructed that it never be played fast.
timbre
Bright upright piano, often slightly out of tune, in a room with hard surfaces. Cleanness and evenness of touch were the prized virtues.

Key figures

  • Scott Joplin (piano, composition) — Wrote the rags that defined the classic style and fought to have it heard as art music rather than novelty.
  • James Scott (piano, composition) — The most rhythmically intricate of the classic ragtime composers; his right-hand writing is harder than Joplin's.
  • Joseph Lamb (piano, composition) — The third of the 'big three' and the only white composer among them; wrote the style's most harmonically ambitious pieces.
  • Jelly Roll Morton (piano, composition) — The hinge figure — took rag form and loosened it with swing, blues and what he called the 'Spanish tinge'.

Labels: John Stark & Son (sheet music) · Victor · Nonesuch (1970s revival)

Contested

Whether ragtime is jazz at all. It has no improvisation and no blues content, and Gunther Schuller and others treat it strictly as a precursor. Ragtime scholars often resent its absorption into jazz history entirely. This guide treats it as a root rather than a branch.

Fed into

  • New Orleans Jazz — direct descendant, 1905
  • Stride Piano — direct descendant, 1919

What to hear

gateway

Joshua Rifkin — Piano Rags by Scott Joplin gateway

recorded/released 1970 · Nonesuch H-71248 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Maple Leaf Rag”

Joshua Rifkin (p)

A musicologist played Joplin slowly, quietly and exactly as written, treating the rags as concert repertoire rather than saloon music — which is what Joplin had asked for and never got. It became one of Nonesuch's best-selling records and started the ragtime revival single-handedly.

Listen for: The left hand never varies its oom-pah while the right keeps landing between the beats; that friction is the entire idea, and at Rifkin's unhurried tempo you can hear it clearly.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

core

Scott Joplin / Houston Grand Opera, Gunther Schuller — Treemonisha core

recorded 1975, released 1976 · Deutsche Grammophon 2707 083 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “A Real Slow Drag”

Carmen Balthrop (s) · Betty Allen (ms) · Curtis Rayam (t) · Gunther Schuller (cond)

Joplin's opera, which he could not get staged in his lifetime and which was not fully produced until 1972. It is the evidence for what he thought ragtime was for, and it is nothing like a novelty rag.

Listen for: The ragtime syncopation is there, but it is carrying recitative and chorus writing — listen to how the rhythmic figure survives being put under sung words.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

New England Conservatory Ragtime Ensemble, Gunther Schuller — Scott Joplin: The Red Back Book core

recorded/released 1973 · Angel S-36060 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “The Entertainer”

Gunther Schuller (cond)

Ragtime as most people actually heard it in 1910 — not solo piano but a small orchestra playing from published stock arrangements. Won a Grammy and reframed the music as ensemble repertoire.

Listen for: The four strains are now shared between clarinet, cornet and strings, so you can hear the multi-strain march form as separate tunes rather than as one continuous piece.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Joseph Lamb — Joseph Lamb: A Study in Classic Ragtime core

recorded 1959, released 1960 · Folkways FG 3562 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “American Beauty Rag” · medium confidence

Joseph Lamb (p)

The last surviving member of ragtime's 'big three', recorded at home in his seventies playing his own compositions. He is technically rusty and it does not matter — this is a primary source.

Listen for: Lamb's rags are harmonically the richest of the classic repertoire; listen for the unexpected chromatic turns in the trio sections that Joplin rarely reaches for.

Note: Recorded by Sam Charters in Lamb's Brooklyn home in 1959; release year is given variously as 1959 or 1960.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Eubie Blake — The Eighty-Six Years of Eubie Blake core

recorded 1968, released 1969 · Columbia C2S 847 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Charleston Rag”

Eubie Blake (p, voc) · Noble Sissle (voc)

Blake was born in 1887, played in the ragtime era and was still recording in his eighties. This double album is a living witness moving between rag, stride and show tune in one sitting.

Listen for: His left hand is bigger and looser than Joplin's classic ragtime allows — you can hear ragtime becoming stride inside a single performer's technique.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Marvin Hamlisch — The Sting: Original Motion Picture Soundtrack core

recorded 1973, released 1974 · MCA MCA-390 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “The Entertainer”

Marvin Hamlisch (p, arr)

Historically unavoidable: this record put 'The Entertainer' in the singles chart in 1974 and made ragtime briefly ubiquitous. It is also anachronistic — the film is set in 1936, by which point ragtime was long over.

Listen for: Hamlisch swings the eighth notes slightly, which classic ragtime does not. It is a small change and it is why this sounds like 1974 rather than 1902.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

William Bolcom — Heliotrope Bouquet: Piano Rags 1900–1970 deep

recorded/released 1971 · Nonesuch H-71257 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Heliotrope Bouquet” · medium confidence

William Bolcom (p)

A composer-pianist putting classic rags next to his own, showing the form as a living compositional resource rather than a period style. Bolcom later won a Pulitzer; this is where he made the argument.

Listen for: Scott Joplin and Louis Chauvin's 'Heliotrope Bouquet' is unusually sad for a rag — the syncopation stays but the harmony keeps sinking.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Max Morath — The Best of Scott Joplin and Other Rag Classics deep

recorded/released 1972 · Vanguard VSD 39/40 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Maple Leaf Rag” · medium confidence

Max Morath (p)

Morath was performing and broadcasting ragtime a decade before the revival and plays it as entertainment rather than as recovered art music — a useful corrective to the Rifkin approach.

Listen for: Compare his tempo on 'Maple Leaf Rag' with Rifkin's. Both claim authenticity; they cannot both be right, and the argument about how fast to play a rag is a hundred years old.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Reginald R. Robinson — Sounds in Silhouette deep

recorded/released 1998 · Delmark DE-508 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Sounds in Silhouette” · medium confidence

Reginald R. Robinson (p)

Robinson writes new rags. He received a MacArthur Fellowship in 2004 for doing so, which settles the question of whether the form is dead.

Listen for: The multi-strain form and the marching left hand are intact, but the harmony has absorbed eighty years that Joplin did not have.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

New Orleans Jazz 1900–present

Three horns improvising different melodies at the same time over a marching-band rhythm section, and somehow it works.

New Orleans, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Dixieland, Trad jazz, Hot jazz

The sound is a polite pile-up. Cornet states the tune in the middle register, clarinet weaves a busy countermelody above it, trombone slides around underneath filling the gaps — all three at once, all the time, nobody waiting their turn. This collective improvisation is the defining feature and the thing that disappeared from jazz almost immediately afterward. Underneath, banjo and tuba or bass mark the beat with the rhythm section still audibly descended from brass bands and street parades. The repertoire came from marches, rags, hymns, blues, and popular songs, and the ensemble had a fixed division of labour that everyone understood, which is what kept the counterpoint from becoming noise. It was recorded first in 1917 by a white band, the Original Dixieland Jass Band, which is a fact worth sitting with: the music was Black in origin and the record industry got there in the wrong order. King Oliver's 1923 sessions and Armstrong's Hot Fives are where you actually hear what the style could do.

What to listen for

  • Three horns playing three different melodies at once, and you can pick out each one
  • The clarinet noodling high and fast above everything else
  • Trombone smearing between notes rather than hitting them squarely
  • A two-beat oom-pah bass line, not the even four of later jazz
  • Everyone getting louder and busier for the last chorus

How it works

harmony
Simple and functional — I, IV, V with passing diminished chords. Blues form sits alongside 32-bar song and multi-strain rag form.
rhythm
Two-beat, with the accent on 1 and 3 and a distinct march inheritance. The 'stomp' feel is heavier and squarer than later swing.
form
Head arrangements over the tune's chorus structure, with breaks, stop-time passages, and a final out-chorus where everyone plays louder.
instrumentation
Front line of cornet/trumpet, clarinet, trombone; rhythm section of banjo or guitar, tuba or string bass, drums, sometimes piano.
improvisation
Collective and simultaneous rather than one soloist at a time. Individual paraphrase of the melody rather than running the changes.
timbre
Vibrato-heavy, vocalised brass — growls, smears, plunger mutes. Nobody is trying to sound clean.

Key figures

  • Louis Armstrong (trumpet, vocals) — Turned the ensemble style inside out by proving a single soloist could carry a record; the pivot from collective to individual runs through him.
  • King Oliver (cornet) — Led the Creole Jazz Band, the best-documented example of the two-cornet New Orleans ensemble working properly.
  • Sidney Bechet (soprano saxophone, clarinet) — The first great jazz soloist on record alongside Armstrong; his wide vibrato is the sound of the idiom's clarinet lineage.
  • Jelly Roll Morton (piano, composition) — Arranged the style rather than just playing it — his Red Hot Peppers sides are composed frameworks with improvisation slotted in.

Labels: Gennett · OKeh · Victor · Columbia · Good Time Jazz (revival)

Contested

'Dixieland' and 'New Orleans jazz' are often used interchangeably, but many musicians and writers use 'Dixieland' specifically for the later white revivalist style and consider the term loaded. There is also a live argument about whether the 1940s revival was preservation or pastiche.

Came out of

  • Ragtime — direct descendant, 1905, via repertoire, form, rhythm. Rags were standard New Orleans band repertoire, and the multi-strain march form came across intact — a lot of early jazz is a rag played by a band. What changed is that the printed notes stopped being binding: players began paraphrasing the strains, and blues inflection entered a music that had none. Jelly Roll Morton's Red Hot Peppers sides are rag architecture with improvisation and blues poured into it.

Fed into

  • Chicago Jazz — direct descendant, 1924
  • Swing & Big Band — direct descendant, 1930
  • Vocal Jazz & the Songbook — direct descendant, 1928
  • Kansas City Jazz — direct descendant, 1928
  • Free Jazz — parallel influence, 1961
  • AACM & Great Black Music — revival of, 1969
  • Neo-Bop & the Young Lions — revival of, 1989

What to hear

gateway

Louis Armstrong — The Complete Hot Five and Hot Seven Recordings gateway

recorded 1925, released 2000 · Columbia/Legacy C4K 63527 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “West End Blues”

Louis Armstrong (cor, tp, voc) · Kid Ory (tb) · Johnny Dodds (cl) · Lil Hardin Armstrong (p) · Johnny St. Cyr (bj)

The single most consequential body of recordings in jazz. Across 1925–28 you can hear the music stop being an ensemble art and become a soloist's art, in real time, in one man's playing.

Listen for: On 'West End Blues' the unaccompanied opening cadenza announces that a soloist can now command a record on his own. Nothing before 1928 sounds like that.

Note: Recorded 1925–1928; the Columbia/Legacy four-CD set collecting them was issued in 2000. Earlier equivalent collections exist.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

core

King Oliver's Creole Jazz Band — King Oliver's Jazz Band 1923 core

recorded 1923, released 1974 · Smithsonian Collection R 001 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Dippermouth Blues”

King Oliver (cor) · Louis Armstrong (cor) · Johnny Dodds (cl) · Honoré Dutrey (tb) · Lil Hardin (p) · Baby Dodds (d)

The best documentation of a working New Orleans band with two cornets, recorded before the style was diluted. Armstrong is the second cornet, playing harmony parts, three years before he takes over.

Listen for: The two cornets play worked-out breaks in perfect unison, apparently improvised. They were not — Oliver signalled them in advance, and the illusion is the point.

Note: Recorded 1923 for Gennett, OKeh, Columbia and Paramount; multiple later compilations exist. Catalogue given here is the widely cited Smithsonian reissue.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jelly Roll Morton's Red Hot Peppers — Birth of the Hot: The Classic Chicago 'Red Hot Peppers' Sessions 1926–27 core

recorded 1926, released 1995 · RCA Bluebird 07863 66641-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Black Bottom Stomp”

Jelly Roll Morton (p, arr) · George Mitchell (cor) · Kid Ory (tb) · Omer Simeon (cl) · Johnny St. Cyr (bj) · John Lindsay (b) · Andrew Hilaire (d)

Morton wrote and rehearsed these, and they are the clearest demonstration that New Orleans jazz could be composed. The rag's multi-strain architecture is intact underneath the improvising.

Listen for: 'Black Bottom Stomp' changes texture every sixteen bars — full ensemble, then a break, then a solo with stop-time, then a different combination. That is arrangement, not jamming.

Note: Recorded Chicago 1926–27 for Victor; the Bluebird CD compilation dates from 1995.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Sidney Bechet — The Best of Sidney Bechet core

recorded 1939, released 1994 · Blue Note CDP 7243 8 28891 2 3 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Summertime” · medium confidence

Sidney Bechet (ss, cl) · various

Bechet's soprano saxophone is the loudest, widest, most dominating sound in early jazz, and he refused to be a section player for sixty years. His 'Summertime' from 1939 is the New Orleans idiom outliving its era.

Listen for: The vibrato is enormous and slow, wobbling nearly a semitone. Modern saxophone teaching would call it a fault; here it is the whole personality.

Note: Compilation of Bechet's Blue Note sessions, chiefly 1939–1953.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Original Dixieland Jazz Band — The Complete Original Dixieland Jazz Band 1917–1936 core

recorded 1917, released 1996 · RCA Bluebird ND 82483 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Livery Stable Blues”

Nick LaRocca (cor) · Larry Shields (cl) · Eddie Edwards (tb) · Henry Ragas (p) · Tony Sbarbaro (d)

The first jazz record, in February 1917, made by a white band from New Orleans who then claimed to have invented the music. Both the musical and the historical facts here matter, and they point in different directions.

Listen for: The energy is real and the collective front line works, but compare the rhythmic looseness to Oliver's band six years later — the difference is what the record industry took twelve years to document.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Johnny Dodds — Blue Clarinet Stomp core

recorded 1928, released 1991 · RCA Bluebird ND 82293 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Blue Clarinet Stomp” · medium confidence

Johnny Dodds (cl) · Natty Dominique (cor) · Bill Johnson (b) · Baby Dodds (d)

Dodds is the New Orleans clarinet voice — blues-drenched, vocal, and far rougher than the Chicago players who copied him. These small-group sides show the idiom without Armstrong dominating it.

Listen for: He bends into notes from below and lets them sag, which is a blues singer's technique applied to a clarinet.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

George Lewis — Jazz at Vespers deep

recorded/released 1954 · Riverside RLP 12-230 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Just a Closer Walk with Thee” · medium confidence

George Lewis (cl) · Avery 'Kid' Howard (tp) · Jim Robinson (tb) · Alton Purnell (p) · Lawrence Marrero (bj) · Alcide 'Slow Drag' Pavageau (b) · Joe Watkins (d)

The New Orleans revival's central document, recorded in a Ohio college chapel by musicians who had never left the idiom. Hymns played by a jazz band, which is where a lot of the repertoire came from in the first place.

Listen for: The band plays hymns almost straight, then lets the collective improvisation build very gradually. It never becomes a showpiece.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Preservation Hall Jazz Band — So It Is deep

recorded 2016, released 2017 · Legacy 88985419112 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Santiago”

Ben Jaffe (b, tuba) · Charlie Gabriel (ts, cl) · Branden Lewis (tp) · Ronell Johnson (tb) · Kyle Roussel (p) · Walter Harris (d)

Proof that the tradition is not a museum. The band went to Cuba, came back, and made a record of original material that is recognisably New Orleans and unmistakably contemporary.

Listen for: The Afro-Cuban rhythms sit naturally against second-line drumming, which is the point — these are branches of one root that were separated by geography rather than by kind.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Wynton Marsalis — The Majesty of the Blues deep

recorded 1988, released 1989 · Columbia CK 45091 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “The Death of Jazz”

Wynton Marsalis (tp) · Todd Williams (ts) · Wessell Anderson (as) · Marcus Roberts (p) · Reginald Veal (b) · Herlin Riley (d) · Dr. Michael White (cl) · Freddie Lonzo (tb)

The record where Marsalis turned back past bebop to New Orleans polyphony and second-line rhythm, with a spoken sermon in the middle. It reset his own project and the neo-traditionalist argument.

Listen for: The 'New Orleans Function' suite uses a real funeral-march-to-second-line structure: slow dirge out, uptempo strut back.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Stride Piano 1919–1945

Ragtime's left hand taught to swing, played by men competing to physically overwhelm each other at rent parties.

New York (Harlem), USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Harlem stride, Harlem piano

The left hand leaps: bass note on 1 and 3, chord up in the middle of the keyboard on 2 and 4, back and forth across a tenth or more. That leap is the stride, and it is genuinely hard — the reach alone excluded a lot of players. Where ragtime's left hand was even and metronomic, stride's is springy and slightly ahead or behind, and the right hand is no longer bound to the printed page. This was competitive music. Harlem rent parties in the 1920s ran on cutting contests, where pianists took turns trying to demolish each other, and the style's showmanship — the tempo escalations, the interpolated classical quotations, the sheer density — comes directly from that. James P. Johnson is the founding figure, Willie 'The Lion' Smith the great character, and Fats Waller the one who took it to a mass audience by adding comedy and a hit-making instinct. Art Tatum then took the technique somewhere nobody has followed since. Ellington, Basie and Monk all played stride, and you can hear it surfacing in their playing decades later.

What to listen for

  • The left hand physically jumping between a low bass note and a mid-range chord, over and over
  • One pianist producing a whole band's worth of sound with no rhythm section
  • Tempos that ratchet up as the performance goes on, showing off
  • Sudden quotations from classical pieces or other songs dropped in mid-solo

How it works

harmony
Richer than ragtime — sixths, ninths, chromatic passing chords, and a growing use of blues inflection. Tatum pushed it into substitutions that anticipated bebop.
rhythm
Alternating bass-note/chord left hand at speed, with rhythmic elasticity the right hand plays against. Swing eighth notes replace ragtime's even ones.
form
Popular song forms and rag strains, with the arrangement built as a series of escalating choruses.
instrumentation
Solo piano. The whole point is that one player covers bass, chords, and melody without help.
improvisation
Extensive right-hand variation over a memorised structure, plus reharmonisation and interpolated quotation.
timbre
Full-range, orchestral use of the piano — deep bass, ringing treble, heavy pedalling on ballads.

Key figures

  • James P. Johnson (piano) — The founder; 'Carolina Shout' was the test piece every aspiring Harlem pianist had to master.
  • Fats Waller (piano, organ, vocals) — Johnson's student, and the one who made the style commercially enormous without diluting the left hand.
  • Willie 'The Lion' Smith (piano) — The most harmonically impressionistic of the strider; Ellington cited him directly as an influence.
  • Art Tatum (piano) — Took stride's mechanics and applied a harmonic imagination and technique that reset what the instrument could do.

Labels: OKeh · Victor · Decca · Commodore

Contested

Whether Art Tatum belongs inside stride or outside it. His left hand is stride-derived, but his harmonic language points forward to bebop; some historians file him as a category of one.

Came out of

  • Ragtime — direct descendant, 1919, via form, rhythm, instrumentation. Stride is ragtime's alternating bass with the metronome removed. The left-hand mechanism is inherited almost unchanged; what the Harlem players added was swung eighth notes, blues harmony, right-hand improvisation, and a competitive tempo escalation that had no place in Joplin's instruction to never play fast.

Fed into

  • Swing & Big Band — direct descendant, 1932
  • Bebop — direct descendant, 1945

What to hear

gateway

James P. Johnson — Carolina Shout gateway

recorded 1921, released 1991 · Biograph BCD 105 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Carolina Shout”

James P. Johnson (p)

The founding stride record and the test piece every Harlem pianist had to learn — Ellington taught himself by slowing down Johnson's piano roll and copying the fingering off the keys.

Listen for: The left hand jumps a tenth or more between the bass note and the chord, on every beat, at speed. That leap is the style's name and its physical difficulty.

Note: 'Carolina Shout' was cut as a piano roll in 1918 and as a disc in October 1921; Biograph collections gather both.

core

Fats Waller — A Good Man Is Hard to Find core

recorded 1935, released 1993 · RCA Bluebird ND 82482 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Ain't Misbehavin'” · medium confidence

Fats Waller (p, voc) · Herman Autrey (tp) · Gene Sedric (ts, cl) · Al Casey (g) · Charles Turner (b) · Yank Porter (d)

Waller sold stride to a mass audience by wrapping it in comedy, and the joke conceals how much piano is going on. The rhythm section is essentially decorative — he does not need it.

Listen for: Ignore the singing for one chorus and follow the left hand alone. It never stops and it never simplifies.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Art Tatum — The Complete Pablo Solo Masterpieces core

recorded 1953, released 1991 · Pablo 7PACD-4404-2 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Tea for Two”

Art Tatum (p)

Norman Granz recorded Tatum alone over several marathon days in 1953–55, producing the largest body of solo jazz piano by the most technically formidable player the music has had.

Listen for: Inside any ballad, he will reharmonise a phrase two or three different ways in a single chorus. The stride left hand is still there under the ornament, holding time.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Willie 'The Lion' Smith — The Lion Roars core

recorded/released 1957 · Dot DLP 3094 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Echoes of Spring” · medium confidence

Willie 'The Lion' Smith (p)

The most harmonically impressionistic of the Harlem pianists, and the one Ellington named as a direct influence. His own compositions sound like Debussy who learned to stride.

Listen for: 'Echoes of Spring' has almost no blues in it and a great deal of whole-tone colour — stride reaching toward the concert hall.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Duke Ellington — The Duke Plays Ellington (Piano Reflections) core

recorded 1953, released 1954 · Capitol T 477 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Reflections in D” · medium confidence

Duke Ellington (p) · Wendell Marshall (b) · Butch Ballard (d) · Dave Black (d)

Ellington almost never recorded as a piano soloist, and this trio session shows how much stride is still in his hands thirty years after Harlem — and how much space he had learned to leave.

Listen for: He plays far fewer notes than Waller or Johnson, but the left-hand placement is identical. Stride reduced to its rhythmic essentials.

Note: Issued on Capitol as 'The Duke Plays Ellington'; the 1989 CD reissue with extra tracks renamed it 'Piano Reflections', which is now the commoner title. Sources give the original LP as 1953 or 1954.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Thelonious Monk — Solo Monk core

recorded 1964, released 1965 · Columbia CL 2349 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Dinah”

Thelonious Monk (p)

Monk's debt to stride is clearest when he is alone: the left hand strides, the ballads come from the 1920s repertoire, and the dissonance sits on top of an old-fashioned foundation.

Listen for: On 'Dinah' the stride is unmistakable and completely lopsided — he keeps dropping or adding a beat's worth of weight where the pattern should be even.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Dick Wellstood — From Ragtime On deep

recorded/released 1971 · Chiaroscuro CR 109 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Squeeze Me” · low confidence

Dick Wellstood (p)

A generation later, a white pianist who had played with Sidney Bechet made stride a working modern language rather than a period recreation. He is also very funny, which the style rewards.

Listen for: He will play a bebop line with a stride left hand underneath it, which should not work.

Note: Wellstood's Chiaroscuro discography is not well documented online; title, year and catalogue number should be treated as provisional.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Donald Lambert — Harlem Stride Classics deep

recorded 1941, released 1990 · Pumpkin PU 117 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Pilgrim's Chorus” · low confidence

Donald Lambert (p)

Lambert barely recorded and worked New Jersey bars for decades, but the surviving sides — including stride arrangements of Massenet and Wagner — show a technique the size of Tatum's.

Listen for: His stride version of the 'Pilgrims' Chorus' from Tannhäuser is not a joke; the left hand keeps perfect time through material that has no business swinging.

Note: Lambert's recordings survive mainly through small reissue labels; catalogue details are poorly documented.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jaki Byard — Blues for Smoke deep

recorded 1960, released 1988 · Candid CCD 79018 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Blues for Smoke”

Jaki Byard (p)

Byard could move between stride, bebop and free playing within one chorus, and did it constantly. This solo session is the clearest demonstration that jazz history is available all at once to a player who knows it.

Listen for: Time-travel inside a single track: a stride passage gives way to something Cecil Taylor might have played, then back, with no seam.

Note: Recorded for Candid in December 1960 but not issued until the Candid catalogue was revived in the late 1980s.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Chicago Jazz 1923–1938

New Orleans jazz after the Great Migration, played hotter and faster by kids who learned it off records.

Chicago, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Chicago style, Austin High Gang style

When the New Orleans players moved north during the Great Migration, a generation of young white Chicagoans — the Austin High Gang among them — learned the music by wearing out King Oliver records and then playing it their own way. What came out was tenser than the original. The collective front line is still there but thinner, the tempos are up, the saxophone starts elbowing into the front line where it never belonged in New Orleans, and there is a nervous, driving quality to the rhythm that the older players did not have. The signature device is the 'explosion' — a hard accented break, often on the last beat before a chorus. It is transitional music: still ensemble-based, but with individual solos taking up more and more space until, by about 1930, the format simply became the small swing band. Its historical importance is partly demographic — this is where jazz stopped being regional and became a national commercial music.

What to listen for

  • Faster tempos and a jumpier, more anxious feel than New Orleans records
  • A hard accented 'explosion' hit just before a new chorus starts
  • A saxophone in the front line, which New Orleans bands mostly did not have
  • Long individual solos rather than continuous group weaving

How it works

harmony
Same functional harmony as New Orleans, with more use of the 32-bar popular song form and slightly richer passing chords.
rhythm
Faster and more forward-leaning than New Orleans, with a shift away from two-beat towards an even four. Tense rather than rolling.
form
Head–solos–head. The out-chorus survives, but the sequence of individual solos is now the centre of the record.
instrumentation
Cornet, clarinet, trombone, plus tenor or C-melody saxophone; guitar replacing banjo, string bass replacing tuba.
improvisation
Individual solo choruses become the point. Bix Beiderbecke's cool, placed lyricism and Frank Teschemacher's jagged clarinet are the two poles.
timbre
Brighter and drier than New Orleans; Beiderbecke's bell-like cornet tone is deliberately un-vibrato'd and became a model for cool jazz twenty years later.

Key figures

  • Bix Beiderbecke (cornet) — Played with a round, unforced tone and unusual interval choices; the first widely copied alternative to Armstrong's approach.
  • Eddie Condon (guitar, banjo, bandleading) — Organiser and lifelong advocate; his sessions kept the style documented for four decades.
  • Frank Teschemacher (clarinet) — The jagged, harmonically restless clarinet voice of the style, killed in a car crash at 26.
  • Gene Krupa (drums) — Brought the full drum kit forward in the mix and carried the Chicago drive into the swing era.

Labels: OKeh · Brunswick · Gennett · Commodore

Contested

Whether 'Chicago style' names a genuine musical style or just a place and a cohort. Some writers treat it as a white derivative of New Orleans jazz rather than a development; others point to the rhythmic shift toward four-beat as a real innovation that fed the swing era.

Came out of

  • New Orleans Jazz — direct descendant, 1924, via instrumentation, form, improvisation. Young Chicagoans learned the New Orleans front line off King Oliver and Armstrong records and then reweighted it. The collective texture thinned, the saxophone pushed in, tempos rose, and the individual solo chorus began displacing the group weave — the change that makes the swing small group possible.

Fed into

  • Swing & Big Band — direct descendant, 1932

What to hear

gateway

Bix Beiderbecke — Singin' the Blues gateway

recorded 1927, released 1990 · Columbia CK 45450 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Singin' the Blues”

Bix Beiderbecke (cor) · Frankie Trumbauer (Cm) · Eddie Lang (g) · various

Beiderbecke's cornet is the first great alternative to Armstrong: warm, unforced, harmonically curious, and completely without showmanship. He was dead at 28 and the recorded evidence is small and perfect.

Listen for: The title track's solo is built from notes chosen for their colour against the chord rather than for display; it was transcribed and memorised by musicians for decades.

Note: Recorded 1927 for OKeh under Frankie Trumbauer's name; the Columbia CD compilation dates from 1990.

core

Eddie Condon — The Chronological Eddie Condon 1927–1938 core

recorded 1927, released 1996 · Classics Classics 742 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Nobody's Sweetheart” · medium confidence

Eddie Condon (g, bj) · Pee Wee Russell (cl) · Jack Teagarden (tb) · various

Condon organised the Chicago players for four decades and these sides are the style at its rawest, before it got polished for concert halls.

Listen for: The 'explosion' — a hard accented hit right before a new chorus begins, which the Chicago players used constantly and New Orleans bands did not.

The December 1927 OKeh session is the style's founding document — four sides by teenagers who had learned jazz from records, playing faster and harder than their models.

Listen for: Krupa's bass drum is audible and driving in a way that 1927 recording technology usually could not handle, and it pushes the whole band forward.

Russell played clarinet like nobody else — croaking, hesitating, choosing notes that sound like mistakes and resolve like arguments. This is the best-recorded document of a genuinely strange musician.

Listen for: He will start a phrase, appear to lose it, and land somewhere unrelated that turns out to be correct. It is deliberate.

Bud Freeman (ts) · Max Kaminsky (tp) · Jack Teagarden (tb) · Pee Wee Russell (cl) · Dave Bowman (p) · Eddie Condon (g) · Dave Tough (d)

Freeman built a tenor style that owes nothing to Coleman Hawkins — lighter, drier, more skittish — and it is one of the few genuinely independent saxophone lineages of the 1930s.

Listen for: The tenor tone is thin and papery compared with Hawkins, and the phrasing skips rather than drives. Lester Young was listening.

recorded 1935, released 1998 · RCA Bluebird 09026-68764-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Body and Soul” · medium confidence

Benny Goodman (cl) · Teddy Wilson (p) · Gene Krupa (d) · Lionel Hampton (vib)

Goodman came out of Chicago and these small groups are where his playing is least constrained. They were also racially integrated on a public stage in 1936, which took real professional risk.

Listen for: With only three or four players, the Chicago habit of driving hard through a chorus has nowhere to hide — the tempo pushes constantly.

Muggsy Spanier — The Great 16 deep

recorded 1939, released 1994 · RCA Bluebird ND 82932 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Relaxin' at the Touro” · medium confidence

Muggsy Spanier (cor) · Rod Cless (cl) · George Brunies (tb) · Joe Bushkin (p) · Bob Casey (b) · Marty Greenberg (d)

Sixteen sides cut in 1939 by a Chicago cornetist who had nearly died the year before, playing plunger-muted and unhurried. It is the point where Chicago style stops chasing swing and becomes a preserved idiom.

Listen for: Spanier's plunger work on 'Relaxin' at the Touro' — the mute is doing what a singer's mouth does to a vowel.

Teagarden made the trombone a legato blues instrument rather than a tailgate noisemaker, and he sang with the same relaxed drawl. Nobody in New Orleans played trombone like this.

Listen for: He barely uses the slide's smearing capability — the lines are as smooth as a saxophone's, which was almost unheard of in 1929.

Vocal Jazz & the Songbook 1926–present

Singers treating a written song as raw material — bending the time, the pitch and the meaning of the lyric.

New York / Chicago, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Jazz singing, Great American Songbook

The difference between a jazz singer and a good singer of the same songs is what they do to the tune. Billie Holiday takes a mediocre Tin Pan Alley number, strips the melody down to a few notes, drags every phrase behind the beat, and makes the lyric mean something the writer never intended. Ella Fitzgerald keeps the melody gleaming and then improvises whole choruses of wordless scat with the accuracy of a horn player. Sarah Vaughan uses an operatically trained instrument to reharmonise on the fly. All three are doing the same underlying thing — improvising, in the specific sense that they are making choices about time and pitch that are not in the sheet music. The repertoire is largely the Great American Songbook: Gershwin, Porter, Berlin, Kern, Rodgers, Arlen, written mostly for Broadway and Hollywood between the 1920s and 1950s, whose 32-bar AABA forms became the harmonic playing field for jazz instrumentalists too. Scat singing is the most obvious marker but far from the most important one; phrasing is.

What to listen for

  • The singer arriving noticeably late on words, letting the band go ahead
  • The written melody getting further from itself with each chorus
  • Wordless syllables used as an instrumental solo (scat)
  • Conversational give-and-take between the voice and one instrument — Holiday and Lester Young are the model

How it works

harmony
Songbook harmony — 32-bar AABA and ABAC forms with rich ii–V movement. Singers often introduce their own passing notes and blue thirds against it.
rhythm
Radical flexibility with the beat. Holiday sits far behind it, Sinatra stretches phrases across bar lines, Fitzgerald floats on top of it.
form
Verse (often omitted) then chorus, with instrumental solo sections and a final chorus that departs furthest from the written melody.
instrumentation
Voice with anything from solo piano to full string orchestra; the small-group-with-horns setting is where the interplay is most audible.
improvisation
Melodic paraphrase first, scat second. The best singers vary the melody so continuously that the written line never returns intact.
timbre
Enormous range, from Holiday's small, frayed sound to Vaughan's four-octave control. Vibrato use is a stylistic signature in itself.

Key figures

  • Billie Holiday (voice) — Rewrote what phrasing could do; her interpretive approach to a lyric is the reference point for everyone who followed.
  • Ella Fitzgerald (voice) — The most technically complete scat improviser, and her Songbook series fixed the canon of American popular song.
  • Sarah Vaughan (voice) — Brought bebop harmony and operatic technique to the same phrase; the most harmonically daring of the major singers.
  • Louis Armstrong (voice, trumpet) — Established that a jazz singer phrases like a horn player, and popularised scat in the 1920s.
  • Betty Carter (voice) — Took the abstraction furthest — extreme tempos, melodies dismantled beyond recognition, and a band she ran like a bebop leader.

Labels: Columbia · Decca · Verve · Capitol · Commodore

Contested

Whether 'vocal jazz' is a genre or a role that cuts across every genre. Purists argue that Sinatra and much of the Songbook repertoire is popular singing with jazz accompaniment rather than jazz. This guide treats it as a genre-shaped tradition with a defined repertoire and a distinct set of techniques, while acknowledging the boundary is porous — an interpretive call, not a settled fact.

Came out of

  • New Orleans Jazz — direct descendant, 1928, via improvisation, timbre, repertoire. Armstrong established that a jazz singer phrases like a horn player — displacing the beat, bending pitch, treating the written melody as a suggestion — and popularised scat as an extension of that. Every jazz singer after him is working from this premise whether or not they scat.
  • Swing & Big Band — direct descendant, 1935, via repertoire, instrumentation, form. The big band vocalist slot gave singers a professional route and fixed the repertoire as the popular songbook. Holiday's small-group sides for Teddy Wilson are the version where the singer is a soloist among soloists rather than a feature act.
  • Bebop — direct descendant, 1946, via harmony, improvisation. Sarah Vaughan and Eddie Jefferson brought bebop harmony and phrasing into singing, and vocalese — writing lyrics to a recorded improvised solo and singing it — makes the debt literal. Vaughan sang with Earl Hines and Billy Eckstine's bands alongside Parker and Gillespie.

Fed into

  • Bossa Nova & Brazilian Jazz — parallel influence, 1959

What to hear

gateway

Billie Holiday — The Quintessential Billie Holiday, Volume 3 (1936–1937) gateway

recorded 1936, released 1987 · Columbia CK 44048 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “This Year's Kisses”

Billie Holiday (voc) · Lester Young (ts) · Teddy Wilson (p) · Buck Clayton (tp) · Freddie Green (g) · Jo Jones (d)

The Teddy Wilson small-group sides are the most perfect singer-and-band records in jazz. Holiday and Lester Young answer each other phrase by phrase, and the material is often disposable pop that they simply transform.

Listen for: Follow the conversation between voice and tenor. Young plays around her line rather than under it, and neither of them ever raises their voice.

The first of the eight Songbook sets, which between them defined the Great American Songbook as a canon. Fitzgerald sings the melodies with unusual fidelity here, which was the point — Norman Granz wanted the songs documented.

Listen for: How little she embellishes. The improvisation is in the time and the timbre, not in the notes, which is a harder discipline than scatting.

recorded/released 1958 · Capitol W 1053 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Angel Eyes”

Whether this is jazz is exactly the argument, and it is worth having in front of this record. Sinatra's phrasing came out of listening to Billie Holiday and Tommy Dorsey's trombone breathing, and Riddle's charts are jazz orchestration.

Listen for: He stretches phrases across the bar line and delays consonants until the last possible moment. That is jazz phrasing regardless of what genre the record sits in.

Carter took melodic abstraction further than any other singer and ran her own label to do it. Her 'Sounds (Movin' On)' runs past twenty minutes and the melody is never stated intact.

Listen for: Tempos so slow the band almost stops, and so fast the words become pure sound. She is treating a standard the way Sonny Rollins treats one.

Kurt Elling (voc) · Laurence Hobgood (p) · Rob Amster (b) · Paul Wertico (d) · Eddie Johnson (ts) · Von Freeman (ts)

The strongest argument that vocalese and scat are still live techniques rather than period devices. Elling writes lyrics to instrumental solos and delivers them with a four-octave range and no irony.

Kansas City Jazz 1928–1946

Blues-soaked big band jazz built on riffs, with a rhythm section so relaxed it seems to float.

Kansas City, Missouri, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called KC swing, Territory band style

Kansas City under the Pendergast machine had wide-open nightlife and clubs that stayed open all night, which meant jam sessions ran for hours and a musician's reputation was made in them. Two things came out of that. First, riff-based arranging: instead of writing intricate charts, sections trade short repeated phrases, building layer on layer, and the arrangement can be assembled on the bandstand by ear. Second, a new rhythm section feel — Basie's band with Walter Page on bass, Freddie Green on guitar and Jo Jones on drums shifted the pulse to an even, buoyant four beats to the bar, moved timekeeping to the hi-hat and ride, and left enormous space. Basie's own piano playing reduced to a few notes, which was a radical act of subtraction. Blues form is everywhere, far more than in eastern swing bands. And the marathon jam sessions turned tenor saxophone into a combat sport, which produced Lester Young — whose light, behind-the-beat lyricism became the direct model for Charlie Parker's generation.

What to listen for

  • Short repeated phrases stacked in layers, brass answering reeds
  • Twelve-bar blues far more often than in other big band music
  • A rhythm section that feels like it's gliding rather than pushing
  • Piano that plays almost nothing — a few notes, then silence
  • Solos that go on for four, six, eight choruses and keep building

How it works

harmony
Blues-dominant. Twelve-bar form is the default rather than an occasional feature, with simple changes that leave room for the soloist.
rhythm
Even four-to-the-bar with a light, springing feel. Bass walks, guitar chunks quarter notes, drums move time to the hi-hat and use the bass drum sparingly.
form
Head arrangements built from riffs, with call-and-response between brass and reeds, and long strings of solo choruses.
instrumentation
Full big band, but also the small jam-session combo. Guitar is a rhythm instrument, not a solo voice.
improvisation
Chorus after chorus over the blues, with the expectation that a soloist builds across many choruses rather than saying it all in one.
timbre
Lester Young's dry, vibratoless tenor against Coleman Hawkins' heavier eastern sound — the two poles of saxophone tone for the next twenty years.

Key figures

  • Count Basie (piano, bandleading) — Built the rhythm section that redefined swing feel, and made minimalism at the piano a style rather than an absence.
  • Lester Young (tenor saxophone) — Played light, behind the beat, with long asymmetric lines — the direct precursor to bebop phrasing and to cool jazz tone.
  • Bennie Moten (piano, bandleading) — Led the band that became Basie's; the 1932 Victor sessions are where the KC rhythmic shift first lands on record.
  • Mary Lou Williams (piano, arranging) — Chief arranger for Andy Kirk's Clouds of Joy and the KC scene's most important compositional mind; later mentored the bebop generation.
  • Jay McShann (piano, bandleading) — Ran the band where Charlie Parker got his first recorded solos, making the KC-to-bebop link literal.

Labels: Decca · Victor · Vocalion · Commodore

Contested

Whether Kansas City is a separate genre or a regional dialect of swing. This guide treats it separately because the rhythmic and blues-form innovations there are specific, traceable, and fed bebop more directly than the New York swing mainstream did.

Came out of

  • New Orleans Jazz — direct descendant, 1928, via instrumentation, repertoire. The territory bands working the Midwest carried the New Orleans repertoire and instrumentation outward, but the KC scene reweighted it toward blues form and riff construction, which the New Orleans model used far less.

Fed into

  • Swing & Big Band — parallel influence, 1936
  • Bebop — direct descendant, 1944
  • Cool Jazz — direct descendant, 1949
  • Soul Jazz & Organ Trio — parallel influence, 1958

What to hear

gateway

Count Basie — The Original American Decca Recordings gateway

recorded 1937, released 1992 · GRP/Decca GRD-3-611 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “One O'Clock Jump”

Count Basie (p) · Lester Young (ts) · Herschel Evans (ts) · Buck Clayton (tp) · Freddie Green (g) · Walter Page (b) · Jo Jones (d) · Jimmy Rushing (voc)

The 1937–39 Decca sides are the Kansas City rhythm section in its first flush, and they changed how every band in America kept time. Lester Young's solos here are the other half of the reason this matters.

Listen for: The rhythm section floats rather than pushes. Freddie Green plays quarter notes on guitar and never solos in his fifty years with the band; Jo Jones moves the pulse to the hi-hat.

Small-group sessions from 1938 and 1944 where Young has nothing to hide behind. This is the tenor playing that Charlie Parker studied and that cool jazz descends from.

Swing & Big Band 1930–present

Jazz as America's pop music: written arrangements for fourteen-plus players, built for dancing.

New York / Kansas City / Chicago, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Big band jazz, The Swing Era

For about a decade, this was simply what popular music sounded like. The instrument is the orchestra: reeds and brass as blocks of sound answering each other, a rhythm section keeping an even four, and arrangements written down so a large band could execute complicated material precisely night after night. The arranger becomes as important as any soloist — Fletcher Henderson, Sy Oliver, Eddie Sauter, Billy Strayhorn. What separates swing from the dance music before it is the rhythmic feel: uneven eighth notes with a forward lean, and a rhythm section that propels rather than merely marks time. Within that commercial frame there was enormous range, from Glenn Miller's tightly controlled hits through Benny Goodman's virtuoso precision to Ellington's orchestra, which was really a composer's laboratory with an unusually stable roster. Its commercial dominance ended after 1946 — economics, a wartime recording ban, changing dance habits, the entertainment tax — but big band writing never stopped, and the repertory and contemporary large-ensemble scenes are direct continuations.

What to listen for

  • Whole sections of horns moving together in harmony, like one huge instrument
  • Brass and reeds trading phrases back and forth in call-and-response
  • A hard-hitting climactic 'shout chorus' near the end where the whole band goes up a gear
  • Danceable, steady tempos — this was functional music for a ballroom floor

How it works

harmony
Popular song harmony of the era, richly voiced — added sixths and ninths, sectional block voicings, and increasingly adventurous writing as the 1940s approach.
rhythm
Even four-to-the-bar with swung eighths. The rhythm section is a unit whose job is lift.
form
Written arrangements on 32-bar AABA popular songs and blues, with solos slotted into designated choruses and shout choruses near the end.
instrumentation
Standardised big band: four or five reeds, three or four trumpets, three or four trombones, piano, guitar, bass, drums. Often a featured vocalist.
improvisation
Present but rationed — usually one or two choruses inside an otherwise written chart.
timbre
Section blend as the primary colour: saxophones as a single voice, brass with and without mutes, and the deliberate contrast between the two.

Key figures

  • Duke Ellington (piano, composition, bandleading) — Wrote for the specific players in his band rather than for generic parts, which is why his music does not transfer well to other orchestras.
  • Fletcher Henderson (arranging, bandleading) — Codified the reed-versus-brass call-and-response template that the entire era used, then sold his charts to Goodman.
  • Benny Goodman (clarinet, bandleading) — Made swing a mass phenomenon in 1935 and led racially integrated small groups in public when that was a professional risk.
  • Billy Strayhorn (composition, arranging, piano) — Ellington's writing partner for nearly thirty years; the harmonic sophistication of the later Ellington book is substantially his.
  • Coleman Hawkins (tenor saxophone) — His 1939 'Body and Soul' is a full chorus of improvisation on the harmony rather than the melody — a bebop premise, two years early.

Labels: Victor · Decca · Columbia · Capitol

Contested

Where swing ends. Some treat 1946 as the hard stop; others point to Basie's second band, Kenton, Herman's Herds and the entire repertory movement as evidence that it simply stopped being pop music without ceasing to exist. This guide takes the latter view.

Came out of

  • New Orleans Jazz — direct descendant, 1930, via repertoire, instrumentation, rhythm. The big band took the New Orleans front line's division of labour and multiplied it into sections: what one clarinet did against one cornet, five reeds now do against four trumpets. Fletcher Henderson's charts are the translation step, turning improvised counterpoint into written call-and-response.
  • Stride Piano — direct descendant, 1932, via harmony, rhythm. Ellington learned directly from Willie 'The Lion' Smith and James P. Johnson, and the harmonic richness of his orchestral writing comes out of stride's keyboard voicings scaled up to a horn section. The stride left hand is also what the swing rhythm section replaced — the bass and guitar took over the job one hand had been doing.
  • Chicago Jazz — direct descendant, 1932, via rhythm, improvisation. Chicago's shift from a two-beat march feel toward an even four, and its promotion of the individual solo over collective improvisation, are the two things swing then standardised. Gene Krupa carried the Chicago drive straight into Benny Goodman's band.
  • Kansas City Jazz — parallel influence, 1936, via rhythm, form. The Basie rhythm section arrived in New York in 1936 and changed the era's feel from underneath: even four-to-the-bar, timekeeping moved to the hi-hat and ride, bass drum reserved for accents, and enormous space. Every swing band adjusted. Riff-based head arrangements spread the same way.

Fed into

  • Vocal Jazz & the Songbook — direct descendant, 1935
  • Jazz Manouche — direct descendant, 1934
  • Bebop — reaction against, 1944
  • Afro-Cuban & Latin Jazz — fusion of, 1943
  • Neo-Bop & the Young Lions — revival of, 1986

What to hear

gateway

Duke Ellington — The Blanton–Webster Band gateway

recorded 1940, released 1986 · RCA Bluebird 5659-2-RB · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Ko-Ko”

Duke Ellington (p, comp) · Billy Strayhorn (comp, arr) · Jimmy Blanton (b) · Ben Webster (ts) · Johnny Hodges (as) · Cootie Williams (tp) · Tricky Sam Nanton (tb)

The 1940–42 band is the peak of orchestral jazz writing. Ellington had Strayhorn, a bassist who reinvented the instrument's role, and a saxophone section he had been writing for individually for fifteen years.

Listen for: 'Ko-Ko' is built almost entirely from a minor blues and short repeated figures, and it is genuinely dark — closer to Bartók's sense of orchestration than to dance music.

January 1938: jazz played at Carnegie Hall for the first time, with an integrated line-up and members of the Basie and Ellington bands sitting in. The 'Sing, Sing, Sing' performance runs twelve minutes and does not sag.

Listen for: Jess Stacy's unplanned piano solo in 'Sing, Sing, Sing' — quiet, wandering and completely unlike the rest of the concert. Goodman just let it run.

Fletcher Henderson — A Study in Frustration core

Fletcher Henderson (p, ldr) · Louis Armstrong (tp) · Coleman Hawkins (ts) · Don Redman (arr) · Benny Carter (as, arr)

Henderson and Don Redman invented the big band arrangement — reeds against brass, call-and-response, soloist framed by written backgrounds — and then Henderson sold the charts to Goodman and watched him get rich.

Listen for: Armstrong joins in 1924 and the whole band's rhythmic conception changes within months. You can hear a rhythm section learning to swing.

Duke Ellington — Ellington at Newport core

recorded/released 1956 · Columbia CL 934 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Diminuendo and Crescendo in Blue”

Duke Ellington (p) · Paul Gonsalves (ts) · Johnny Hodges (as) · Sam Woodyard (d) · Jimmy Woode (b)

Paul Gonsalves plays 27 blues choruses, the crowd loses its composure, and Ellington's career restarts at 57. It is the most famous single performance in big band jazz.

Listen for: Sam Woodyard's drumming and Jimmy Woode's bass during the Gonsalves solo — they simply hold a groove for six minutes and the tension does the rest.

Note: The 1956 Columbia LP is partly a studio re-recording; the 1999 'Complete' edition restores the actual concert tapes.

Shaw was a better clarinettist than Goodman and a far more reluctant bandleader; his Gramercy Five small groups and the string-band experiments are the era's most restless writing.

Listen for: The Gramercy Five uses a harpsichord, in 1940, in a swing band. It is not a novelty — it is a genuine attempt at a different ensemble colour.

Woody Herman — The Thundering Herds 1945–1947 core

Woody Herman (cl, as) · Flip Phillips (ts) · Bill Harris (tb) · Ralph Burns (arr) · Stan Getz (ts) · Zoot Sims (ts)

The First Herd absorbed bebop harmony into a big band while it was still happening, and Stravinsky wrote the Ebony Concerto for them in 1945. This is swing refusing to die quietly.

Listen for: The Four Brothers saxophone voicing — three tenors and a baritone instead of the standard two altos on top — gives the section a darker, thicker sound than any other band.

Duke Ellington and Billy Strayhorn — Such Sweet Thunder deep

Duke Ellington (p) · Billy Strayhorn (comp) · Johnny Hodges (as) · Clark Terry (tp) · Ray Nance (tp, vln)

A twelve-part Shakespeare suite, written with Strayhorn, where each movement is a character portrait. It is the strongest evidence that Ellington's real form was the suite, not the three-minute side.

The most precisely drilled band of the era, with Sy Oliver's two-beat arrangements and a showmanship that made them nearly unbeatable live. Basie and Ellington both said Lunceford's band was the one to fear.

Listen for: Oliver's charts sit on a loping two-beat rather than an even four, which makes the band sound like it is leaning back while moving fast.

Big band writing is not a historical practice. Schneider studied with Gil Evans and writes long-form orchestral pieces with Brazilian and flamenco rhythm underneath; this was the first record to win a Grammy on internet-funded distribution alone.

Jazz Manouche 1934–present

Swing played on acoustic guitars with no drums, where the rhythm guitars are the drum kit.

Paris, France · Global Currents · also called Gypsy jazz, Gypsy swing, Hot club jazz

No drums, no horns, no piano — two or three acoustic guitars, a violin, and a double bass. The rhythm guitars play 'la pompe', a percussive chunk-chunk on every beat with the chord damped immediately, and that sound does the entire job of a drum kit. Over it, a lead guitar plays fast, ornamented single-note lines full of arpeggios, chromatic runs, and a wide fingered vibrato that comes out of Romani string tradition rather than American jazz. Django Reinhardt built the style with violinist Stéphane Grappelli in the Quintette du Hot Club de France, formed in Paris in 1934, and he did it with two working fingers on his fretting hand after a caravan fire — the resulting chord voicings and octave-heavy lines are partly a workaround that became a vocabulary. The repertoire mixes American standards with French musette waltzes and Django's own compositions. It is one of the very few jazz styles born outside the United States that fed back into the American mainstream, and it remains a living tradition with festivals, dynasties and a defined technique.

What to listen for

  • Guitars chopping out every beat with the chord cut short — that's the drum kit
  • Absolutely no drums and no horns anywhere
  • Lead guitar lines running up and down arpeggios at improbable speed
  • Violin as a front-line solo instrument, which is rare elsewhere in jazz
  • A wide, fast shimmer of vibrato at the end of held notes

How it works

harmony
Swing-era changes with a strong minor-key and diminished flavour, plus musette-derived chromaticism. Sixth and diminished chords everywhere.
rhythm
'La pompe' — a damped, percussive four-to-the-bar guitar stroke with a hard accent on 2 and 4 that replaces drums entirely.
form
Standards and originals in 32-bar and 16-bar forms, often taken at extreme tempos, with breaks and unaccompanied intros.
instrumentation
Lead guitar, one or two rhythm guitars, violin, double bass. Selmer-Maccaferri guitars with a distinctive cutting tone.
improvisation
Fast arpeggio-based lines, chromatic approach notes, tremolo and octave passages. More ornamental and less blues-inflected than American jazz of the same era.
timbre
All-acoustic, bright and nasal. Wide, fast vibrato on both guitar and violin.

Key figures

  • Django Reinhardt (guitar) — Invented the idiom's guitar language, with a fretting hand damaged by fire that forced a distinctive approach to voicings and octaves.
  • Stéphane Grappelli (violin) — Made violin a credible jazz lead voice and carried the style forward for fifty years after Django's death.
  • Biréli Lagrène (guitar) — The most prominent of the modern inheritors; recorded as a teenage prodigy and later moved between manouche and fusion.
  • Baro Ferret (guitar) — Key figure in the Parisian Romani guitar scene whose musette-inflected playing shows the style's non-American roots.

Labels: Ultraphone · Swing (French label) · Decca (France) · Frémeaux & Associés (reissues)

Contested

The name. 'Gypsy jazz' is standard in English-language usage but 'Gypsy' is widely considered a slur by Romani people; 'jazz manouche' is the French term and is preferred by many players. This guide leads with the latter. There is also debate over whether Django's postwar electric bebop-influenced work is the same genre at all.

Came out of

  • Swing & Big Band — direct descendant, 1934, via repertoire, harmony, rhythm. Django and Grappelli took the American swing repertoire and its harmony wholesale, then rebuilt the rhythm section without drums, piano or horns. The other parent is not on this map: Romani string-band practice and Parisian musette, which supply the ornamentation, the minor-key chromaticism and the fingered vibrato.

What to hear

gateway

Django Reinhardt & Stéphane Grappelli — Djangologie 1936–1937 gateway

recorded 1936, released 2009 · Frémeaux & Associés FA 303 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Minor Swing” · medium confidence

Django Reinhardt (g) · Stéphane Grappelli (vln) · Joseph Reinhardt (g) · Roger Chaput (g) · Louis Vola (b)

The Quintette du Hot Club de France at its peak, with the classic all-string line-up. 'Minor Swing', 'Djangology' and 'Nuages' are here and everything the idiom later became is already fully formed.

Listen for: There is no drummer. The two rhythm guitars playing 'la pompe' — a hard chop with the chord instantly damped — do the entire job of a kit.

Note: The Quintette's 1930s sides appear in many reissue configurations; the Frémeaux 'Djangologie' series is the most complete and best documented.

Listen for: 'Nuages' has a descending chromatic inner line that is pure Parisian musette, not American jazz. This is the non-jazz half of the style showing itself.

Four generations of jazz violin in one room in Basel, 1966. Grappelli's manouche lineage sits next to Stuff Smith's swing and Ponty's incipient fusion, and the differences are immediately audible.

Listen for: Grappelli's tone is the sweetest and his time the most elastic; Ponty's is hard and fast and points straight at the electric violin he would take into fusion.

Lagrène was thirteen when this was recorded and already played the Django vocabulary fluently. It is both an astonishing document and a demonstration that the idiom is transmitted within Romani families rather than through schools.

Listen for: The tremolo and the octave passages are lifted directly from Django, but the time feel is a teenager's — faster and more aggressive.

The Dutch Sinti trio that carried the style into the 1990s with a cleanness of execution Django never had access to. This is the record that made the modern manouche festival circuit possible.

Listen for: Stochelo Rosenberg's picking is faster and more even than Django's, which raises a real question about whether technical perfection suits this music.

recorded 1947, released 1992 · Verve 835 418-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Nuages” · medium confidence

Django's last sessions, on electric guitar with a bebop rhythm section, weeks before his death in 1953. He had absorbed Parker and Gillespie and was building something new when he stopped.

Listen for: The lines are longer and more chromatic than anything from the 1930s, and the amplified tone removes the acoustic bark. It sounds like a different guitarist who happens to phrase identically.

Afro-Cuban & Latin Jazz 1940–present

Jazz harmony and soloing built on Cuban clave instead of swing — the rhythm section is doing something completely different.

Havana / New York, Cuba / USA · Global Currents · also called Cubop, Latin jazz, Salsa jazz

The change is rhythmic and it is total. Swing divides the beat into a lilting triplet feel; Afro-Cuban music divides it evenly and organises everything around clave, a two-bar asymmetric pattern (three strokes in one bar, two in the other) that every other part must align with. Get the clave backwards and the whole band sounds wrong to anyone who knows. Layer on top the tumbao bass pattern that anticipates the downbeat rather than landing on it, the montuno piano figure, congas, timbales, bongos, cowbell — and you have a rhythm section that is polyrhythmic by design rather than by accident. Dizzy Gillespie and Cuban percussionist Chano Pozo made the fusion explicit in 1947 with 'Manteca' and 'Cubana Be, Cubana Bop', and Machito's orchestra with arranger Mario Bauzá had been doing it in New York since 1940. Later waves brought Brazilian, Puerto Rican and Panamanian currents, and by the 1970s the Fania-era salsa scene and jazz were sharing personnel constantly. The African retentions in Cuban music were less interrupted than in the US, which is part of why the meeting felt like a reunion.

What to listen for

  • Even, straight eighth notes instead of the loping swing feel
  • A repeating two-bar pattern (clave) that everything else lines up against
  • Congas and timbales, not just a drum kit
  • The bass landing just before the downbeat rather than on it
  • A long open section where the band vamps on two chords and the percussion takes over

How it works

harmony
Jazz harmony largely intact — bebop changes, modal vamps, and montuno progressions that cycle a short chord sequence indefinitely.
rhythm
Clave-organised, with even (straight) eighth notes rather than swung ones. Tumbao bass anticipates beat 1, and multiple percussion parts interlock.
form
Often two-part: a written, arranged section followed by a montuno — an open vamp for solos, mambo horn riffs and percussion features.
instrumentation
Full jazz horn section plus congas, timbales, bongos, güiro, claves, cowbell. Piano plays montuno figures rather than comping freely.
improvisation
Bebop-derived soloing over vamps, plus percussion soloing as a featured element in its own right.
timbre
Bright, brassy and dense. Percussion is foregrounded rather than treated as accompaniment.

Key figures

  • Mario Bauzá (trumpet, arranging) — Machito's musical director and the person who actually engineered the meeting of Cuban rhythm and jazz orchestration.
  • Chano Pozo (congas, composition) — Brought authentic Afro-Cuban drumming and its religious repertoire into Gillespie's band; murdered in 1948 at 33.
  • Dizzy Gillespie (trumpet, bandleading) — The bebop figure who insisted the rhythmic connection mattered and had the platform to prove it.
  • Tito Puente (timbales, vibraphone, arranging) — Bridged mambo dance music and jazz for five decades with formidable arranging chops.
  • Eddie Palmieri (piano, bandleading) — Brought McCoy Tyner's harmonic density and free-jazz edges into salsa without losing the dance floor.
  • Chucho Valdés (piano, composition) — Led Irakere in Cuba, fusing Yoruba batá drumming with post-bop harmony under conditions of near-total isolation from the US scene.

Labels: Verve · Tico · Fania · Blue Note · Concord Picante

Contested

Whether 'Latin jazz' is one genre or a shorthand for several unrelated ones. Cuban-derived, Brazilian, Puerto Rican and Argentine jazz traditions have different rhythmic bases and grouping them can obscure more than it explains. This guide separates the Brazilian stream out for that reason.

Came out of

  • Swing & Big Band — fusion of, 1943, via instrumentation, form. Machito's Afro-Cubans, with Mario Bauzá as musical director, put a full jazz horn section and big-band arranging over a Cuban rhythm section. The orchestration is American; the rhythmic organisation underneath is entirely Cuban, and the two do not blend so much as coexist under tension.
  • Bebop — fusion of, 1947, via harmony, improvisation, rhythm. Gillespie hired Chano Pozo in 1947 and 'Manteca' and 'Cubana Be, Cubana Bop' followed within months. Bebop supplied the harmonic language and the soloing; Pozo supplied clave, the tumbao and drum patterns carried from Afro-Cuban religious practice. The swing lilt is what got discarded — the eighth notes go straight.

Fed into

  • Ethio-Jazz — direct descendant, 1966
  • M-Base — parallel influence, 1993

What to hear

gateway

Machito and His Afro-Cubans — Afro-Cuban Jazz Suite gateway

recorded 1950, released 1952 · Mercury (Clef series) MG C-505 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Afro-Cuban Jazz Suite” · medium confidence

Machito (maracas, voc) · Mario Bauzá (mus. dir) · Charlie Parker (as) · Flip Phillips (ts) · Buddy Rich (d) · Chico O'Farrill (comp, arr)

Chico O'Farrill's five-movement suite with Charlie Parker soloing over an authentic Cuban rhythm section. It is the meeting stated at full length rather than as a novelty single.

Listen for: Parker plays exactly as he would over bebop changes, and the rhythm section does not adjust to him at all. Both languages stay intact and the friction is the record.

Note: Recorded in New York on 21 December 1950; first issued on Mercury's Clef series as MG C-505 and reissued repeatedly on Clef, Norgran and Verve through the 1950s, so the release year is given variously as 1951-1957.

Cuba's leading band recorded live in the US during a brief diplomatic thaw, playing Yoruba batá drumming, post-bop harmony and rock guitar in the same piece. It won a Grammy and then the members started defecting.

Listen for: The batá drums are sacred instruments from Santería practice, used here inside a jazz arrangement. That combination was politically and religiously loaded in Cuba at the time.

A Cuban pianist of formidable technique playing Gillespie's repertoire, which closes a circle that opened in 1947. Rubalcaba's rhythmic displacement is at a level almost nobody else attempts.

Listen for: He plays with the beat as though it were elastic, stretching phrases far past where they should land and arriving exactly right. The clave is still underneath it all.

Mongo Santamaría — Afro Roots deep

Note: Combines the 1958 Fantasy albums 'Yambú' and 'Mongo'; the Prestige double LP reissue dates from 1972.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jerry González and the Fort Apache Band — Rumba para Monk deep

recorded 1988, released 1989 · Sunnyside SSC 1036 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Evidence”

Jerry González (tp, congas) · Carter Jefferson (ts) · Larry Willis (p) · Andy González (b) · Steve Berrios (d)

Monk's tunes played in Afro-Cuban rhythm, and they fit disturbingly well — Monk's angularity and clave's asymmetry turn out to be compatible.

Listen for: 'Evidence' in a rumba feel. Monk's melody was already displacing accents; putting it over clave doubles the effect.

recorded/released 2015 · Motéma MTM-193 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Cuba: The Conversation Continues”

Arturo O'Farrill (p, cond) · Rudresh Mahanthappa (as) · Vijay Iyer (comp) · Bobby Sanabria (percussion)

Recorded in Havana days after the US–Cuba diplomatic reopening was announced, by the son of the man who wrote the Afro-Cuban Jazz Suite. The lineage is literal and the commissioned new pieces are serious.

Listen for: Vijay Iyer and Rudresh Mahanthappa's contributions put contemporary rhythmic thinking on top of the clave tradition, which is a different fusion than 1947's.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Bebop 1944–present

Small-group jazz played fast and dense enough that it stopped being dance music and became something you sit down for.

New York (Harlem / 52nd Street), USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Bop, Rebop

Bebop is what happens when musicians decide the audience is other musicians. A small group — trumpet, alto or tenor, piano, bass, drums — plays a fast, angular unison melody, then everyone solos at length over the chord changes, then the melody returns. The tunes are often new heads written over the harmony of existing standards, so the players get fresh material without paying royalties or relearning forms. The lines are long, asymmetric, and full of chromatic approach notes and upper-structure intervals — flattened fifths and ninths that sounded wrong to swing-trained ears. Rhythmically the whole timekeeping apparatus moves: the ride cymbal carries the pulse, the bass drum stops marking every beat and instead 'drops bombs' as accents, and the pianist stops stride-style timekeeping and comps in irregular stabs. It developed at after-hours sessions in Harlem, notably Minton's Playhouse, during the early 1940s, and the 1942–44 recording ban means the crucial transition is largely undocumented — the records pick up when the style is already formed.

What to listen for

  • A fast, twisting melody played in unison by two horns with no harmony underneath
  • Drums keeping time on a cymbal while the bass drum punches at unpredictable moments
  • Solos that run in continuous eighth notes for a very long time without obvious pause
  • Tunes you half-recognise — the chords are from a standard but the melody is new
  • Tempos that are simply too fast to dance to

How it works

harmony
Fast-moving functional harmony with extensions and substitutions: flat nines, sharp elevens, tritone substitutions, and ii–V chains inserted where the original song had none.
rhythm
Ride cymbal as primary timekeeper, walking bass in even four, bass drum used for irregular accents rather than pulse, piano comping in syncopated punctuation.
form
Head–solos–head over 32-bar AABA, rhythm changes, and 12-bar blues. Contrafacts — new melodies on borrowed changes — are everywhere.
instrumentation
Quintet: trumpet, saxophone, piano, bass, drums. Guitar drops out of the rhythm section.
improvisation
The central activity. Long eighth-note lines that outline the harmony precisely, with chromatic enclosures and deliberate targeting of the upper extensions.
timbre
Dry, hard, minimal vibrato. Parker's alto tone and Gillespie's high, brilliant trumpet are deliberately unsentimental.

Key figures

  • Charlie Parker (alto saxophone) — Built the melodic vocabulary. Almost every jazz improviser since has absorbed his phrasing, whether or not they play his style.
  • Dizzy Gillespie (trumpet, composition) — Parker's counterpart and the style's theorist and teacher; also the one who brought Afro-Cuban rhythm into it.
  • Bud Powell (piano) — Translated Parker's horn lines to the keyboard and invented the modern jazz piano role — right-hand lines over sparse left-hand chords.
  • Thelonious Monk (piano, composition) — Present at the creation but never really a bebop player; his compositions and use of space run at an angle to the whole style.
  • Max Roach (drums) — Moved the drum kit from timekeeping to conversation, playing melodically against the soloist.
  • Kenny Clarke (drums) — The Minton's house drummer who first shifted the pulse to the ride cymbal — the technical change that made bebop possible.

Labels: Savoy · Dial · Blue Note · Clef/Norgran (later Verve) · Prestige

Contested

Whether bebop was a revolution or an evolution. Musicians involved often described it as a natural extension of what Lester Young, Charlie Christian and Coleman Hawkins were already doing; the press framed it as a rupture. Both accounts have evidence behind them.

Came out of

  • Swing & Big Band — reaction against, 1944, via form, instrumentation, social-context. Bebop kept swing's harmony and rhythmic base and rejected almost everything else: the big band, the written arrangement, the dance floor, the entertainment function, and the economics that put Black musicians in front of white audiences as employees. The small group playing at tempos nobody could dance to was a position, not just a sound.
  • Kansas City Jazz — direct descendant, 1944, via improvisation, rhythm, harmony. Charlie Parker came out of Jay McShann's Kansas City band, and Lester Young's light tone, long asymmetric phrases and behind-the-beat placement are the direct model for bebop line construction. The KC jam-session culture — many choruses, blues form, cutting contests — is bebop's working method before bebop existed.
  • Stride Piano — direct descendant, 1945, via harmony, instrumentation. Bud Powell's right hand is Parker's line and his left hand is stride's, stripped to sparse rootless punctuation. The route runs through Art Tatum, whose substitutions and passing harmony gave the bebop generation most of its reharmonisation vocabulary.

Fed into

  • Vocal Jazz & the Songbook — direct descendant, 1946
  • Afro-Cuban & Latin Jazz — fusion of, 1947
  • Cool Jazz — reaction against, 1949
  • Hard Bop — direct descendant, 1954
  • Modal Jazz — reaction against, 1958

What to hear

gateway

Charlie Parker — The Complete Savoy and Dial Studio Recordings gateway

recorded 1944, released 2000 · Savoy Jazz 92911-2 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Ko-Ko”

Charlie Parker (as) · Dizzy Gillespie (tp) · Miles Davis (tp) · Bud Powell (p) · Max Roach (d) · Duke Jordan (p) · Tommy Potter (b)

The 1945–48 Savoy and Dial sides are bebop's primary text. They are short, most are three minutes, and the alternate takes let you hear a solo being reconsidered from scratch each time.

Listen for: On 'Ko-Ko' the head is played at a tempo nobody had attempted, and Parker's first chorus lands on the flattened fifth of every chord as though it were consonant. In 1945 it was not.

Note: Recorded 1944–48 for Savoy and Dial; multiple box-set configurations exist under different catalogue numbers.

Dizzy Gillespie — The Complete RCA Victor Recordings core

recorded 1947, released 1995 · RCA Victor 07863 66528-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Manteca”

Dizzy Gillespie (tp) · Chano Pozo (congas) · John Lewis (p) · Milt Jackson (vib) · Al McKibbon (b) · George Russell (arr)

Gillespie's big band, where bebop met both orchestral arranging and Afro-Cuban rhythm. 'Manteca' and 'Cubana Be, Cubana Bop' are on this and they start a whole branch of the music.

Listen for: 'Manteca' has an A section that is pure Cuban vamp and a bridge that is pure bebop. Gillespie later said Pozo wrote the vamp and could not write a bridge because it needed changes.
