Bud Powell — The Amazing Bud Powell, Volume 1 core

recorded 1949, released 1952 · Blue Note BLP 1503 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Un Poco Loco”

Bud Powell (p) · Fats Navarro (tp) · Sonny Rollins (ts) · Tommy Potter (b) · Roy Haynes (d) · Curley Russell (b) · Max Roach (d)

Powell invented the modern jazz piano role here: right-hand horn lines over sparse left-hand chords, with the timekeeping handed to the bass and drums. Every pianist since works from this.

Listen for: 'Un Poco Loco' has Max Roach playing a cowbell figure that fights the piano's meter for the whole track. There are three released takes and the argument gets more extreme in each.

Note: Recorded August 1949 and May 1951; issued as a 12-inch LP in 1955 after earlier 10-inch releases.

Thelonious Monk — Brilliant Corners core

recorded 1956, released 1957 · Riverside RLP 12-226 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Brilliant Corners”

Thelonious Monk (p) · Sonny Rollins (ts) · Ernie Henry (as) · Oscar Pettiford (b) · Max Roach (d) · Clark Terry (tp) · Paul Chambers (b)

Monk's compositions are the strangest thing in the bebop era and this is the best-documented session of them. The title track was so difficult that the released version is spliced from multiple incomplete takes.

Listen for: 'Brilliant Corners' alternates between two tempos, doubling and halving every eight bars. The band audibly struggles, and that struggle is on the record.

Clifford Brown & Max Roach — Clifford Brown and Max Roach core

recorded 1954, released 1955 · EmArcy MG 36036 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Joy Spring”

Clifford Brown (tp) · Max Roach (d) · Harold Land (ts) · Richie Powell (p) · George Morrow (b)

The quintet that connects bebop to hard bop, with the most fluent trumpet playing anyone had heard. Brown died in a car crash in June 1956 at twenty-five.

Listen for: Brown's tone never breaks, at any speed or register. Compare the strain audible in most bebop trumpet — this is why every trumpeter after him tried to sound like this.

Dexter Gordon — Go core

recorded/released 1962 · Blue Note BLP 4112 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Cheese Cake”

Dexter Gordon (ts) · Sonny Clark (p) · Butch Warren (b) · Billy Higgins (d)

Gordon was the first tenor player to translate Parker's alto language to the bigger horn, and by 1962 he had had twenty years to relax into it. This is bebop played with total ease.

Listen for: He plays a long way behind the beat and quotes constantly — nursery rhymes, other standards, opera. It is a conversational habit, not a trick.

Dameron was bebop's finest composer-arranger and Navarro its most lyrical trumpeter before Clifford Brown. Navarro died of tuberculosis at twenty-six in 1950.

Late Powell, after years of institutionalisation and electroshock treatment, still writing and still playing. 'Cleopatra's Dream' is one of his best compositions and comes from this period.

Cool Jazz 1948–1964

Bebop's harmony at half the temperature — soft attack, written counterpoint, and space where the heat used to be.

New York, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called The cool school, Birth of the Cool style

Take bebop's harmonic sophistication and remove its aggression. Cool jazz keeps the extended chords and the fast changes but plays them with a light, vibrato-free tone, at moderate tempos, with far more written material and much more silence. The Miles Davis nonet sessions of 1949–50 are the founding document: nine pieces including French horn and tuba, arranged by Gil Evans, Gerry Mulligan and John Lewis into something closer to chamber music than to a jam session. The other root is Lennie Tristano's circle in New York, which pursued long, seamless improvised lines with almost no rhythmic emphasis, deliberately suppressing the drama. Lester Young is the shared ancestor of both — his dry tone and behind-the-beat placement is what 'cool' means as a sound. Critics have often written the style off as bloodless or as a whitening of bebop, and the racial politics of its reception are genuinely ugly, but the actual music of Tristano, Konitz, Evans and Lewis is intellectually demanding and unlike anything else.

What to listen for

  • Horns playing with a soft, breathy tone and almost no vibrato
  • Two or more melody lines moving independently in a written arrangement, not just chords
  • Instruments you don't expect in jazz — French horn, tuba, cello
  • Drums using brushes and staying out of the way
  • Medium tempos with noticeable space between phrases

How it works

harmony
Bebop's extended harmony plus impressionist colouring — quartal voicings, whole-tone passages, and polytonal writing in the arranged pieces.
rhythm
Even, unemphatic, deliberately undramatic. Drummers use brushes often and avoid punctuation; the pulse is stated rather than driven.
form
Arranged ensemble passages framing solos, with contrapuntal writing and unusual section lengths rather than straight head–solos–head.
instrumentation
Unusual for jazz: French horn, tuba, baritone saxophone, flute, cello. Piano-less groups also appear.
improvisation
Long, evenly phrased melodic lines with little rhythmic display. Tristano's students practised eliminating cliché and accent entirely.
timbre
Soft attack, minimal vibrato, blended ensemble sound. Nothing is played loudly for effect.

Key figures

  • Miles Davis (trumpet, bandleading) — Assembled the nonet and provided its middle-register, unhurried lead voice; then left the style behind within a few years.
  • Gil Evans (arranging) — The orchestral imagination behind the nonet and later the Davis–Evans collaborations; treated the ensemble as a single instrument with variable colour.
  • Lennie Tristano (piano, teaching) — Ran a rigorous school of improvisation aimed at pure line, and cut two free improvisations in 1949, years before Ornette Coleman.
  • Lee Konitz (alto saxophone) — The one major altoist of his generation who did not sound like Charlie Parker — a genuine alternative line of descent.
  • John Lewis (piano, composition) — Nonet arranger who then built the Modern Jazz Quartet around baroque counterpoint and blues.

Labels: Capitol · Prestige · Atlantic · Verve

Contested

Whether cool jazz and West Coast jazz are one thing. This guide separates them: cool jazz is the New York nonet/Tristano lineage, West Coast is the Los Angeles studio-musician scene that grew partly out of it. Many writers merge the two, and the overlap in personnel is real.

Came out of

  • Bebop — reaction against, 1949, via timbre, rhythm, form. The Miles Davis nonet kept bebop's harmony and threw out its heat: soft attack, no vibrato, medium tempos, and written contrapuntal arrangement where bebop had head-and-blow. Gil Evans' charts restore the arranger to the centre of a music that had just deposed him.
  • Kansas City Jazz — direct descendant, 1949, via timbre, improvisation. Lester Young's dry, vibratoless tenor and unhurried melodic placement is what 'cool' names as a sound. Lee Konitz and Stan Getz descend from Young rather than from Parker, which is why they are the two major post-war saxophonists who do not sound like everyone else.

Fed into

  • West Coast Jazz — direct descendant, 1952
  • Hard Bop — reaction against, 1954
  • Third Stream — direct descendant, 1957
  • Modal Jazz — direct descendant, 1958
  • Bossa Nova & Brazilian Jazz — direct descendant, 1958

What to hear

gateway

Miles Davis — Birth of the Cool gateway

recorded 1949, released 1957 · Capitol T 762 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Boplicity”

Miles Davis (tp) · Gerry Mulligan (bs, arr) · Lee Konitz (as) · J.J. Johnson (tb) · Gunther Schuller (frh) · Bill Barber (tuba) · Gil Evans (arr) · John Lewis (arr) · Max Roach (d) · Kenny Clarke (d)

Nine players including French horn and tuba, arranged as chamber music, recorded across three sessions in 1949–50. The sides sold poorly and then reorganised a decade of jazz.

Listen for: 'Boplicity' — the ensemble breathes as one instrument, and the voicings move in ways that no big band section writing had attempted. Gil Evans arranged it.

Note: Recorded January 1949, April 1949 and March 1950; collected as a Capitol LP under this title in 1957.

recorded 1949, released 1972 · Capitol M-11060 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Intuition”

The 1949 Capitol sessions include 'Intuition' and 'Digression' — completely free improvisations with no theme, key or tempo agreed in advance, recorded ten years before Ornette Coleman.

Listen for: On the composed tracks, listen to how evenly everything is phrased. Tristano trained his students to eliminate rhythmic emphasis entirely, so the line carries all the interest.

The one major altoist of his generation who did not sound like Charlie Parker. Konitz's tone is pale and dry and his lines avoid every bebop cliché deliberately.

John Lewis's fugal writing against Milt Jackson's completely unreconstructed blues vibraphone. The tension between those two impulses is what the MJQ is, for forty years.

Miles Davis + 19 — Miles Ahead core

recorded/released 1957 · Columbia CL 1041 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “The Maids of Cadiz”

Miles Davis (flugelhorn) · Gil Evans (arr, cond) · Lee Konitz (as) · Bill Barber (tuba) · Paul Chambers (b) · Art Taylor (d)

The nonet idea scaled to nineteen players, with Davis on flugelhorn as the only soloist across a continuous suite. Evans' writing here is the most sophisticated orchestration in jazz to that date.

Listen for: There is no saxophone section in the conventional sense. Evans built the ensemble around flutes, French horns, tuba and bass clarinet, so the colour is closer to Ravel's orchestra than to a big band.

recorded 1955, released 1956 · Norgran MGN 1032 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Shine”

Stan Getz (ts) · Conte Candoli (tp) · Lou Levy (p) · Leroy Vinnegar (b) · Shelly Manne (d)

Getz's tone descends directly from Lester Young and is the purest example of what 'cool' means as a sound. The band is Los Angeles' best rhythm section of the period.

Listen for: There is almost no vibrato and almost no attack — notes appear rather than start. Then listen to how hard he actually swings inside that soft surface.

Jimmy Giuffre 3 — Fusion deep

No drums, almost no volume, and a freedom of pulse that arrives before European free improvisation existed. It sold nothing and Giuffre's career did not recover for years.

Marsh was Tristano's most rigorous student and possibly the least commercially concerned musician in jazz history. The lines here are long, unaccented and harmonically ruthless.

Listen for: Two tenors improvising counterpoint simultaneously, without either taking the lead. It is the Tristano school's ideal and almost nobody else attempted it.

recorded 1960, released 1961 · Impulse! A-4 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “La Nevada”

Gil Evans (p, arr) · Johnny Coles (tp) · Jimmy Knepper (tb) · Budd Johnson (ts) · Ron Carter (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

Evans without Miles Davis, writing for his own band and leaving far more room for improvisation. 'La Nevada' is fifteen minutes built from almost nothing — a vamp and some cues.

Listen for: 'La Nevada' has no real composition to speak of. Evans shapes it in real time by bringing sections in and out over a static harmony, which is arranging as live improvisation.

West Coast Jazz 1952–1963

Cool jazz relocated to LA studios: light, arranged, lyrical, and made by musicians with day jobs scoring films.

Los Angeles, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called California jazz, Cool West Coast

Los Angeles in the 1950s had a large population of superb reading musicians working in film and television studios, and a club scene — the Lighthouse in Hermosa Beach, Zardi's, the Haig — where they played jazz at night. The result is a music that prizes arrangement, clarity and craft: contrapuntal writing, unusual instrumentation, tightly rehearsed ensembles, and solos that stay lyrical rather than combative. The Gerry Mulligan Quartet with Chet Baker dropped the piano entirely, which forced the baritone and trumpet to imply the harmony through counterpoint, and produced the style's defining sound. Shorty Rogers, Shelly Manne, Jimmy Giuffre and Bud Shank are the other central figures. The style has been criticised, then and since, as polite and racially coded — the LA studio system was overwhelmingly white and the Black LA scene around Central Avenue was a distinct thing that got far less attention. Both criticisms have force. The music itself, taken on its own terms, is more rhythmically inventive than its reputation suggests.

What to listen for

  • No piano at all in many groups — just horns, bass and drums
  • Two horns weaving separate lines around each other like a written duet
  • Flute, oboe or baritone saxophone carrying the melody
  • Everything sounding rehearsed rather than jammed
  • Quiet, detailed drumming with a lot of brushwork and rim sounds

How it works

harmony
Clean bebop-derived changes with a taste for counterpoint over vertical density; some pieces borrow directly from fugue and canon procedures.
rhythm
Light and buoyant rather than driving. Shelly Manne's playing is detailed and quiet, closer to a percussionist's approach than a swing drummer's.
form
Heavily arranged: written introductions, interludes, backgrounds behind solos, and shaped endings. Less head–solos–head than anywhere else in 1950s jazz.
instrumentation
Piano-less quartets, flute and oboe doubling, baritone saxophone as a lead voice, occasional strings and woodwind sections.
improvisation
Melodic and thematic, often developing a motif from the written material rather than running changes.
timbre
Dry, close-recorded, transparent. Chet Baker's trumpet and Mulligan's baritone define the palette.

Key figures

  • Gerry Mulligan (baritone saxophone, arranging) — The piano-less quartet was his idea and it defined the style's texture; also a first-rate contrapuntal writer.
  • Chet Baker (trumpet, vocals) — The style's lyrical voice — middle register, no vibrato, melodically simple and rhythmically subtle.
  • Shorty Rogers (trumpet, arranging) — The scene's most prolific arranger; his Giants sessions codified the West Coast small-band sound.
  • Shelly Manne (drums) — Redefined what a drummer could contribute texturally, and co-led the LA scene's most exploratory sessions.
  • Jimmy Giuffre (clarinet, saxophones, composition) — Pushed furthest out — drumless trios, folk-derived material, and by 1961 a chamber-free-jazz that predates most European improv.

Labels: Pacific Jazz · Contemporary · Atlantic · World Pacific

Contested

Whether it is a real style or a marketing geography. Critics including many at the time argued 'West Coast jazz' described where records were made, not how they sounded — Ornette Coleman and Eric Dolphy were also in Los Angeles. This guide keeps it because the piano-less contrapuntal small group and the arranger-forward approach are a genuinely coherent set of choices.

Came out of

  • Cool Jazz — direct descendant, 1952, via instrumentation, form, timbre. Mulligan and other nonet alumni moved to Los Angeles and found a studio-musician labour pool that could read anything. The piano-less quartet took the nonet's contrapuntal premise and reduced it to two horns implying the harmony between them.

Fed into

  • Third Stream — direct descendant, 1957
  • European Free Improvisation — parallel influence, 1963
  • ECM & Nordic Chamber Jazz — parallel influence, 1972

What to hear

gateway

Gerry Mulligan Quartet — The Original Gerry Mulligan Quartet with Chet Baker gateway

recorded 1952, released 1953 · Pacific Jazz PJLP-1 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “My Funny Valentine”

Gerry Mulligan (bs) · Chet Baker (tp) · Bob Whitlock (b) · Chico Hamilton (d)

The piano-less quartet, which forced two horns to imply the harmony between them through counterpoint. 'My Funny Valentine' from these sessions is one of the most-copied records in jazz.

Listen for: Nobody is playing chords. Baker and Mulligan weave two independent lines and your ear assembles the harmony from the intervals between them.

Note: The 1952–53 Pacific Jazz quartet sides appear in many configurations; the original 10-inch LP is PJLP-1.

Shorty Rogers (tp, arr) · Art Pepper (as) · Jimmy Giuffre (ts) · Shelly Manne (d) · Hampton Hawes (p)

Rogers wrote the arrangements that defined the LA small-band sound: tight, contrapuntal, and playable by people who could read anything at sight.

Listen for: The horn writing is chamber-scaled but the swing is genuine, which is the West Coast claim in one record.

Art Pepper — Art Pepper Meets the Rhythm Section core

recorded/released 1957 · Contemporary C 3532 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “You'd Be So Nice to Come Home To”

Art Pepper (as) · Red Garland (p) · Paul Chambers (b) · Philly Joe Jones (d)

Pepper was reportedly given no warning and had not played in weeks when Miles Davis's rhythm section arrived at his house. The record is a West Coast altoist stress-tested by the hardest-swinging East Coast trio available.

Listen for: He is playing catch-up for the first minute and then locks in. The lightness of his sound against Philly Joe Jones's drive is the whole East–West argument in one band.

Shelly Manne & His Friends — My Fair Lady core

A jazz trio playing a Broadway score, which became one of the best-selling jazz records of the 1950s and invented the show-tune concept album.

Listen for: Manne plays almost entirely with brushes and rims. He is a colourist here rather than a timekeeper, which is the West Coast drummer's characteristic move.

Chico Hamilton Quintet — The Chico Hamilton Quintet featuring Buddy Collette core

Chico Hamilton (d) · Buddy Collette (fl, cl, as, ts) · Fred Katz (cello) · Jim Hall (g) · Carson Smith (b)

A jazz group with a cello and no piano, playing what amounts to chamber music with a rhythm section. Jim Hall's first significant recordings are here.

Listen for: The cello is a full member, playing arco lines and pizzicato counterpoint. In 1955 nothing else in jazz sounded like this.

The Dave Brubeck Quartet — Time Out core

recorded/released 1959 · Columbia CL 1397 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Take Five”

Dave Brubeck (p) · Paul Desmond (as) · Eugene Wright (b) · Joe Morello (d)

The first jazz album to sell a million copies, built entirely on time signatures other than 4/4. Its commercial success is the reason odd meters entered jazz's ordinary vocabulary.

Listen for: 'Blue Rondo à la Turk' is in 9/8 grouped 2+2+2+3, then drops into a straight 4/4 blues for the solos and back. Count the introduction and you will feel the asymmetry.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Curtis Counce — You Get More Bounce with Curtis Counce! deep

recorded/released 1957 · Contemporary C 3539 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Sonar”

Curtis Counce (b) · Jack Sheldon (tp) · Harold Land (ts) · Carl Perkins (p) · Frank Butler (d)

The corrective to the idea that West Coast jazz was white and polite. Counce's group was Black, Los Angeles-based and played harder than most New York bands of the period.

Listen for: Frank Butler's drumming is aggressive and busy in a way no West Coast stereotype accommodates. This is what the LA scene actually contained.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Hampton Hawes Trio — The Trio, Vol. 1 deep

recorded 1955, released 1956 · Contemporary C 3505 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “I Got Rhythm”

Hampton Hawes (p) · Red Mitchell (b) · Chuck Thompson (d)

Hawes was a Los Angeles pianist with a blues attack as hard as anyone in New York, and this trio session was cut in a single night.

Listen for: The left hand punches rather than comps, and the blues content is constant. Nothing about this fits the cool West Coast description.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Bud Shank & Bob Cooper — Blowin' Country deep

recorded 1956, released 1958 · Pacific Jazz PJ-1226 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Blowin' Country” · low confidence

Bud Shank (as, fl) · Bob Cooper (ts, oboe) · Claude Williamson (p) · Don Prell (b) · Chuck Flores (d)

Flute and oboe as jazz lead instruments, played by studio musicians who could actually handle them. It is the most literal expression of the West Coast scene's double-life economics.

Listen for: The oboe is genuinely being improvised on, not merely featured. It is an instrument with almost no jazz literature and Cooper builds one here.

Note: Pacific Jazz reissued and recombined these Shank–Cooper sessions repeatedly; title and catalogue attribution should be verified.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Hard Bop 1954–present

Bebop with the blues and the church put back in — heavier, slower, and built to hit you in the chest.

New York / Detroit / Philadelphia, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Funky hard bop, East Coast jazz

By 1954 bebop had become a specialist's music and cool jazz had gone pale. Hard bop is the correction: the same harmonic language and the same quintet format, but slowed down, made bluesier, and reconnected to gospel and rhythm-and-blues. The tunes are catchier — often written by the band members themselves, with singable heads and memorable bass figures rather than abstract contrafacts. The drums get heavier, with a backbeat on 2 and 4 appearing regularly where swing drummers would have kept it on the cymbal. Piano comping picks up gospel voicings and repeated amen cadences. Art Blakey's Jazz Messengers, running from 1955 until his death in 1990, functioned as both the style's flagship and its university — Horace Silver, Benny Golson, Lee Morgan, Wayne Shorter, Freddie Hubbard, Keith Jarrett and dozens more came through it. Blue Note under Alfred Lion, with Rudy Van Gelder engineering and paid rehearsal time, gave the style a house sound that is still what most people mean when they picture a jazz record.

What to listen for

  • Drums hitting hard on beats 2 and 4, not just riding the cymbal
  • Gospel-style church chords in the piano comping, with repeated amen-like cadences
  • Heads you can actually hum, often with a strong bass riff underneath
  • A drum roll that builds and crashes into the next soloist's entrance
  • Minor-key tunes with a Latin feel on the head that switches to swing for the solos

How it works

harmony
Bebop changes plus explicit blues and gospel material — minor blues, modal vamps, IV–I plagal cadences lifted from church music.
rhythm
Heavier and more grounded than bebop, with backbeats, Latin-tinged intros, and Blakey's press rolls and hard shuffle feels.
form
Head–solos–head, but with composed introductions, interludes and shout choruses that make the records feel arranged.
instrumentation
Trumpet–tenor quintet is the default; sextets with trombone or alto are common. Organ and vibes appear at the edges.
improvisation
Blues-saturated and rhythmically emphatic. Motivic repetition and worrying at a phrase are valued over pure line.
timbre
Big, fat horn tones with more vibrato than bebop; Van Gelder's close-miked recording gives the drums and piano unusual weight.

Key figures

  • Art Blakey (drums, bandleading) — Ran the Jazz Messengers for 35 years as both a band and a training system; his press roll and backbeat are the style's rhythmic signature.
  • Horace Silver (piano, composition) — Wrote the tunes that defined the funky side of the style and made the piano a percussive, blues-drenched instrument.
  • Clifford Brown (trumpet) — The technical and lyrical model for every hard bop trumpeter; dead at 25 in 1956, which is why the recordings are so concentrated.
  • Cannonball Adderley (alto saxophone) — Carried the blues-and-joy side of the style to a large audience without softening the playing.
  • Lee Morgan (trumpet) — Blakey's most quotable soloist and the maker of the style's biggest commercial hit.
  • Sonny Rollins (tenor saxophone) — Built solos by developing motifs across whole choruses — thematic improvisation as an explicit method.

Labels: Blue Note · Prestige · Riverside · Savoy · Columbia

Contested

Some writers treat hard bop and soul jazz as a single continuum rather than two genres, and there is no clean line — Horace Silver sits on both sides of it. This guide splits them at the point where the organ trio and the R&B backbeat become the format rather than a flavour. The label 'East Coast jazz' as an answer to West Coast is a critics' construction more than a musicians' one.

Came out of

  • Bebop — direct descendant, 1954, via harmony, form, improvisation. Same quintet, same changes, same head–solos–head. What hard bop adds is weight and blues content: slower tempos, heavier drums, composed introductions and interludes, and heads written to be memorable rather than abstract.
  • Cool Jazz — reaction against, 1954, via timbre, rhythm, social-context. Hard bop's heat is aimed at cool jazz's restraint, and the aim was partly racial and geographic: an East Coast, largely Black music positioning itself against a West Coast, largely white studio scene that the press had been treating as jazz's future. Gospel and rhythm-and-blues content was reclaimed rather than merely added.

Fed into

  • Soul Jazz & Organ Trio — direct descendant, 1956
  • Post-Bop — direct descendant, 1963
  • Free Jazz — reaction against, 1959
  • Jazz Rap — direct descendant, 1991
  • J-Jazz — direct descendant, 1961
  • Neo-Bop & the Young Lions — revival of, 1980

What to hear

gateway

Art Blakey & The Jazz Messengers — Moanin' gateway

recorded 1958, released 1959 · Blue Note BLP 4003 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Moanin'”

Lee Morgan (tp) · Benny Golson (ts) · Bobby Timmons (p) · Jymie Merritt (b) · Art Blakey (d)

The record that fixed what hard bop sounded like: a gospel head everyone can sing, Golson's arranging, Morgan at twenty playing like he has nothing to lose, and Blakey underneath refusing to be background.

Listen for: The opening: Timmons plays a churchy piano phrase and the horns answer 'amen'. That call-and-response is lifted from Black church practice and dropped whole into jazz.

Horace Silver and the Jazz Messengers — Horace Silver and the Jazz Messengers core

recorded 1954, released 1956 · Blue Note BLP 1518 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “The Preacher”

Horace Silver (p) · Kenny Dorham (tp) · Hank Mobley (ts) · Doug Watkins (b) · Art Blakey (d)

The band that became the Jazz Messengers, with Silver's tunes — 'The Preacher', 'Doodlin'' — that made gospel and blues content the point rather than a garnish.

Listen for: 'The Preacher' is built on a sixteen-bar structure closer to a hymn than to a jazz form, and Blue Note's Alfred Lion initially thought it was too old-fashioned to release.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Cannonball Adderley — Somethin' Else core

recorded/released 1958 · Blue Note BLP 1595 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Autumn Leaves”

Cannonball Adderley (as) · Miles Davis (tp) · Hank Jones (p) · Sam Jones (b) · Art Blakey (d)

Adderley's date with Davis as sideman, a year before Kind of Blue. The version of 'Autumn Leaves' here is the one everyone knows, and it is a masterclass in setting a mood in eight bars.

Listen for: Davis's muted entry on 'Autumn Leaves' after the vamp — three notes, almost nothing, and the whole temperature of the track is set.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Sonny Rollins — Saxophone Colossus core

recorded/released 1956 · Prestige PRLP 7079 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “St. Thomas”

Sonny Rollins (ts) · Tommy Flanagan (p) · Doug Watkins (b) · Max Roach (d)

Rollins builds solos by developing a motif across whole choruses rather than running changes, and 'Blue 7' is the record where that method is most exposed. Gunther Schuller wrote an analysis of it that changed how jazz criticism worked.

Listen for: On 'Blue 7' he takes a three-note fragment and keeps turning it over for chorus after chorus. Almost nothing new is introduced and it never stops developing.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

John Coltrane — Blue Train core

recorded 1957, released 1958 · Blue Note BLP 1577 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Blue Train”

John Coltrane (ts) · Lee Morgan (tp) · Curtis Fuller (tb) · Kenny Drew (p) · Paul Chambers (b) · Philly Joe Jones (d)

Coltrane's only session as leader for Blue Note, made in the year he got clean, with a sextet and his own writing. It is the last record where he sounds unambiguously like a hard bop tenor player.

Listen for: The three-horn voicing on the title theme is dark and thick — trombone underneath rather than a second saxophone, which is why the minor blues sounds so heavy.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Lee Morgan — The Sidewinder core

recorded 1963, released 1964 · Blue Note BLP 4157 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “The Sidewinder”

Lee Morgan (tp) · Joe Henderson (ts) · Barry Harris (p) · Bob Cranshaw (b) · Billy Higgins (d)

Blue Note's biggest hit, a boogaloo built on a bass riff that crossed onto radio and briefly rescued the label's finances. It also pushed Blue Note toward chasing a groove hit on every subsequent date.

Listen for: The 24-bar structure is unusual and the whole thing sits on one insistent bass figure. Morgan's solo builds by repeating a single phrase up in pitch until it breaks.

deep

Hank Mobley — Soul Station deep

recorded/released 1960 · Blue Note BLP 4031 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “This I Dig of You”

Hank Mobley (ts) · Wynton Kelly (p) · Paul Chambers (b) · Art Blakey (d)

Mobley was called the middleweight champion of the tenor saxophone, meaning he was neither Coltrane nor Rollins, which was intended as faint praise and reads now as a description of perfect proportion.

Listen for: He never overplays and he never coasts. The tone is round and slightly withheld, and every phrase resolves where you want it to about a beat later than you expect.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Art Blakey & The Jazz Messengers — Free for All deep

recorded 1964, released 1965 · Blue Note BLP 4170 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Free for All”

Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Wayne Shorter (ts) · Curtis Fuller (tb) · Cedar Walton (p) · Reggie Workman (b) · Art Blakey (d)

The most violent record the Messengers made. Shorter was writing and directing by then and Blakey plays the title track as if trying to destroy the drum kit.

Listen for: The title track's tempo and volume never let up for eleven minutes. This is hard bop pushed to the edge of where free jazz starts.

Clifford Jordan — Glass Bead Games deep

recorded 1973, released 1974 · Strata-East SES-19737/8 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Powerful Paul Robeson”

Clifford Jordan (ts) · Stanley Cowell (p) · Cedar Walton (p) · Bill Lee (b) · Sam Jones (b) · Billy Higgins (d)

Hard bop still developing in 1973, on a musician-owned label, with a spiritual jazz undertow. It is proof that the style did not stop when the critics moved on.

Listen for: The playing is straight hard bop but the album's shape and the modal pieces put it in a different world from a 1959 Blue Note date.

Soul Jazz & Organ Trio 1956–present

Jazz for the neighbourhood bar: Hammond organ, guitar, drums, a hard groove, and no interest in impressing critics.

Philadelphia / Newark / Chicago, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Funky jazz, Groove jazz, Organ combo

The Hammond B-3 organ with a Leslie rotating speaker is the whole sound. One player covers bass with the pedals or the left hand, chords and melody with the right, and can swell the volume with a foot pedal in a way no piano can — which is why the organ trio needs no bassist and can fill a room. Jimmy Smith worked out the vocabulary in the mid-1950s and effectively created a job market: organ, guitar, drums, sometimes tenor, working the chitlin' circuit and neighbourhood taverns rather than concert halls. The music is groove-first, blues-based, often built on simple vamps and 12-bar forms, with long solos and an audience that responds out loud. It was hugely popular and largely dismissed by the jazz press at the time as commercial, which says more about the press than the music. Everything in later jazz-funk, acid jazz and hip-hop-sampled jazz runs back through here — a huge proportion of sampled jazz breaks come from soul jazz records.

What to listen for

  • Hammond organ with that swirling, rotating speaker sound
  • No double bass — the bass line is being played by the organist's left hand or feet
  • A hard backbeat you could dance to, closer to R&B than to swing
  • Long stretches sitting on one chord while the solo builds
  • Audible crowd noise — a lot of these were recorded live in bars

How it works

harmony
Blues and simple functional changes, gospel plagal cadences, and long single-chord vamps. Harmonic ambition is deliberately not the point.
rhythm
Shuffle and backbeat feels drawn from rhythm and blues, with the drummer often playing a boogaloo or a hard 12/8.
form
12-bar blues, simple riff tunes, and modal vamps stretched out for extended solos. Head arrangements, minimal writing.
instrumentation
Hammond B-3 through a Leslie cabinet, electric guitar, drums, and often tenor saxophone. No upright bass — the organ covers it.
improvisation
Long, riff-driven, and openly crowd-pleasing. Repetition and building intensity matter more than harmonic navigation.
timbre
The Leslie speaker's swirl is the defining colour, plus hollow-body guitar with a warm, unhurried attack and a big-toned tenor.

Key figures

  • Jimmy Smith (Hammond organ) — Invented the modern jazz organ vocabulary — bass lines in the left hand, bebop lines in the right — and created the format.
  • Grant Green (guitar) — The most rhythmically pure single-note guitarist of the style; almost never played chords in solos.
  • Stanley Turrentine (tenor saxophone) — The definitive soul jazz tenor tone — huge, warm, blues-first.
  • Jack McDuff (Hammond organ) — Ran the hardest-grooving of the working organ bands and gave George Benson his first job.
  • Ramsey Lewis (piano) — Proved the piano trio could do the same job, and took soul jazz into the pop charts in 1965.

Labels: Blue Note · Prestige · Argo/Cadet · Verve

Contested

Where hard bop ends and soul jazz begins. Horace Silver, Cannonball Adderley and Lee Morgan sit on both sides. Some writers also fold Ramsey Lewis and the piano-based groove records into a separate 'crossover' category. The genre's low critical standing at the time has been substantially revised.

Came out of

  • Hard Bop — direct descendant, 1956, via rhythm, harmony, instrumentation. Soul jazz is hard bop with the gospel and R&B content promoted from flavour to structure, and the Hammond organ replacing piano and bass. Horace Silver's funky tunes are the shared ancestor both styles claim; the split happens when the backbeat and the vamp stop being devices and become the format.
  • Kansas City Jazz — parallel influence, 1958, via form, rhythm. The riff-over-blues construction and the long open jam are Kansas City procedures reappearing in a neighbourhood bar twenty years later. Interpretation: the organ trio's working economy — nightly, local, audience-facing — is closer to 1930s KC than to the 1950s concert circuit.

Fed into

  • Jazz Fusion — parallel influence, 1969
  • Jazz-Funk — direct descendant, 1969
  • Acid Jazz — revival of, 1988
  • Ethio-Jazz — direct descendant, 1969

What to hear

gateway

Jimmy Smith — Back at the Chicken Shack gateway

recorded 1960, released 1963 · Blue Note BLP 4117 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Back at the Chicken Shack”

Jimmy Smith (org) · Stanley Turrentine (ts) · Kenny Burrell (g) · Donald Bailey (d)

The organ trio plus tenor, at its most relaxed. Nothing is being proved and the groove never stops, which is precisely the format's proposition.

Listen for: There is no bass player. Smith is playing the bass line with his left hand and the pedals while soloing with his right — listen for the walk continuing underneath the solo.

Note: Recorded April 1960 in the same session block as 'Midnight Special'; Blue Note held it until 1963.

The title track runs past twenty minutes over a slow blues and simply does not need to end. It is the organ jam session as a form.

Listen for: Each soloist enters and takes as long as they want. The structure is entirely determined by how the room is responding.

The title track was supposed to run seven minutes and the band misread the form, so it runs fifteen. Nobody stopped them and it is the best thing on the record.

Listen for: Green plays single notes almost exclusively — no chords, no doubling — with a rhythmic placement that comes from horn players rather than guitarists.

Soul jazz crossing into the 1970s and into CTI's glossier production, with the groove intact and the solos still substantial. The title track became a standard.

Listen for: Turrentine's tone is enormous and never strained — the sound of a saxophonist who came up playing behind R&B singers.

A piano trio taking a soul record into the pop top five by playing it as a groove with an audience clapping along. The commercial success reset what a jazz trio could aim at.

Listen for: The crowd is not incidental — they are part of the arrangement, and the trio plays to them rather than past them.

Listen for: The organ voicings are quartal and open rather than bluesy, and Elvin Jones is playing his Coltrane-quartet triplet feel. This is soul jazz's format with modal jazz's content.

Shirley Scott (org) · Stanley Turrentine (ts) · Bob Cranshaw (b) · Otis Finch (d)

Scott was the major organist the histories keep leaving out, with a lighter touch than Jimmy Smith and a stronger harmonic sense. Recorded live at Birdland with Turrentine, then her husband.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Third Stream 1957–present

Composers trying to get classical form and jazz improvisation into the same piece without either one winning.

New York / Boston, USA · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called Jazz-classical fusion

Gunther Schuller coined the term in a 1957 lecture at Brandeis University, describing a third stream flowing between the classical and jazz mainstreams. The premise: use the compositional resources of Western art music — twelve-tone rows, fugue, extended form, orchestral instrumentation — while keeping improvisation as a live element rather than decoration. The Modern Jazz Quartet under John Lewis had been circling the idea since 1952 with baroque counterpoint over blues; Schuller, Charles Mingus, George Russell and Jimmy Giuffre pushed it further. The problems are obvious in hindsight and were obvious then: classical players could not improvise convincingly, jazz players found the written material stiff, and a lot of the results sound like two musics politely taking turns. But the successful examples are genuinely strange and good, and the idea did not die — it re-emerges in ECM's chamber jazz, in Anthony Braxton's compositional systems, and in almost every contemporary large-ensemble writer. Schuller later expanded the term to mean any fusion of an improvised and a notated tradition, not only European ones.

What to listen for

  • Strings or woodwinds that are clearly a classical section, not a pop string sweetening
  • Passages of counterpoint that sound like Bach, followed by a swinging solo
  • Dissonance that never resolves, used on purpose
  • Long pieces in several distinct movements rather than head–solos–head
  • An audible seam where the written part stops and improvisation begins

How it works

harmony
Twelve-tone and freely atonal writing alongside jazz harmony; polytonality and unresolved dissonance used structurally.
rhythm
Often deliberately un-swung in the composed sections, with metric modulation and irregular meters, then a swing feel entering with the improvisation.
form
Extended, through-composed structures — sonata-like development, fugue, theme and variations — with improvised sections written into the architecture.
instrumentation
Jazz group plus string quartet, chamber orchestra, or full symphony; also the Modern Jazz Quartet's austere vibes-piano-bass-drums.
improvisation
Constrained and located — the soloist improvises within a specified section, sometimes on a row or a set of pitches rather than changes.
timbre
Concert-hall clarity and dynamic range. Strings, woodwinds and percussion used for colour in ways rare elsewhere in jazz.

Key figures

  • Gunther Schuller (composition, French horn, criticism) — Named the movement, wrote much of its repertoire, and later wrote the two-volume history of jazz that shaped how it is taught.
  • John Lewis (piano, composition) — The Modern Jazz Quartet applied fugal and rondo procedures to blues material for four decades without gimmickry.
  • Charles Mingus (bass, composition) — His extended works use classical scale and ambition while remaining rooted in blues and gospel; he rejected the label but fits the description.
  • George Russell (composition, theory) — Combined a rigorous theoretical system with big-band writing that neither classical nor jazz orthodoxy could claim.
  • Jimmy Giuffre (clarinet, composition) — His 1961 trio with Paul Bley and Steve Swallow is chamber music and free improvisation at the same time.

Labels: Atlantic · Columbia · Verve · ECM (later)

Contested

Whether it is a genre, a project, or a failure. Many musicians it describes — Mingus above all — rejected the label. Schuller himself later widened the definition so far that it arguably stopped naming anything specific. Critics of the term argue it presumes classical music is a separate, higher stream that jazz must be married into.

Came out of

  • Cool Jazz — direct descendant, 1957, via form, instrumentation, harmony. The nonet's arrangers — John Lewis, Gunther Schuller, Gil Evans — are the same people who then argued for a formal marriage with concert music. Schuller played French horn on the nonet sessions before he coined 'third stream' at Brandeis in 1957.
  • West Coast Jazz — direct descendant, 1957, via form, instrumentation. The Los Angeles scene's appetite for fugue, canon and unusual instrumentation put it halfway to third stream already. Jimmy Giuffre's drumless chamber groups and Shorty Rogers' woodwind writing sit on the boundary.

Fed into

  • Modal Jazz — parallel influence, 1959
  • Free Jazz — parallel influence, 1960
  • AACM & Great Black Music — parallel influence, 1967
  • ECM & Nordic Chamber Jazz — direct descendant, 1970

What to hear

gateway

Modern Jazz Quartet — Third Stream Music gateway

recorded 1957, released 1960 · Atlantic 1345 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Sketch for Double String Quartet”

John Lewis (p) · Milt Jackson (vib) · Percy Heath (b) · Connie Kay (d) · Jimmy Giuffre (cl) · Beaux Arts String Quartet

The record that carries the name, with the MJQ playing alongside a string quartet and Giuffre's trio. It is the most accessible entry to an idea that usually arrives as a lecture.

Listen for: When the string quartet plays, it plays classical music; when the MJQ plays, it swings. Listening for the seam is the most useful thing you can do with third stream.

Charles Mingus — The Black Saint and the Sinner Lady core

recorded/released 1963 · Impulse! A-35 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Track A – Solo Dancer”

Charles Mingus (b, p, comp) · Charlie Mariano (as) · Jaki Byard (p) · Quentin Jackson (tb) · Dannie Richmond (d) · Bob Hammer (arr)

An eleven-piece band playing a continuous six-part ballet score with flamenco guitar, gospel shouting and heavy tape editing. Mingus rejected the third stream label and made its most convincing record anyway.

Listen for: The whole thing was assembled with overdubs and splices, which was near-heretical for jazz in 1963. Mingus treated the tape as a compositional surface.

Gunther Schuller / John Lewis — Jazz Abstractions core

recorded 1960, released 1961 · Atlantic 1365 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Abstraction”

Ornette Coleman (as) · Eric Dolphy (as, bcl) · Jim Hall (g) · Bill Evans (p) · Scott LaFaro (b) · Gunther Schuller (comp, cond)

Schuller writing twelve-tone frameworks for Ornette Coleman and Eric Dolphy to improvise inside. It is the one third stream record where the improvisers are more radical than the composition.

Listen for: 'Abstraction' puts Coleman in front of a string quartet playing a serial texture. He plays exactly as he would with his own group and the collision is the piece.

Stan Getz — Focus core

recorded/released 1961 · Verve V6-8412 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “I'm Late, I'm Late”

Stan Getz (ts) · Eddie Sauter (comp, arr) · string orchestra · Roy Haynes (d)

Eddie Sauter wrote a seven-movement work for string orchestra and left no written part for Getz at all — he improvises across the whole thing. Getz called it his favourite of his own records.

Listen for: The strings are not accompanying. They have their own complete piece, and Getz is a second, unscripted layer on top of it.

George Russell — Jazz in the Space Age core

recorded/released 1960 · Decca DL 9219 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Chromatic Universe – Part I”

George Russell (comp, cond) · Bill Evans (p) · Paul Bley (p) · Hal McKusick (as) · Milt Hinton (b) · Don Lamond (d)

Two pianists improvising simultaneously inside a large-ensemble score built on Russell's Lydian theory. It is ambitious in a way that only a theorist with a band would attempt.

Listen for: Evans and Bley improvise against each other with no agreed harmony beyond the scale relationships Russell specified. It works far more often than it should.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jimmy Giuffre 3 — Free Fall core

recorded 1962, released 1963 · Columbia CL 1964 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Propulsion”

Jimmy Giuffre (cl) · Paul Bley (p) · Steve Swallow (b)

Solo clarinet pieces alongside trio improvisations at almost inaudible volume. Columbia dropped him afterwards and the trio broke up because they were earning nothing.

Listen for: The dynamic range is extreme and downward — much of this is quieter than a conversation. Turn it up and the detail is enormous.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Charles Mingus — Mingus Ah Um deep

recorded/released 1959 · Columbia CL 1370 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Goodbye Pork Pie Hat”

Charles Mingus (b, comp) · John Handy (as) · Booker Ervin (ts) · Shafi Hadi (ts) · Jimmy Knepper (tb) · Horace Parlan (p) · Dannie Richmond (d)

Mingus at his most direct: gospel, blues, Ellington homage and an explicitly political protest song, all played by a band he conducted by shouting at them mid-take.

Listen for: 'Fables of Faubus' is about the governor who blocked school integration in Arkansas. Columbia refused to let the lyrics be sung, so the sarcasm has to come through the horns.

Sauter-Finegan Orchestra — New Directions in Music deep

recorded 1952, released 1953 · RCA Victor LPM-3115 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Doodletown Fifers” · medium confidence

Eddie Sauter (comp, arr) · Bill Finegan (comp, arr)

Two of swing's best arrangers building an orchestra with harp, recorders, percussion and woodwinds, aimed at concert listening rather than dancing. It anticipates third stream by five years and is largely forgotten.

Listen for: The instrumentation is closer to a film score than a jazz band, and the group barely swings by design. Whether that counts as a failure depends on what you think jazz is.

Anthony Braxton — For Alto deep

recorded 1969, released 1971 · Delmark DS-420/421 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “To Composer John Cage”

Anthony Braxton (as)

The first full-length album of solo saxophone improvisation, with pieces dedicated to John Cage and Cecil Taylor. It is where the compositional and improvisational streams meet in a single unaccompanied player.

Listen for: Each piece has a defined structural premise — a set of intervals, a rhythmic cell — that Braxton then works through. This is composition executed live, not free blowing.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Modal Jazz 1958–present

Take away most of the chords and see what a soloist does with the space.

New York, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Modal improvisation

Bebop and hard bop hand the improviser a chord every two beats; modal jazz might hand them one scale for sixteen bars. The reduction is the innovation. With the harmony slowed almost to a stop, a soloist can no longer succeed by outlining changes accurately, and has to build a line out of melodic shape, motif and colour instead. George Russell's Lydian Chromatic Concept, published in 1953, gave the idea a theoretical frame; Miles Davis's 'Milestones' in 1958 and Kind of Blue in 1959 gave it a sound. The consequences show up everywhere: quartal voicings in the piano (stacked fourths, which are ambiguous about what key you are in), pedal points in the bass, drummers freed from marking chord changes and able to play texture, and modes borrowed from outside the major/minor system. Coltrane pushed it further and harder than Davis did, turning single-chord vamps into ecstatic marathons, and by way of that the style opens directly into spiritual jazz and free playing.

What to listen for

  • The harmony sits still — you can wait a long time for the chord to change
  • Piano chords built from stacked fourths, which sound open and unresolved
  • A bass note held or repeated under everything (a pedal point)
  • Melodies that sound slightly foreign because the scale isn't ordinary major or minor
  • A soloist building intensity by repetition rather than by moving through chords

How it works

harmony
Static or very slow-moving. One scale or mode held for long stretches, with quartal and suspended voicings that avoid stating a clear tonal centre.
rhythm
Freed from chord-marking. Bass often plays pedal points or ostinatos; drums play colour and wave-shape rather than navigation.
form
Long sections over a single mode, sometimes with a second mode as contrast. Forms can be irregular lengths because they are not tied to song structure.
instrumentation
Standard quintet or sextet, but the piano's role changes fundamentally — texture and colour over functional comping.
improvisation
Melodic and motivic. With no changes to navigate, the soloist must generate interest from shape, repetition, register and intensity.
timbre
Space becomes audible. Bill Evans' voicings and Coltrane's overtone-rich upper register are the two ends of the style's colour range.

Key figures

  • Miles Davis (trumpet, bandleading) — Made the theoretical idea audible and commercially enormous with Kind of Blue in 1959.
  • John Coltrane (tenor and soprano saxophone) — Took modal frameworks to their limit — long vamps played at maximum intensity, which is where free jazz starts for him.
  • Bill Evans (piano) — Supplied the voicing language, borrowed partly from Ravel and Debussy, that makes modal harmony sound the way it does.
  • George Russell (composition, theory) — Wrote the Lydian Chromatic Concept, the theoretical framework that gave modal thinking a vocabulary.
  • McCoy Tyner (piano) — Turned quartal voicings into a percussive, pentatonic-driven style that became the default sound of modal piano.
  • Herbie Hancock (piano, composition) — Wrote modal pieces with ambiguous, floating harmony that avoid both functional resolution and static vamps.

Labels: Columbia · Impulse! · Blue Note · Atlantic

Contested

Whether modal jazz is a genre or a technique. Almost every post-1960 style uses modal devices, and albums like Kind of Blue get filed as modal, cool, or post-bop depending on the writer. Whether Kind of Blue is modal jazz or a modal-flavoured hard bop record is a genuinely live argument.

Came out of

  • Bebop — reaction against, 1958, via harmony, improvisation. Bebop handed a soloist a chord every two beats; modal jazz hands them one scale for sixteen bars. The reduction is aimed squarely at bebop's harmonic congestion — Miles Davis described wanting fewer chords so there was more to do with each one.
  • Cool Jazz — direct descendant, 1958, via harmony, timbre. Bill Evans brought Ravel- and Debussy-derived voicings into the Davis sextet, and those quartal, ambiguous chords are what make static harmony sound rich rather than empty. Davis himself carried the cool tone and the taste for space directly across.
  • Third Stream — parallel influence, 1959, via harmony. George Russell's Lydian Chromatic Concept, published in 1953, supplied the theoretical frame that let musicians think in scales rather than chords. Russell taught it to Davis and Evans among others; it is the closest thing modal jazz has to a written source.

Fed into

  • Post-Bop — direct descendant, 1964
  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1961
  • Spiritual Jazz — direct descendant, 1965
  • Jazz Fusion — fusion of, 1969
  • ECM & Nordic Chamber Jazz — direct descendant, 1971
  • J-Jazz — direct descendant, 1969

What to hear

gateway

Miles Davis — Kind of Blue gateway

recorded/released 1959 · Columbia CL 1355 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “So What”

Miles Davis (tp) · John Coltrane (ts) · Cannonball Adderley (as) · Bill Evans (p) · Wynton Kelly (p) · Paul Chambers (b) · Jimmy Cobb (d)

The best-selling jazz album ever made, and it earns it. Davis brought sketches rather than charts to two sessions and the band read them nearly cold, which is why nothing sounds worked over.

Listen for: 'So What' gives the soloist two chords in thirty-two bars. Listen to how Coltrane and Adderley respond completely differently to the same emptiness — one fills it, one plays around it.

Miles Davis — Milestones core

recorded/released 1958 · Columbia CL 1193 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Milestones”

Miles Davis (tp) · John Coltrane (ts) · Cannonball Adderley (as) · Red Garland (p) · Paul Chambers (b) · Philly Joe Jones (d)

The title track is the first fully modal piece Davis recorded, a year before Kind of Blue. The rest of the album is hard bop, so you can hear the two languages side by side.

Listen for: 'Milestones' alternates between two modes with no chord changes inside either. Then play 'Dr. Jackle' from the same album and hear how much harder the changes are moving.

The title track, recorded live at the Village Vanguard, is 'So What' taken at speed and stretched to fourteen minutes. It is modal jazz turned into an endurance event.

Listen for: Tyner's left hand plays almost nothing but stacked fourths, and Elvin Jones stops marking bars entirely — the pulse is a wave rather than a grid.

The trio that made the bass a melodic equal rather than a timekeeper. Scott LaFaro died in a car crash ten days after this recording, at twenty-five.

Listen for: LaFaro is not walking. He plays counter-melodies in the upper register while Motian keeps time with brushes and Evans leaves gaps for both of them.

Tyner's first record after leaving Coltrane, with Elvin Jones alongside him. It codifies the modal piano language — quartal voicings, pentatonic right hand, pedal points — that became the default.

Listen for: 'Passion Dance' sits on one chord for its entire solo section. The interest is generated entirely by rhythm, register and Tyner's left-hand attack.

Herbie Hancock (p) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · George Coleman (ts) · Ron Carter (b) · Tony Williams (d)

Modal harmony that floats rather than sits still. The suspended chords never resolve and the whole record has a weightlessness nothing else in 1965 achieved.

Listen for: The title track uses four suspended chords that each last four bars and none of which function as a tonic. Your ear keeps waiting for a resolution that is never coming.

Shorter with three-quarters of the Coltrane quartet, writing modal pieces with a darker and more elliptical cast than Coltrane's own.

Bossa Nova & Brazilian Jazz 1958–present

Samba played quietly, with jazz harmony and a voice barely above speech.

Rio de Janeiro, Brazil · Global Currents · also called Bossa nova, Samba-jazz, MPB-jazz

Bossa nova is samba with the volume and the percussion battery removed and the harmony made much more complicated. João Gilberto worked out the guitar pattern — a syncopated thumb-and-fingers figure that implies a whole samba percussion section on one instrument — and a way of singing almost without projection, so close to the microphone that it sounds like someone thinking out loud. Antônio Carlos Jobim wrote the songs, with chromatic inner voices and chord movements that come as much from Debussy and Chopin as from any Brazilian source. Vinicius de Moraes wrote many of the lyrics. It arrived in the US in 1962 through Charlie Byrd and Stan Getz, and 'The Girl from Ipanema' turned it into a global commercial phenomenon so complete that it became lounge wallpaper, which has damaged its reputation ever since. Underneath the easy-listening reception there is a serious body of work, and the wider Brazilian instrumental tradition — Hermeto Pascoal, Egberto Gismonti, Airto Moreira, Milton Nascimento — pushed far past bossa into territory that fed 1970s fusion directly.

What to listen for

  • One nylon-string guitar playing a syncopated pattern that implies a whole percussion section
  • Singing that is quiet, conversational and almost never held out
  • Chords that shift in unexpected directions under a very simple melody
  • Even eighth notes with no swing lilt
  • Drums played with brushes and a rim-click, kept very low in the mix

How it works

harmony
The most harmonically complex popular song repertoire of its era: chromatic descending inner lines, altered dominants, and modulations that arrive without warning.
rhythm
The bossa guitar pattern displaces accents across two bars, derived from samba but stripped to essentials. Even eighths, soft dynamics, brushes or light sticks on the kit.
form
Song forms, often through-composed and irregular rather than 32-bar AABA. Instrumental versions extend them with solo choruses.
instrumentation
Nylon-string guitar, voice, piano, bass, drums with brushes; often flute or saxophone. Percussion is minimal compared with samba.
improvisation
Restrained and melodic in bossa proper; the later samba-jazz and Brazilian instrumental scenes are far more virtuosic.
timbre
Soft, warm, close-miked. Gilberto's near-whispered singing and the nylon-string guitar define the palette.

Key figures

  • João Gilberto (guitar, voice) — Invented both the guitar pattern and the vocal delivery; without him bossa nova does not exist as a sound.
  • Antônio Carlos Jobim (composition, piano, guitar) — Wrote the repertoire, and wrote it with a harmonic sophistication that made it durable jazz material.
  • Stan Getz (tenor saxophone) — The American who carried it to a mass audience, with a tone light enough not to crush the material.
  • Hermeto Pascoal (multi-instrumentalist, composition) — The Brazilian tradition at its most unrestrained — folk forms, free improvisation, and sounds made from anything at hand.
  • Airto Moreira (percussion) — Brought Brazilian percussion into American fusion, appearing on Bitches Brew and with Weather Report and Return to Forever.
  • Egberto Gismonti (guitar, piano, composition) — Combined Brazilian folk material, classical technique and improvisation, mostly through ECM.

Labels: Odeon · Elenco · Verve · ECM · Milestone

Contested

Whether bossa nova is jazz at all. In Brazil it is classified as popular song, and Brazilian musicians often find the American framing of it as a jazz style distorting. Its 1960s American reception also flattened a sharply defined art movement into background music, a reading that Brazilian critics have pushed back on for sixty years.

Came out of

  • Cool Jazz — direct descendant, 1958, via harmony, timbre, rhythm. The Rio musicians who built bossa nova were listening to Mulligan, Chet Baker and Barney Kessel records — the soft attack, the restraint and the absence of vibrato come from there. Jobim's harmony draws on Debussy as much as on jazz, but the performance manner is cool jazz's.
  • Vocal Jazz & the Songbook — parallel influence, 1959, via timbre, repertoire, form. Gilberto's near-inaudible delivery is a deliberate rejection of the big-voiced Brazilian singing of the era, and the model for singing quietly and conversationally into a microphone was American. The songbook idea — a body of harmonically rich standards that instrumentalists then colonise — repeats exactly.

Fed into

  • Jazz Fusion — parallel influence, 1972

What to hear

gateway

Stan Getz & João Gilberto — Getz/Gilberto gateway

recorded 1963, released 1964 · Verve V6-8545 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “The Girl from Ipanema”

Stan Getz (ts) · João Gilberto (g, voc) · Antônio Carlos Jobim (p) · Astrud Gilberto (voc) · Sebastião Neto (b) · Milton Banana (d)

It sold enormously, won Album of the Year, and made bossa nova a global commodity — and it is also, underneath the ubiquity, a very well-made record with three principals at their best.

Listen for: João Gilberto's guitar alone. The right hand is doing what an entire samba percussion section does, and the singing sits so far inside the microphone that it is almost private.

Pascoal is the Brazilian tradition without any of the restraint bossa nova imposed — folk forms, free improvisation, and on this record a chorus of live piglets used as an instrument.

Listen for: The piglets are not a joke about avant-garde music; Pascoal tunes and arranges them. His whole practice is that any sound can be composed with.

The Minas Gerais collective's double album, made under dictatorship, mixing Brazilian folk, Beatles-derived song, jazz harmony and choral writing. Wayne Shorter recruited Nascimento for Native Dancer afterwards.

Listen for: The vocal writing — Nascimento's falsetto against layered harmony parts — has almost no precedent in either Brazilian or American music.

Free Jazz 1959–present

Jazz that abandoned the chord changes, and then, in some hands, the beat and the form as well.

New York / Los Angeles, USA · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called The New Thing, Energy music

The chord sequence had been the container for jazz improvisation for forty years. Free jazz threw it out. Ornette Coleman's quartet — no piano, so nothing stating harmony — played tunes with clear melodies and then improvised on the melodic and emotional content rather than a fixed harmonic grid, keeping a pulse. Cecil Taylor came from the other side, with a percussive, dense, atonal piano attack rooted in twentieth-century composition as much as in jazz. Albert Ayler kept marches, folk melodies and hymns as raw material, then obliterated them with a huge shuddering tone and mass collective improvisation. Coltrane arrived in 1965 with Ascension and took his audience with him. Common features: no pre-set changes, frequently no fixed meter, collective rather than sequential improvisation, and an expanded idea of acceptable sound — squeals, overtones, screams. The reception was hostile in a way that is hard to overstate; musicians lost work over it. The politics were inseparable, running alongside the civil rights and Black Power movements, and several key figures framed the music explicitly in those terms.

What to listen for

  • No repeating chord pattern to follow — the harmony never comes around
  • Several players soloing at once instead of taking turns
  • Saxophones screaming, honking and producing more than one note at a time
  • The beat disappearing entirely for long stretches
  • A clear, almost naive melody at the start and end of something otherwise chaotic

How it works

harmony
No pre-agreed progression. Pitch centres emerge and dissolve; players may follow a melodic contour or a mood rather than a key.
rhythm
Pulse is optional. Ornette's groups mostly keep one; Taylor and Ayler's often abandon meter entirely for waves of density.
form
Frequently a stated theme, then open collective improvisation, then a return. Duration is determined by the performance, not the form.
instrumentation
Often piano-less, so no instrument is fixing the harmony. Double bassists sometimes doubled; two drummers appear regularly.
improvisation
Simultaneous and collective. Everyone can lead at once; the New Orleans front line comes back in radically altered form.
timbre
Enormously expanded — overblowing, altissimo screams, split tones, vocalised cries. Sound as expression, not as tone production.

Key figures

  • Ornette Coleman (alto saxophone, composition) — Removed the chord changes while keeping melody and pulse; his 1959–61 Atlantic records are the founding statement.
  • Cecil Taylor (piano) — Built a percussive, atonal, physically overwhelming piano language from a different starting point than Coleman's.
  • Albert Ayler (tenor saxophone) — Used hymns, marches and folk tunes as launch material and a vast vibrato-drenched tone; the most emotionally extreme voice in the music.
  • John Coltrane (tenor saxophone) — His move into free playing after 1965 gave the style credibility and an audience it could not otherwise have reached.
  • Sun Ra (piano, keyboards, composition) — Ran the Arkestra from the mid-1950s with a mix of big-band writing, electronics, free improvisation and cosmology all at once.
  • Archie Shepp (tenor saxophone) — Tied the music explicitly to Black political struggle in his writing and speech as well as his playing.

Labels: Atlantic · ESP-Disk · Impulse! · Blue Note · BYG Actuel

Contested

Whether 'free jazz' is one thing. Ornette Coleman's melodic, pulse-keeping music and Cecil Taylor's atonal density have almost nothing in common technically, and Coleman himself disliked the term. 'The New Thing' was the period label; 'energy music' names only one wing of it.

Came out of

  • Hard Bop — reaction against, 1959, via harmony, form, social-context. Ornette Coleman came up playing rhythm-and-blues and bebop in Texas and Los Angeles, and the quartet that arrived at the Five Spot in 1959 kept hard bop's melodic directness while removing the chord sequence entirely. The hostility from hard bop musicians was immediate and personal.
  • Modal Jazz — direct descendant, 1961, via harmony, improvisation, form. Modal jazz had already reduced harmonic motion to almost nothing; free jazz took the last step and removed the pre-agreed frame altogether. Coltrane's route runs directly through this — the extended vamps of 'Impressions' become the open playing of 1965 without an obvious break.
  • Third Stream — parallel influence, 1960, via harmony, form, timbre. Cecil Taylor studied at the New England Conservatory and his atonal cluster language owes more to Bartók, Stockhausen and Henry Cowell than to any jazz pianist. The permission to abandon tonality came partly from European concert music, which third stream had already brought into the room.
  • New Orleans Jazz — parallel influence, 1961, via improvisation, instrumentation. Interpretation, though one participants made themselves: collective simultaneous improvisation with no designated soloist is a New Orleans procedure returning after forty years of one-at-a-time soloing. Ornette Coleman's Free Jazz double quartet and Albert Ayler's use of marches and hymns both make the connection audible rather than merely arguable.

Fed into

  • Post-Bop — parallel influence, 1964
  • AACM & Great Black Music — direct descendant, 1965
  • Spiritual Jazz — direct descendant, 1966
  • European Free Improvisation — reaction against, 1966
  • Loft Jazz — direct descendant, 1970
  • Free Funk & Harmolodics — direct descendant, 1975
  • ECM & Nordic Chamber Jazz — direct descendant, 1970
  • J-Jazz — direct descendant, 1969
  • Downtown & Punk Jazz — direct descendant, 1978
  • UK Jazz Revival — direct descendant, 2016

What to hear

gateway

Ornette Coleman — The Shape of Jazz to Come gateway

recorded/released 1959 · Atlantic SD 1317 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Lonely Woman”

Ornette Coleman (as) · Don Cherry (cor) · Charlie Haden (b) · Billy Higgins (d)

The most approachable free jazz record ever made. The melodies are singable, the pulse is steady, and there is simply no chord sequence — which is the only thing that was actually removed.

Listen for: 'Lonely Woman' has a clear, mournful theme that anyone can follow. Then notice that the bass and horns are not agreeing on a tempo, and no piano is telling you what key you are in.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

core

Ornette Coleman Double Quartet — Free Jazz: A Collective Improvisation core

recorded 1960, released 1961 · Atlantic SD 1364 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Free Jazz”

Ornette Coleman (as) · Eric Dolphy (bcl) · Don Cherry (pocket tp) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Charlie Haden (b) · Scott LaFaro (b) · Billy Higgins (d) · Ed Blackwell (d)

Two quartets, one in each stereo channel, improvising together for thirty-seven unbroken minutes with only brief written cues. It gave the movement its name and remains its most extreme statement of collective method.

Listen for: Play it with headphones. The two quartets are completely separated left and right, and you can follow one band at a time or hear them collide.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Cecil Taylor — Unit Structures core

recorded/released 1966 · Blue Note BLP 4237 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Steps”

Cecil Taylor (p) · Jimmy Lyons (as) · Ken McIntyre (as, ob, bcl) · Henry Grimes (b) · Alan Silva (b) · Andrew Cyrille (d)

Taylor's densest and most organised record, on a major label, with his own written notes explaining the structural units. It is atonal, percussive, and completely unlike Coleman's version of freedom.

Listen for: The piano is being played as a percussion instrument — clusters, forearms, extreme velocity — but the pitch material is organised, not random. Follow one interval and you will hear it return.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Albert Ayler Trio — Spiritual Unity core

recorded 1964, released 1965 · ESP-Disk ESP 1002 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Ghosts: First Variation”

Albert Ayler (ts) · Gary Peacock (b) · Sunny Murray (d)

Thirty minutes, three players, no chords, no meter, and melodies that sound like nursery rhymes or Salvation Army hymns played by someone in distress. It is the most emotionally extreme record in jazz.

Listen for: 'Ghosts' has a simple, almost childish tune. Ayler states it plainly and then destroys it with a vibrato so wide it becomes a howl, then returns to the tune intact.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

John Coltrane — Ascension core

recorded 1965, released 1966 · Impulse! A-95 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Ascension (Edition II)”

John Coltrane (ts) · Pharoah Sanders (ts) · Archie Shepp (ts) · Marion Brown (as) · John Tchicai (as) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Dewey Johnson (tp) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Jimmy Garrison (b) · Art Davis (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

Eleven musicians, forty minutes, alternating between full collective improvisation and individual solos. Coltrane's audience followed him here, which is why free jazz got a hearing it would not otherwise have had.

Listen for: The structure is simple and audible: massed ensemble, then a solo, then massed ensemble again. Once you hear the alternation the wall of sound becomes navigable.

Note: Two complete takes exist; Impulse! substituted Edition II for Edition I shortly after release.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Sun Ra and His Solar Arkestra — The Heliocentric Worlds of Sun Ra, Volume One core

recorded/released 1965 · ESP-Disk ESP 1014 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Heliocentric”

Sun Ra (p, bass marimba, tympani) · Marshall Allen (as, piccolo) · John Gilmore (ts) · Pat Patrick (bs) · Ronnie Boykins (b)

The Arkestra at its most abstract, using tuned percussion, tympani and space rather than horn blowing. It sounds closer to Varèse than to jazz, which is the point.

Listen for: There is almost no steady pulse and a great deal of tuned percussion. Sun Ra was working with orchestral colour and silence a decade before the AACM made it a method.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Archie Shepp — Fire Music deep

recorded/released 1965 · Impulse! A-86 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Hambone”

Archie Shepp (ts) · Ted Curson (tp) · Joseph Orange (tb) · Marion Brown (as) · Reggie Johnson (b) · Joe Chambers (d)

Shepp ties the music explicitly to Black political struggle — there is an elegy for Malcolm X with spoken text — while also playing a Duke Ellington ballad straight. The combination is deliberate.

Listen for: 'Malcolm, Malcolm — Semper Malcolm' is Shepp reciting his own poem over the band. Free jazz's politics are usually inferred; here they are stated.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

John Coltrane — Interstellar Space deep

recorded 1967, released 1974 · Impulse! ASD-9277 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Mars”

John Coltrane (ts) · Rashied Ali (d)

Duets for tenor saxophone and drums, recorded in February 1967, five months before Coltrane's death. There is nothing left to hold onto — no bass, no harmony, no meter.

Listen for: Each piece is named for a planet and starts with bells. Ali is not keeping time; he is producing a continuous field that Coltrane moves through.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Charles Mingus — Changes One deep

recorded 1974, released 1975 · Atlantic SD 1677 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Sue's Changes”

Charles Mingus (b) · George Adams (ts, voc) · Jack Walrath (tp) · Don Pullen (p) · Dannie Richmond (d)

Mingus's last great band, where the tunes are solidly composed and the solos go completely outside. Don Pullen plays with his knuckles and the record still swings.

Listen for: Pullen's solos start inside the changes and end in clusters played with the backs of his hands, then return. The transition between languages is seamless and audible.

J-Jazz 1961–present

Japanese players taking modal and spiritual jazz somewhere harder, denser and more audiophile-recorded than anyone in America was.

Tokyo, Japan · Global Currents · also called Japanese jazz, Deep modern jazz from Japan

Japan had a jazz scene from the 1920s, but the thing worth hearing starts in the late 1960s, when a generation of players stopped copying American records and started producing something with its own weight. The reference points are Coltrane's quartet, Blue Note post-bop and free jazz, but the results are distinct: harder-edged, often darker, with a taste for extended modal vamps, unusual instrumentation, and a physical intensity in the playing. Yosuke Yamashita's trio played free jazz with the violence of a rock band. Masabumi Kikuchi and Terumasa Hino went deep into electric fusion. Masahiko Togashi built a percussion language after a spinal injury left him unable to use his legs. Traditional Japanese instruments and materials appear — shakuhachi, koto, folk melodies — but usually as structure rather than decoration. Crucially, the independent labels, above all Three Blind Mice, founded in 1970 by Takeshi Fujii with the explicit ambition of being 'the Blue Note of Japan', recorded the music with extraordinary audio quality, which is part of why the reissue market went so hard for it after the BBE compilations from 2018 onward.

What to listen for

  • Modal vamps held far longer than an American record of the same period would
  • Bass and drums recorded with startling clarity and presence
  • Piano attacked percussively, sometimes almost violently
  • Traditional Japanese instruments used structurally rather than as exotic colour
  • Long slow builds from near-silence to full intensity across ten minutes or more

How it works

harmony
Modal and post-bop derived, with a marked preference for minor modes, quartal voicings and long static vamps.
rhythm
Often heavier and more insistent than American models; rock and funk backbeats appear alongside free time and traditional Japanese metrical ideas.
form
Extended pieces, frequently side-long, with slow builds from near-silence to collective intensity.
instrumentation
Standard jazz groups plus electric keyboards, and occasionally shakuhachi, koto, biwa or taiko.
improvisation
Physical and sustained. The free wing plays at very high energy; the modal wing favours long, patient development.
timbre
Exceptional recording quality — Three Blind Mice and Nippon Columbia's engineers captured bass and drums with unusual depth and dynamic range.

Key figures

  • Terumasa Hino (trumpet) — The most internationally visible Japanese jazz musician of the era; moved from hard bop through free to electric fusion.
  • Yosuke Yamashita (piano) — His drummerless-free trio played with a physical ferocity that made European free-jazz audiences take Japan seriously in the 1970s.
  • Masabumi Kikuchi (piano) — Moved between Gil Evans' orchestra, electric fusion and, much later, an extremely spare ECM trio; one of the most restless careers in the music.
  • Masahiko Togashi (percussion) — Rebuilt his entire approach to the kit after a 1969 injury, producing a uniquely spacious percussion language.
  • Sadao Watanabe (alto saxophone, flute) — Studied at Berklee and returned to teach; the central populariser and educator of the Japanese scene.
  • Toshiko Akiyoshi (piano, composition, bandleading) — The first Japanese musician to break through in the US, and a major big-band composer who wrote Japanese material into the idiom.

Labels: Three Blind Mice · Nippon Columbia · Victor Japan · Nippon Phonogram/Philips · BBE (reissues)

Contested

Whether 'J-jazz' is a genre or a nationality with a reissue market attached. The term was popularised by European compilers in the 2010s rather than by Japanese musicians, and it lumps free jazz, spiritual jazz, fusion and hard bop together on the basis of geography. This guide keeps it because the scene had its own labels, its own recording aesthetic and a distinct set of shared reference points — but the objection is fair.

Came out of

  • Hard Bop — direct descendant, 1961, via harmony, form, instrumentation. Japanese players learned from Blue Note records and initially reproduced the format exactly. The first decade of Japanese modern jazz is hard bop played extremely well and without much local inflection; what follows is the departure.
  • Modal Jazz — direct descendant, 1969, via harmony, form. The Coltrane quartet is the central reference point for the Japanese scene, and its modal vamps get stretched further there than in the US — side-long single-mode pieces are common on Three Blind Mice and Nippon Columbia sessions.
  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1969, via improvisation, timbre, rhythm. Yosuke Yamashita's trio and Masayuki Takayanagi's groups played free music with a physical violence that had few American parallels, and the Japanese free scene developed largely without contact with the New York players it started from.

Fed into

  • UK Jazz Revival — parallel influence, 2018

What to hear

gateway

Terumasa Hino — Hi-Nology gateway

recorded/released 1969 · Columbia (Japan) YS-10022-J · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Hi-Nology”

Terumasa Hino (tp) · Hiromasa Suzuki (p) · Kunimitsu Inaba (b) · Motohiko Hino (d) · Takeru Muraoka (ts)

The record that announced Japanese modern jazz as its own thing rather than an import. Modal, hard-driving, and recorded with a punch that American records of the same year rarely had.

Listen for: The title track sits on a vamp and builds by sheer accumulation. Motohiko Hino's drumming is heavier and more rock-inflected than any Blue Note session of 1969.

core

Masabumi Kikuchi — Poo-Sun core

recorded/released 1970 · Philips (Japan) FX-8501 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Poo-Sun” · medium confidence

Masabumi Kikuchi (p, elp) · Terumasa Hino (tp) · Kohsuke Mine (as) · Yoshio Ikeda (b) · Motohiko Hino (d)

Kikuchi's early electric work, with the Hino brothers, sitting between modal jazz and something more abrasive. It is one of the most sought-after Japanese jazz records for good reason.

Listen for: The electric piano is used percussively and harshly rather than for warmth, which is the opposite of how American fusion was using it in 1970.
