Yosuke Yamashita Trio — Clay core

recorded/released 1974 · Enja enja 2050 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Clay”

Yosuke Yamashita (p) · Akira Sakata (as, cl) · Takeo Moriyama (d)

Recorded live at the Moers festival in Germany, this is the trio that made European audiences take Japanese free jazz seriously. The physical violence of the playing is the point.

Listen for: Yamashita attacks the piano with his forearms and elbows in places, but the harmonic thinking underneath never becomes random. Sakata's alto screams and stays in tune.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Isao Suzuki Trio/Quartet — Blow Up core

recorded/released 1973 · Three Blind Mice TBM-15 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Aqua Marine”

Isao Suzuki (b, cello) · Kunihiko Sugano (p) · Tetsujiro Obara (d) · Takashi Mizuhashi (b)

The Three Blind Mice house sound at its best: audiophile recording, a bassist leading, and material that swings hard without being retro. This is the label's calling card.

Listen for: The bass is recorded with a presence and depth that almost no American jazz record of 1973 attempted. TBM's engineering is a genuine part of the music's identity.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Masahiko Togashi — We Now Create: Music for Strings, Winds and Percussion core

recorded/released 1969 · Victor (Japan) SJV-259 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “We Now Create” · medium confidence

Masahiko Togashi (percussion) · Masayuki Takayanagi (g) · Mototeru Takagi (ts) · Motoharu Yoshizawa (b)

Recorded shortly before the injury that paralysed Togashi's legs and forced him to rebuild his entire approach to percussion. The use of silence and space here is unlike anything in American free jazz.

Listen for: Long stretches of near-nothing, then sudden density. The dynamic strategy is closer to the AACM than to New York energy playing, arrived at independently.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Ryo Fukui — Scenery core

recorded/released 1976 · Trio Records PA-7153 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Early Summer”

Ryo Fukui (p) · Satoshi Denpo (b) · Yoshinori Fukui (d)

A self-taught pianist from Sapporo making a straight-ahead trio record that went almost unnoticed for forty years and then found an enormous online audience. The playing is unshowy and completely assured.

Listen for: His version of 'Willow Weep for Me' is unhurried to the point of stillness, and the touch is the whole story — every note is placed rather than played.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Toshiko Akiyoshi–Lew Tabackin Big Band — Kogun deep

recorded/released 1974 · RCA (Japan) JRS-7211 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Kogun”

Toshiko Akiyoshi (p, comp, arr) · Lew Tabackin (ts, fl) · tsuzumi drums · noh vocal

Akiyoshi wrote Japanese traditional instruments and noh vocal technique into big band scoring, on a piece about a soldier still fighting the Second World War in 1972. Nobody else was writing like this.

Listen for: The tsuzumi hand drums and the noh-derived vocal cry are structural, not decorative — the whole arrangement is built to accommodate their sense of time.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Kohsuke Mine — Mine deep

recorded/released 1970 · Three Blind Mice TBM-1 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Morning Tide” · medium confidence

Kohsuke Mine (as, ss) · Masabumi Kikuchi (p) · Hideo Ichikawa (p) · Juni Booth (b) · Hiroshi Murakami (d)

Three Blind Mice's first release, and the record that set the label's standard. Mine plays with a hard, keening alto tone that owes something to Coltrane's soprano and nothing to Cannonball.

Listen for: The engineering — this was TBM-1, and Yoshihiko Kannari's recording of the drum kit already sounds better than most contemporary American jazz.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Koichi Matsukaze Trio — At the Room 427 deep

recorded/released 1975 · ALM Records AL-1 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Earth Mother” · medium confidence

Koichi Matsukaze (ts, ss) · Toshiyuki Daitoku (p) · Hiroshi Kato (b) · Ryojiro Furusawa (d)

Spiritual jazz from Japan, made outside the major-label system, with the modal intensity of the Impulse! catalogue and a distinctly Japanese sense of space.

Listen for: The long modal build follows the Coltrane quartet's shape, but the dynamics drop far lower than an American band of the period would have allowed.

Post-Bop 1963–present

The music of players who absorbed free jazz without abandoning form — ambiguous, floating, still structured.

New York, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Modern mainstream, The Blue Note new thing

By the mid-1960s a group of musicians had heard Ornette Coleman and Cecil Taylor and did not want to go all the way, but could not go back either. Post-bop is what they built. The forms are still there but stretched — 22 bars instead of 32, sections of irregular length, tunes that never quite resolve. The harmony floats: chords with ambiguous function, key centres that shift without announcing themselves, melodies that imply several possible tonalities. The rhythm section becomes genuinely interactive rather than supportive — the Miles Davis Second Great Quintet with Hancock, Carter and Williams played 'time, no changes', keeping a pulse while treating the harmony as negotiable, and Williams could imply three tempos at once. Wayne Shorter's compositions are the central body of writing: short, enigmatic heads with harmony that resists analysis. Andrew Hill, Bobby Hutcherson, Joe Henderson and Herbie Hancock make up most of the rest. It is still the default language of conservatory-trained modern jazz, which is why the term also gets used loosely for anything mainstream and post-1960.

What to listen for

  • Chords that never settle — you can't tell what key the tune is in
  • The drummer commenting and disrupting rather than keeping steady time
  • Forms that don't come around when you expect them to
  • Solos that go deliberately 'wrong' against the harmony, then snap back
  • Heads that are short, strange, and hard to hum

How it works

harmony
Functionally ambiguous. Slash chords, unresolved suspensions, modal interchange, and progressions that move by step or third rather than by fifth.
rhythm
Elastic. The pulse is present but the group can imply double time, half time, or a different meter simultaneously; the drummer is a co-improviser.
form
Irregular section lengths, forms that don't loop cleanly, and pieces where the solo section differs structurally from the head.
instrumentation
Quintet and sextet formats; vibraphone becomes prominent, and the piano sometimes drops out entirely.
improvisation
Freer relationship to the written harmony — playing 'outside' deliberately and resolving back, or ignoring the changes while keeping the form.
timbre
Cooler and more transparent than hard bop. Space and dynamics are compositional elements.

Key figures

  • Wayne Shorter (tenor and soprano saxophone, composition) — Wrote the style's core repertoire — harmonically slippery pieces that are still being played and still resist easy analysis.
  • Herbie Hancock (piano, composition) — Developed a comping approach that suggests rather than states harmony, and wrote the floating pieces that define the sound.
  • Tony Williams (drums) — Joined Miles at 17 and rebuilt the drummer's role as an equal improviser who can move the time without losing it.
  • Andrew Hill (piano, composition) — The most structurally radical of the Blue Note post-bop writers; his forms are asymmetric in ways that still sound unusual.
  • Joe Henderson (tenor saxophone) — Moved between inside and outside playing more fluently than almost anyone, on both hard bop and avant-garde dates.
  • Bobby Hutcherson (vibraphone) — Made the vibraphone a colour instrument for ambiguous harmony rather than a novelty.

Labels: Blue Note · Columbia · Milestone

Contested

The term is used two incompatible ways: narrowly for the mid-1960s Blue Note/Miles quintet music described here, and broadly for any acoustic jazz made after bebop that isn't free. Some writers file Kind of Blue and much of the 1970s mainstream here too. This guide uses the narrow definition and says so.

Came out of

  • Hard Bop — direct descendant, 1963, via instrumentation, improvisation, repertoire. Same Blue Note quintets, same players, often the same session week. What changes is that the forms stop being regular and the harmony stops resolving — Wayne Shorter and Herbie Hancock were writing hard bop dates in 1961 and post-bop ones by 1964.
  • Modal Jazz — direct descendant, 1964, via harmony, form, rhythm. Post-bop takes modal jazz's harmonic ambiguity and puts it back inside moving forms, so the chords change but never resolve. The Davis quintet's 'time, no changes' approach is modal freedom applied to a rhythm section rather than to a scale.
  • Free Jazz — parallel influence, 1964, via improvisation, rhythm, harmony. Post-bop is what musicians made after hearing Ornette Coleman and deciding to go partway. Tony Williams' elastic time, Andrew Hill's asymmetric forms and the habit of playing deliberately outside the harmony all depend on free jazz having made those moves thinkable first.

Fed into

  • Jazz Fusion — fusion of, 1969
  • Neo-Bop & the Young Lions — direct descendant, 1982
  • M-Base — direct descendant, 1984

What to hear

gateway

Miles Davis — Miles Smiles gateway

recorded 1966, released 1967 · Columbia CL 2601 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Footprints”

Miles Davis (tp) · Wayne Shorter (ts) · Herbie Hancock (p) · Ron Carter (b) · Tony Williams (d)

The Second Great Quintet's clearest statement of 'time, no changes' — the pulse holds while the harmony becomes negotiable. Hancock lays out for long stretches so nothing states the chord at all.

Listen for: On 'Footprints' the bass line implies one harmony and the piano implies another, while Williams shifts between a 6/4 and a 4/4 feel underneath. It never resolves and never falls apart.

Wayne Shorter — Speak No Evil core

recorded 1964, released 1966 · Blue Note BLP 4194 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Infant Eyes”

Wayne Shorter (ts) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Herbie Hancock (p) · Ron Carter (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

Shorter's compositions at their most perfectly balanced between hard bop's directness and post-bop's ambiguity. Every tune here is still played, and none of them analyse cleanly.

Listen for: 'Infant Eyes' has a thirteen-bar form — an odd number that you will not notice until you try to count it. The asymmetry is what makes the melody feel suspended.

Eric Dolphy — Out to Lunch! core

recorded/released 1964 · Blue Note BLP 4163 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Hat and Beard”

Eric Dolphy (as, bcl, fl) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Bobby Hutcherson (vib) · Richard Davis (b) · Tony Williams (d)

Dolphy's last studio album as leader, recorded four months before he died at thirty-six. There is no piano; the vibraphone leaves the harmony deliberately unstated and everything wobbles.

Listen for: Tony Williams plays around the beat rather than on it for the entire record, and Hutcherson answers with chords that never confirm a key. The instability is composed, not accidental.

Andrew Hill — Point of Departure core

recorded 1964, released 1965 · Blue Note BLP 4167 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Refuge”

Andrew Hill (p) · Eric Dolphy (as, bcl, fl) · Kenny Dorham (tp) · Joe Henderson (ts) · Richard Davis (b) · Tony Williams (d)

The most structurally radical of the Blue Note post-bop records. Hill's forms are asymmetric and his harmony moves by intervals nobody else was using, and the band handles it without strain.

Listen for: 'Refuge' has a form that will not resolve into a countable number of bars on first hearing. Hill wrote it that way and the band solos over it without visible difficulty.

recorded 1964, released 1966 · Blue Note BLP 4189 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Inner Urge”

Joe Henderson (ts) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Bob Cranshaw (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

Henderson with the Coltrane rhythm section, on a title track whose chords move by tritone and never sit still. He plays inside and outside the harmony continuously without ever sounding lost.

Listen for: The changes on 'Inner Urge' move in a pattern that gives no comfortable resting place, and Henderson keeps finding one anyway.

A quartet with no chordal instrument except the piano and a cornet instead of a trumpet, which makes the whole record lighter. 'Cantaloupe Island' has had a second life through sampling.

Listen for: 'The Egg' is fourteen minutes of near-free playing on a Blue Note record in 1964, which shows how close post-bop had come to the edge.

Hutcherson's first date as leader, with Andrew Hill's writing and Joe Chambers' — two of the most structurally adventurous composers Blue Note had. It goes further out than Out to Lunch in places.

Rivers had just been in Miles Davis's band and would soon be running the loft scene. This is him operating exactly at the boundary — the forms hold, barely, and the playing keeps pushing past them.

Listen for: Rivers described this as 'inside-outside' playing: the tune is there, the harmony is there, and the solo relates to both by choice rather than obligation.

Williams was nineteen and leading a session with two tenors and no chord instrument on half of it. The rhythmic freedom is further out than anything he did with Miles.

Spiritual Jazz 1964–present

Modal jazz aimed at transcendence — drones, chanting, harps, and long ecstatic builds that function as devotion.

New York / Detroit / Los Angeles, USA · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called Astral jazz, Cosmic jazz, Devotional jazz

A Love Supreme in 1965 established the template: a suite structured as a religious offering, with a chanted mantra, a bass ostinato functioning as a drone, and a build to ecstatic release. What follows takes those elements and adds the instruments and modes of Indian, African and Middle Eastern devotional music — tanpura drones, harp, sitar, hand percussion, bells, kalimba, voices chanting in unison. Alice Coltrane brought the harp and Hindu devotional practice; Pharoah Sanders combined near-nursery-rhyme melodies with tenor playing at the edge of what the horn can produce; Sun Ra was already there by a different route. Detroit's Tribe collective and Strata-East in New York provided the institutional base — artist-run labels making records that no major would touch, which is also why originals are so scarce and so expensive. The category is largely retrospective: it was assembled by DJs, reissue compilers and record collectors from the 1990s onward, and the musicians themselves generally just said they were playing jazz. That does not make it unreal, but it is worth knowing.

What to listen for

  • A drone or repeated bass figure that holds under the whole piece
  • Voices chanting a short phrase over and over
  • Harp, bells, or hand percussion where you would expect a piano
  • Saxophone playing that goes from gentle melody to screaming and back
  • Pieces built as slow builds over ten or fifteen minutes, structured like a rite

How it works

harmony
Modal and drone-based. Long pedal points, pentatonic and Eastern-derived modes, and simple two-chord frames that can be sustained indefinitely.
rhythm
Ostinato-driven, often in 6/8 or 3/4, with hand percussion layered over the kit. Tempo is usually stable but not the focus.
form
Suites and long-form pieces with distinct movements; a common shape is calm statement, escalation to collective intensity, and return.
instrumentation
Saxophones and piano plus harp, tanpura, sitar, oud, bells, shakers, kalimba, choral voices, and occasionally strings.
improvisation
Long, cumulative and intensity-driven rather than harmonically navigational; overblowing and multiphonics used as devotional expression.
timbre
Reverb-heavy, wide, with drones underneath. The harp and bells give the style its distinctive shimmer.

Key figures

  • John Coltrane (tenor and soprano saxophone) — A Love Supreme gave the idiom its structure, its intent and its permission.
  • Alice Coltrane (harp, piano, organ, composition) — Brought the harp, Hindu devotional practice and string orchestration into jazz and made a body of work nobody has matched.
  • Pharoah Sanders (tenor saxophone) — Paired simple, almost childlike melodies with extreme overblowing — the style's characteristic contrast in a single player.
  • Sun Ra (keyboards, composition) — Was doing cosmology, robes and collective ritual a decade before the term existed.
  • Don Cherry (trumpet, multi-instrumentalist) — Took the impulse global — Turkish, Indian, Malian and Scandinavian folk material inside an improvising frame.
  • Doug Carn (organ, composition) — With vocalist Jean Carn, made the most song-based version of the style for Black Jazz Records.

Labels: Impulse! · Strata-East · Black Jazz · Tribe · Flying Dutchman · Soul Jazz / Jazzman (reissues)

Contested

Whether it is a genre at all. The term was constructed retrospectively by compilers and collectors, largely from the 1990s onward, and the musicians described by it saw themselves as playing modal or free jazz with religious intent. Reading it as a genre risks flattening Alice Coltrane's Vedantic practice, Sanders' Islam and the Detroit collectives' Black nationalism into one aesthetic mood.

Came out of

  • Modal Jazz — direct descendant, 1965, via harmony, form, rhythm. A Love Supreme is a modal record with a devotional programme attached: a bass ostinato functioning as a drone, one mode held for minutes, and a chanted mantra where a head would be. Everything the idiom does harmonically is already in the modal toolkit.
  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1966, via improvisation, timbre, social-context. Overblowing, multiphonics and collective ecstatic climaxes come straight from the free jazz vocabulary — Pharoah Sanders was in Coltrane's late band and Sun Ra's before that. The difference is intent: the same sounds are framed as devotion rather than as rupture.

Fed into

  • LA Beat-Scene Jazz — revival of, 2015
  • UK Jazz Revival — revival of, 2016

What to hear

gateway

John Coltrane — A Love Supreme gateway

recorded 1964, released 1965 · Impulse! A-77 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Acknowledgement”

John Coltrane (ts, voc) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Jimmy Garrison (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

A four-part suite offered explicitly as a religious statement, recorded in one session in December 1964. It gave the whole idiom its structure, its intent and its permission.

Listen for: The four-note bass figure in 'Acknowledgement' is the whole record's seed. Coltrane plays it in every key before finally singing it as words — the melody becomes the mantra.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

core

Pharoah Sanders — Karma core

recorded/released 1969 · Impulse! AS-9181 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “The Creator Has a Master Plan”

Pharoah Sanders (ts) · Leon Thomas (voc) · Lonnie Liston Smith (p) · Reggie Workman (b) · Julius Watkins (frh) · Billy Hart (d)

'The Creator Has a Master Plan' runs thirty-two minutes and moves from a serene pentatonic melody to total collective violence and back. It is the idiom's defining shape in one track.

Listen for: Leon Thomas's yodelling — a technique he adapted from Central African pygmy singing — is doing something no jazz vocalist had attempted.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Alice Coltrane — Journey in Satchidananda core

recorded 1970, released 1971 · Impulse! AS-9203 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Journey in Satchidananda”

Alice Coltrane (harp, p) · Pharoah Sanders (ss) · Cecil McBee (b) · Rashied Ali (d) · Tulsi (tanpura) · Majid Shabazz (bells)

The harp, the tanpura drone and the Hindu devotional framework in one record, made by a musician who was practising what she was playing. Nothing else in jazz sounds like this.

Listen for: The tanpura drone under everything is not an effect — it is the harmonic foundation, and it means the music has no chord changes at all, only a fixed centre.

Pharoah Sanders — Thembi core

recorded 1970, released 1971 · Impulse! AS-9206 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Astral Traveling”

Pharoah Sanders (ts, ss, percussion) · Lonnie Liston Smith (p, elp) · Cecil McBee (b) · Roy Haynes (d) · Michael White (vln)

More varied than Karma and more concise, taking in Cecil McBee's solo bass piece, electric piano textures and African percussion. It is the idiom's most producer-minded record.

Listen for: 'Astral Traveling' opens with Lonnie Liston Smith's electric piano through echo, which is essentially the sound that nu jazz rediscovered twenty-five years later.

recorded/released 1971 · Black Jazz BJ/2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Infant Eyes”

Doug Carn (org, p) · Jean Carn (voc) · George Harper (ts) · Nate Morgan (p)

Jean Carn sings lyrics written to Wayne Shorter and McCoy Tyner instrumentals, over organ-led spiritual jazz on a Black-owned label. It is the most song-shaped version of the idiom.

Listen for: Putting words to 'Infant Eyes' should not work — Shorter's melody is not a song melody — and the strain of making it fit is part of what makes it moving.

European Free Improvisation 1965–present

Improvisation that cut its ties to jazz entirely — no tunes, no pulse, no blues, no American vocabulary.

London / Amsterdam / Wuppertal, UK / Netherlands / Germany · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called Free improv, Non-idiomatic improvisation, Echtzeitmusik (later)

American free jazz kept a great deal: a rhythmic pulse most of the time, a blues sensibility, the sound of the saxophone as a voice, and often a head to start from. European improvisers in the mid-1960s decided to cut all of it. Derek Bailey called the result 'non-idiomatic improvisation' and meant it as a technical description — music that deliberately avoids belonging to any style, including free jazz. In practice that means extended techniques, extreme dynamic range, silence used structurally, and a rejection of anything that would let a listener predict the next event. The scenes were national and distinct: London around the Spontaneous Music Ensemble, Bailey, Evan Parker and the Incus label, favouring quiet, fast, detailed interaction; Amsterdam around Misha Mengelberg, Han Bennink and the Instant Composers Pool, full of theatre, absurdity and sudden collapses into Monk tunes; Wuppertal and Berlin around Peter Brötzmann, at maximum volume and maximum density. Politics were explicit — several of these musicians framed the work as a rejection of American cultural dominance and of the market itself, and set up their own labels to prove it.

What to listen for

  • No steady beat anywhere, and no melody that repeats
  • Instruments making sounds you can't identify — scrapes, squeaks, air, feedback
  • Long silences used as part of the music, not as mistakes
  • Volume that jumps from almost nothing to overwhelming without warning
  • Players reacting to each other within a fraction of a second, like fast conversation

How it works

harmony
None in any functional sense. Pitch is used as texture and colour; consonance appears as one option among many, never as a resting place.
rhythm
No steady pulse. Rhythm exists as gesture, density and interruption. When a groove appears it is usually a joke or a quotation.
form
Real-time and emergent. Pieces begin and end when the players decide; recordings are often single unbroken improvisations.
instrumentation
Anything. Prepared and amplified instruments, homemade devices, objects, voice. Bailey used amplification and volume pedals as compositional tools.
improvisation
The entire content. Listening and response operate on a very fast timescale, closer to conversation than to soloing.
timbre
The primary parameter. Multiphonics, circular breathing, scraped strings, feedback, near-inaudible sounds next to sudden violence.

Key figures

  • Derek Bailey (guitar) — Theorised and practised non-idiomatic improvisation more rigorously than anyone; also wrote the movement's key book.
  • Evan Parker (soprano and tenor saxophone) — Developed a circular-breathing, multiphonic solo soprano language that has no antecedent in jazz saxophone.
  • Peter Brötzmann (reeds) — The German wing's ferocity in one player; Machine Gun in 1968 remains a hard line in the sand.
  • Han Bennink (drums, percussion) — Brought physical comedy and total unpredictability to the kit, and played anything in the room as an instrument.
  • Misha Mengelberg (piano, composition) — Ran the Dutch scene's mix of rigour and absurdism, and kept Monk and Herbie Nichols alive inside free improvisation.
  • John Stevens (drums) — The Spontaneous Music Ensemble's organiser, whose 'insect music' aesthetic prioritised listening over projection.

Labels: Incus · FMP · ICP · Emanem · Ogun

Contested

Whether it belongs in a jazz lineage at all. Bailey argued explicitly that free improvisation is not a jazz style, and many practitioners reject the connection. The counter-argument is genealogical rather than musical: nearly all the first generation came out of playing jazz, and the break only makes sense in relation to what it broke from.

Came out of

  • Free Jazz — reaction against, 1966, via timbre, rhythm, social-context, harmony. European improvisers took free jazz's central permission and then rejected the rest of it as American idiom: the blues inflection, the residual pulse, the saxophone-as-preaching-voice, the whole gestural vocabulary. Derek Bailey's term 'non-idiomatic' is aimed at free jazz as much as at bebop, and the politics of refusing American cultural dominance were stated openly.
  • AACM & Great Black Music — parallel influence, 1969, via instrumentation, form. The Art Ensemble and other AACM members moved to Paris in 1969 and recorded prolifically for BYG Actuel, putting little instruments, extreme dynamic contrast and composed framing in front of European improvisers who were already working in that direction. Traffic ran both ways from then on.
  • West Coast Jazz — parallel influence, 1963, via instrumentation, timbre, form. Interpretation: Jimmy Giuffre's 1961–62 trio with Paul Bley and Steve Swallow — no drums, near-silent dynamics, free but chamber-scaled — anticipates the quiet end of European improvisation by several years. The direct historical link is thin; the resemblance is not.

Fed into

  • Downtown & Punk Jazz — parallel influence, 1982

What to hear

gateway

Peter Brötzmann Octet — Machine Gun gateway

recorded/released 1968 · BRÖ (self-released), later FMP BRÖ 2 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Machine Gun”

Peter Brötzmann (ts) · Evan Parker (ts) · Willem Breuker (ts) · Fred Van Hove (p) · Peter Kowald (b) · Buschi Niebergall (b) · Han Bennink (d) · Sven-Åke Johansson (d)

The line in the sand. Eight players at maximum volume for the whole record, self-released because no label would touch it. It is not the gentlest introduction but it is the clearest statement of what European free music was refusing.

Listen for: There is a written theme — a repeated stabbing figure — that returns at intervals. Once you hear it, the record stops being noise and becomes a structure.

Note: Originally self-issued on Brötzmann's own BRÖ label in 1968 and later reissued by FMP.

Bailey alone, on the label he co-founded to avoid the record industry entirely. This is non-idiomatic improvisation as a fully worked-out practice rather than a manifesto.

Listen for: No phrase resembles a jazz line, a rock riff or a classical figure. He is actively avoiding every idiom the guitar has, and the discipline of that is audible.

Solo soprano saxophone, direct to disc, using circular breathing and multiphonics to produce continuous overlapping lines from one horn. There is no jazz antecedent for this technique.

Listen for: He never stops to breathe, and you will hear two or three simultaneous lines from a monophonic instrument. It is a physical achievement and a compositional one.

The quiet, fast, densely interactive end of British improvisation — Stevens called it 'insect music'. Dave Holland is on it, months before Miles Davis hired him.

Listen for: Everyone is playing quietly and reacting within a fraction of a second. Nobody projects and nobody leads. It is the opposite pole from Machine Gun in the same year.

The Dutch approach: absurdism, sudden collapses into Monk or Herbie Nichols tunes, and a refusal to take the avant-garde's seriousness at face value. It is also very funny.

Listen for: A free improvisation will resolve without warning into a fully swinging Monk tune, then fall apart again. The joke has a real point about how idioms work.

AACM & Great Black Music 1965–present

Free jazz that got organised — a musicians' collective that added composition, silence, theatre and a hundred small instruments.

Chicago, USA · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called Chicago avant-garde, Creative music, BAG (St. Louis)

The Association for the Advancement of Creative Musicians was founded in Chicago in 1965 by Muhal Richard Abrams and others as a self-governing organisation: members ran their own concerts, taught a free music school, and refused to depend on club owners or record companies. That structure produced a music distinct from New York free jazz. Where the New York scene favoured continuous high-energy blowing, the AACM built pieces with composed sections, extreme dynamic contrast, and long stretches of near-silence. The Art Ensemble of Chicago's slogan, 'Great Black Music, Ancient to the Future', announces the scope: field hollers, marches, bebop, free improvisation and African percussion inside a single performance, often in face paint and costume, with theatre as an explicit element. 'Little instruments' — bells, whistles, gongs, toys, bicycle horns, anything — became a signature, and members routinely play four or five instruments each. Anthony Braxton took the compositional side furthest, into diagrammatically titled works and multi-orchestra pieces. A parallel collective, BAG, formed in St. Louis in 1968, and both fed the New York loft scene in the 1970s.

What to listen for

  • Very long quiet passages where almost nothing happens, then sudden density
  • Bells, whistles, gongs and toys used as seriously as the horns
  • One player switching between four or five instruments within a piece
  • Abrupt swerves between styles — a march, then chaos, then a bebop line
  • Composed written sections that clearly aren't improvised

How it works

harmony
Ranges from fully atonal to plain diatonic depending on the section. Harmony is a compositional choice made per passage, not a governing system.
rhythm
Anything from strict written meter to free time to processional march. Silence is treated as a rhythmic value.
form
Composed multi-section works, graphic and verbal scores, and structures that allocate freedom differently in each part.
instrumentation
Multi-instrumentalism as standard, plus a percussion battery of small instruments. Contrabass clarinet, sopranino saxophone, bassoon and non-Western instruments all appear.
improvisation
Inside a designed frame. The question is usually 'what kind of freedom does this section have', not 'is this free'.
timbre
Extreme range and extreme contrast — a bicycle horn next to a bass saxophone next to four minutes of almost nothing.

Key figures

  • Muhal Richard Abrams (piano, composition) — Co-founded the AACM and ran the Experimental Band that trained most of its first generation.
  • Roscoe Mitchell (saxophones) — Sound in 1966 is the first AACM record and one of the first jazz records to use silence as a structural material.
  • Anthony Braxton (reeds, composition) — Built the largest and most systematic compositional apparatus in the music, while also playing standards and solo saxophone.
  • Art Ensemble of Chicago (collective) — The style's public face: theatre, costume, little instruments, and a repertoire spanning the whole history of Black music.
  • Henry Threadgill (saxophones, flute, composition) — Wrote for unusual fixed instrumentations — tuba and cello ensembles, two guitars, two tubas — with a compositional rigour that won a Pulitzer in 2016.
  • Julius Hemphill (alto saxophone, composition) — The central figure of St. Louis's BAG and founder of the World Saxophone Quartet.

Labels: Delmark · Nessa · BYG Actuel · ECM · Black Saint/Soul Note · Arista Freedom

Contested

Whether the AACM constitutes a genre or an institution whose members made very different music. Braxton, Threadgill and the Art Ensemble share an approach to structure and self-organisation more than a sound. 'Great Black Music' is the Art Ensemble's own framing and is broader than any style label.

Came out of

  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1965, via improvisation, harmony, form. The AACM's founding generation were playing free jazz in Chicago before the organisation existed. What the collective added was structure — composed sections, notated frames, and a refusal of the continuous-blowing model that New York had made standard.
  • Third Stream — parallel influence, 1967, via form, instrumentation. Anthony Braxton's compositional apparatus, Muhal Richard Abrams' orchestral writing and Roscoe Mitchell's use of silence all draw on twentieth-century concert music as directly as third stream did — but from inside a Black self-organised institution rather than through the conservatory. That difference is the point.
  • New Orleans Jazz — revival of, 1969, via repertoire, timbre, social-context. 'Great Black Music, Ancient to the Future' is a claim on the whole history, and the Art Ensemble made it audible by putting marches, field hollers and New Orleans polyphony inside free improvisation. The vocalised brass timbres and the collective front line are reached for deliberately, not inherited passively.

Fed into

  • European Free Improvisation — parallel influence, 1969
  • Loft Jazz — direct descendant, 1973
  • Contemporary Creative Jazz — direct descendant, 1998

What to hear

gateway

Art Ensemble of Chicago — Les Stances à Sophie gateway

recorded/released 1970 · Nessa N-4 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Theme de Yoyo”

Lester Bowie (tp) · Roscoe Mitchell (reeds) · Joseph Jarman (reeds) · Malachi Favors (b) · Don Moye (percussion) · Fontella Bass (voc, p)

A film soundtrack recorded in Paris, and the most immediately enjoyable thing the Art Ensemble made. 'Theme de Yoyo' has a bass line, a backbeat and Fontella Bass singing, and it is still recognisably them.

Listen for: 'Theme de Yoyo' is a funk record made by an avant-garde collective, and the free playing sits on top of the groove without disturbing it.

A major label paid for a large-ensemble avant-garde record, and Braxton delivered one that includes a genuine circus march alongside the abstraction. It won critics' polls and is still startling.

Ethio-Jazz 1966–present

Ethiopian five-note modes and jazz-funk instrumentation, producing a sound that seems to be in a minor key and a major key at once.

Addis Ababa, Ethiopia · Global Currents · also called Ethiopian jazz, Ethio-groove

Ethiopian traditional music uses a system of pentatonic modes called qignit, of which four are common — tizita, bati, ambassel and anchihoye. They are five-note scales with wide intervals, and to ears trained on Western music they sound simultaneously mournful and unresolved. Mulatu Astatke, who studied in London and at Berklee in Boston and had absorbed Latin jazz along the way, put those modes over jazz and funk instrumentation — vibraphone, electric bass, organ, horn sections, congas — and called the result Ethio-jazz. The rhythmic feel is its own thing: a loping, slightly dragging groove often in 6/8 or a swung 4, quite unlike either American funk or Cuban clave. The music flourished under Haile Selassie's later years, then was largely shut down by the Derg regime's curfews and nationalisation of the record industry after 1974. It was rediscovered internationally through the Éthiopiques reissue series, launched in 1997 by Francis Falceto on Buda Musique, and Jim Jarmusch's use of Astatke's music in Broken Flowers in 2005.

What to listen for

  • A five-note scale with big gaps in it that sounds neither major nor minor
  • Vibraphone playing lead lines over an electric bass groove
  • A groove that drags slightly behind the beat rather than pushing
  • Horn riffs that repeat without developing, hypnotically
  • Organ tones that sound closer to 1960s garage rock than to jazz

How it works

harmony
Built on pentatonic qignit modes rather than functional harmony. Chords are often ambiguous or drone-like, supporting the mode rather than moving through changes.
rhythm
Loping and slightly behind, frequently in compound meters. Neither swing nor clave — the feel derives from Ethiopian dance rhythms like the chik chika.
form
Short repeating structures with extended vamps for solos; horn riffs stated and restated rather than developed.
instrumentation
Vibraphone (Astatke's signature), electric organ, electric bass, horn section, congas and kit; sometimes the traditional krar and masenqo.
improvisation
Modal soloing within the qignit, often quite short. The groove and the mode, rather than solo display, carry the music.
timbre
Warm, slightly murky analogue recording; vibraphone and Farfisa-style organ against a reedy horn section.

Key figures

  • Mulatu Astatke (vibraphone, percussion, arranging) — Named and defined the style; the only person who deliberately set out to fuse Ethiopian modes with jazz as a project.
  • Girma Bèyènè (piano, arranging, voice) — Arranger on much of the Addis golden-age catalogue and a distinctive singer in his own right.
  • Mahmoud Ahmed (voice) — The great vocalist of the era; his Erè Mèla Mèla was the record that broke Ethiopian music in Europe in the mid-1980s.
  • Getatchew Mekurya (tenor saxophone) — Adapted the shellela, a sung Ethiopian war chant, to saxophone, arriving at something that sounds like free jazz by a completely different route.
  • Hailu Mergia (keyboards, accordion) — Led the Walias Band, then spent decades driving a taxi in Washington DC before his rediscovery.

Labels: Amha Records · Kaifa Records · Philips-Ethiopia · Buda Musique (Éthiopiques) · Awesome Tapes From Africa

Contested

Whether Ethio-jazz names a genre or one man's project. Astatke coined the term for his own music, and much of what gets filed under it — Mahmoud Ahmed's vocal records, the Walias Band's instrumentals — is Ethiopian popular music with horns rather than a jazz-derived fusion. The Éthiopiques series bundled all of it together for a European audience.

Came out of

  • Afro-Cuban & Latin Jazz — direct descendant, 1966, via rhythm, instrumentation. Mulatu Astatke's training in London and Boston was in Latin jazz, and his vibraphone-and-congas instrumentation comes straight from that world. He described building Ethio-jazz by fitting Ethiopian modes into arrangements he already knew how to write.
  • Soul Jazz & Organ Trio — direct descendant, 1969, via instrumentation, rhythm, form. Electric organ, electric bass, a horn section playing short riffs, and a groove held without harmonic movement — the Addis Ababa records of the golden age use the soul jazz and early jazz-funk template, with pentatonic qignit modes replacing blues scales.

Fed into

  • UK Jazz Revival — parallel influence, 2016

What to hear

gateway

Mulatu Astatke — Éthiopiques 4: Ethio Jazz & Musique Instrumentale 1969–1974 gateway

recorded 1969, released 1998 · Buda Musique 82964-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Yèkèrmo Sèw”

Mulatu Astatke (vib, percussion, arr)

The compilation that introduced Ethio-jazz to the world outside Ethiopia, collecting Astatke's Amha and Philips sides from the golden age. Jim Jarmusch used several of these in Broken Flowers.

Listen for: 'Yèkèrmo Sèw' — the five-note mode has wide gaps in it, so the melody sounds neither major nor minor, and the groove drags slightly behind the beat throughout.

core

Mulatu Astatke and His Ethiopian Quintet — Afro-Latin Soul core

recorded/released 1966 · Worthy Records W-1014 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Mascaram Setaba” · medium confidence

Mulatu Astatke (vib, congas) · Rudy Houston (voc) · Felix Torres (b)

Recorded in New York before Astatke returned to Ethiopia, and it is Latin jazz with Ethiopian melodic material starting to appear. The genre is visibly under construction.

Listen for: The rhythm section is straightforwardly Latin. The Ethiopian element is in the melodies and in the vibraphone lines, which is the exact seam Astatke was working on.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Mahmoud Ahmed — Ere Mela Mela core

recorded/released 1975 · Kaifa Records KF 20 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Ere Mela Mela”

Mahmoud Ahmed (voc) · Ibex Band

The record that broke Ethiopian music in Europe when Crammed Discs reissued it in 1986 — a decade before the Éthiopiques series. Ahmed's singing is the vocal counterpart to Astatke's instrumental project.

Listen for: The vocal ornamentation moves in microtonal steps that the Western scale does not contain, over a band playing a fairly straight organ-and-horns groove.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Getatchew Mekurya — Éthiopiques 14: Negus of Ethiopian Sax core

recorded 1972, released 2003 · Buda Musique 82250-2 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Shellela”

Getatchew Mekurya (ts)

Mekurya adapted the shellela — a sung Ethiopian war chant, delivered solo and unaccompanied — to the tenor saxophone, and arrived at something that sounds like Albert Ayler by an entirely independent route.

Listen for: The long unaccompanied declamatory passages have no jazz precedent behind them at all. He had not heard the free jazz his playing resembles.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Hailu Mergia & The Walias Band — Tche Belew core

recorded/released 1977 · Kaifa Records KF 41 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Musicawi Silt”

Hailu Mergia (org, keys) · Moges Habte (ts) · Melake Gebre (b)

An all-instrumental Ethiopian funk record from the Derg years, with Mergia's organ carrying the modes. He later spent decades driving a taxi in Washington DC before being rediscovered.

Listen for: 'Musicawi Silt' is built on a two-bar riff that never changes for the whole track. The interest is in the groove's slight drag and the organ's ornamentation.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Girma Bèyènè & Akalé Wubé — Ethiopiques 30: Mistakes on Purpose core

recorded 2016, released 2017 · Buda Musique 860269 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Ené Négn Bay Manesh”

Girma Bèyènè (voc, p) · Akalé Wubé (band)

The arranger behind much of the golden-age catalogue, returning to record after roughly thirty years of silence, backed by a young French Ethio-jazz band. The transmission across generations is the story.

Listen for: The band plays the idiom with the accuracy of students, and Bèyènè's phrasing is looser and stranger than theirs. The gap is instructive.

deep

Various Artists — Éthiopiques 13: Ethiopian Groove — The Golden Seventies deep

recorded 1969, released 2003 · Buda Musique 82249-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Tashamanaletch” · medium confidence

Alèmayèhu Eshèté (voc) · Muluqèn Mèllèssè (voc) · various bands

The vocal and dance side of the same scene, showing that Ethio-jazz sat inside a broader Ethiopian popular music rather than standing apart from it.

Listen for: Alèmayèhu Eshèté was called the Ethiopian Elvis and the influence is audible, sitting on top of entirely non-Western melodic material.

Mulatu Astatke & The Heliocentrics — Inspiration Information 3 deep

recorded 2008, released 2009 · Strut STRUT045CD · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Cha Cha”

Mulatu Astatke (vib, keys) · Malcolm Catto (d) · Jake Ferguson (b)

Astatke at sixty-five with a London psych-jazz band, and the collaboration is genuinely two-way rather than a legend being backed. It is also the main channel by which Ethio-jazz entered the current UK scene.

Listen for: The Heliocentrics play with a deliberately murky, dubbed-out production that suits the modes better than the clean 1970s Addis recordings did.

Alèmayèhu Eshèté — Éthiopiques 9: Alèmayèhu Eshèté 1969–1974 deep

recorded 1969, released 2000 · Buda Musique 82966-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Tèlantèna Zaré” · medium confidence

Alèmayèhu Eshèté (voc) · Walias Band · Shebele's Band

The golden age's most charismatic singer, with the Walias Band behind him. It is the pop end of the scene and shows how thin the line between Ethio-jazz and Ethiopian pop actually was.

Listen for: The band's horn riffs and organ are lifted from American soul records, while the vocal line stays entirely inside Ethiopian modal practice.

Jazz Fusion 1968–present

Jazz plugged into amplifiers and rebuilt around rock volume and funk rhythm, with the improvising kept intact.

New York, USA · Electric & Crossover · also called Jazz-rock, Electric jazz

Two things happen at once around 1969. Jazz musicians start using electric instruments — Fender Rhodes, electric bass, distorted guitar, ring modulators — and they stop swinging, replacing the triplet lilt with straight-eighth rock and funk feels. Miles Davis is the hinge: In a Silent Way in 1969 is quiet and static, Bitches Brew in 1970 is dense and assembled in the edit room by Teo Macero, and by 1972 On the Corner is an unbroken funk machine. Nearly everyone in those bands then formed their own: Weather Report, Mahavishnu Orchestra, Return to Forever, Headhunters, Lifetime. The results were technically formidable and often extremely popular, which is why the backlash was severe — critics called it a commercial capitulation and the neo-bop movement in the 1980s defined itself against it. The music itself varies enormously: Mahavishnu is aggressive odd-meter virtuosity, Weather Report is collective and textural, Headhunters is groove-first. What they share is electricity, straight eighths, and the assumption that a rock audience's volume and energy could be jazz's too.

What to listen for

  • Electric piano (Fender Rhodes) instead of acoustic — bell-like and slightly bent
  • Straight, even eighth notes with a rock or funk feel, no swing
  • Distorted electric guitar taking solos where a saxophone would have
  • Bars that don't come out in fours — count and you'll lose your place
  • Long stretches on one chord with the intensity coming from rhythm and volume

How it works

harmony
Modal vamps and pedal points dominate, with slash chords, suspended voicings, and stretches where harmony is essentially static.
rhythm
Straight eighths from rock and funk, plus odd meters — 7/8, 9/8, 5/4 — as a virtuosic marker in the Mahavishnu and Return to Forever wing.
form
Long, sectional, often built from vamps rather than song forms. Studio editing becomes a compositional tool.
instrumentation
Fender Rhodes and synthesizers, electric bass, distorted electric guitar, expanded percussion. Horns often processed with wah or echo.
improvisation
Modal and pentatonic soloing at rock volume, with electric guitar and synthesizer as lead voices alongside horns.
timbre
Effects as composition — wah, ring modulation, fuzz, echo, and the Rhodes' bell-like decay. Studio production is part of the music.

Key figures

  • Miles Davis (trumpet, bandleading) — Made the transition audible across four years and staffed most of the bands that followed.
  • Joe Zawinul (keyboards, composition) — Weather Report's principal writer and an early master of the synthesizer as an orchestral rather than a novelty instrument.
  • Wayne Shorter (soprano and tenor saxophone) — Weather Report's other half; brought post-bop compositional ambiguity into an electric setting.
  • John McLaughlin (guitar) — Mahavishnu Orchestra defined the high-velocity, odd-meter, Indian-influenced wing of fusion.
  • Herbie Hancock (keyboards, composition) — Head Hunters in 1973 took fusion into a funk-first direction and became one of the best-selling jazz records ever made.
  • Jaco Pastorius (electric bass) — Redefined the fretless electric bass as a lead melodic instrument with a singing, harmonics-rich tone.
  • Tony Williams (drums) — Lifetime, formed in 1969, was arguably louder and more rock-committed than anything Miles did.

Labels: Columbia · CTI · Warner Bros. · Nemperor · Arista

Contested

Whether fusion is a genre or a period. 'Jazz-rock' and 'fusion' are used interchangeably by some and distinguished by others (jazz-rock for the 1969–74 aggressive wing, fusion for what came after). The critical rehabilitation of the era is recent and still contested; a lot of writers still treat 1975–85 fusion as a lost decade.

Came out of

  • Modal Jazz — fusion of, 1969, via harmony, form, technology. Fusion's harmony is modal jazz's — one chord for a long time — transferred to a Fender Rhodes and set over a rock backbeat. The two parents not on this map are rock and funk, which supply the volume, the straight eighths and the electric instruments.
  • Post-Bop — fusion of, 1969, via improvisation, instrumentation, rhythm. The personnel are identical: Hancock, Shorter, Williams, Corea, Zawinul and Holland all came out of the post-bop world and went electric more or less at once. The interactive rhythm-section thinking of the Davis quintet survives the change of instruments.
  • Soul Jazz & Organ Trio — parallel influence, 1969, via technology, instrumentation. Soul jazz had been amplifying jazz for over a decade — the Hammond organ, the electric guitar, the R&B backbeat — and had absorbed the critical contempt for it first. Fusion's electric turn is less unprecedented than the 1970s controversy suggested.
  • Bossa Nova & Brazilian Jazz — parallel influence, 1972, via rhythm, timbre, instrumentation. Airto Moreira played on Bitches Brew and then with Weather Report and Return to Forever, and Flora Purim sang on the latter. Brazilian percussion and straight-eighth feels are woven into fusion from the beginning rather than added later.

Fed into

  • Jazz-Funk — direct descendant, 1972
  • Smooth Jazz — direct descendant, 1979
  • Neo-Bop & the Young Lions — reaction against, 1981
  • LA Beat-Scene Jazz — direct descendant, 2013

What to hear

gateway

Miles Davis — Bitches Brew gateway

recorded 1969, released 1970 · Columbia GP 26 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Miles Runs the Voodoo Down”

Miles Davis (tp) · Wayne Shorter (ss) · Bennie Maupin (bcl) · Chick Corea (elp) · Joe Zawinul (elp) · John McLaughlin (g) · Dave Holland (b) · Harvey Brooks (elb) · Jack DeJohnette (d) · Lenny White (d) · Airto Moreira (percussion)

The record that made fusion a commercial and critical event. Thirteen musicians, three keyboards, two drummers, and Teo Macero assembling the released tracks from tape splices after the fact.

Listen for: The harmony barely moves for twenty minutes at a time. What changes is density and texture — musicians entering and dropping out — and the editing that Macero imposed afterwards.

core

Miles Davis — In a Silent Way core

recorded/released 1969 · Columbia CS 9875 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “In a Silent Way / It's About That Time”

Miles Davis (tp) · Wayne Shorter (ss) · John McLaughlin (g) · Herbie Hancock (elp) · Chick Corea (elp) · Joe Zawinul (org) · Dave Holland (b) · Tony Williams (d)

Quieter and stranger than Bitches Brew, and the first place the electric approach fully works. Two side-long pieces built by Macero from short takes, with Tony Williams playing only the hi-hat for long stretches.

Listen for: The opening and closing sections of the title suite are the same take, used twice. Macero built the record's shape in the edit, and Davis approved it.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Weather Report — Heavy Weather core

recorded 1976, released 1977 · Columbia PC 34418 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Birdland”

Joe Zawinul (keys) · Wayne Shorter (ss, ts) · Jaco Pastorius (elb) · Alex Acuña (d) · Manolo Badrena (percussion)

Fusion at its most melodically generous, with Jaco Pastorius in the band and 'Birdland' as an actual hit. The group had a rule that everyone soloed all the time and nobody soloed, and you can hear it work.

Listen for: Pastorius plays fretless electric bass with harmonics and a singing tone that had no precedent. On 'Teen Town' he plays bass and drums both.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Mahavishnu Orchestra — The Inner Mounting Flame core

recorded/released 1971 · Columbia KC 31067 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Meeting of the Spirits”

John McLaughlin (g) · Jerry Goodman (vln) · Jan Hammer (keys) · Rick Laird (b) · Billy Cobham (d)

The loudest, fastest and most technically aggressive fusion band, playing odd meters at rock volume with a distorted guitar and an amplified violin. It genuinely frightened people in 1971.

Listen for: 'Dance of Maya' moves between 20/8 and a shuffle blues. Count the opening and you will fail; feel it and it works.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Return to Forever — Romantic Warrior core

recorded/released 1976 · Columbia PC 34076 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Medieval Overture”

Chick Corea (keys) · Al Di Meola (g) · Stanley Clarke (elb) · Lenny White (d)

Fusion's most unembarrassed virtuoso record, with a medieval concept and playing of frightening precision. It is the high-water mark of the technique-forward wing and the point where the backlash became inevitable.

Listen for: Everything is executed in unison at speed — guitar, bass and keyboard playing the same intricate line. The ensemble precision is the achievement, not the solos.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

The Tony Williams Lifetime — Emergency! core

recorded/released 1969 · Polydor 25-3001 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Emergency”

Tony Williams (d, voc) · John McLaughlin (g) · Larry Young (org)

Recorded months before Bitches Brew and considerably more violent. Organ, distorted guitar and Williams playing at full volume, badly recorded, and all the better for it.

Listen for: The recording is genuinely poor — distorted and muddy — and the band plays through it. This is jazz musicians choosing rock's volume without rock's production.

deep

Miles Davis — On the Corner deep

recorded/released 1972 · Columbia KC 31906 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “On the Corner”

Miles Davis (tp) · Michael Henderson (elb) · John McLaughlin (g) · Herbie Hancock (keys) · Bennie Maupin (bcl) · Badal Roy (tabla) · Jack DeJohnette (d)

Reviled on release and now treated as prophetic. It is an unbroken funk machine with tabla and sitar, aimed explicitly at a young Black audience that Davis felt he had lost.

Listen for: The bass line barely changes for the whole record and the trumpet is buried in the mix. Davis is making a groove record, and the soloing is a texture inside it.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jaco Pastorius — Jaco Pastorius deep

recorded 1975, released 1976 · Epic PE 33949 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Donna Lee”

Jaco Pastorius (elb) · Herbie Hancock (keys) · Wayne Shorter (ss) · Michael Brecker (ts) · Don Alias (percussion) · Sam & Dave (voc)

A debut that reintroduced the electric bass as a lead instrument, covering Charlie Parker, playing solo bass with harmonics, and putting a soul vocal duo on a jazz record.

Listen for: 'Portrait of Tracy' is solo fretless bass played almost entirely in artificial harmonics. Nobody had made the instrument sound like that.

Soft Machine — Third deep

recorded/released 1970 · CBS 66246 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Facelift”

Mike Ratledge (org, p) · Elton Dean (as, saxello) · Hugh Hopper (elb) · Robert Wyatt (d, voc)

The British route into fusion, coming from psychedelic rock rather than from jazz. Four side-long pieces, tape loops, fuzz organ, and a rhythmic approach that owes more to minimalism than to funk.

Listen for: 'Out-Bloody-Rageous' opens with tape loops that predate most electronic jazz by two decades. The Canterbury scene arrived at fusion independently.

Jazz-Funk 1969–present

The groove is the composition — jazz players building on a bass line and a drum break instead of a chord sequence.

New York / Los Angeles / Detroit, USA · Electric & Crossover · also called Funky jazz, Crossover jazz, Rare groove

Fusion took jazz toward rock; jazz-funk took it toward James Brown and Sly Stone. The difference is priority: in fusion the solo is usually the point and the groove supports it, while in jazz-funk the groove is the piece and the solo is one layer in it. The rhythm is on the one, with a syncopated electric bass line as the primary compositional material, clavinet or Rhodes stabs, a horn section playing tight riffs, and drum parts built for dancing. Herbie Hancock's Head Hunters, Donald Byrd's work with the Mizell brothers, Roy Ayers' Ubiquity, Lonnie Liston Smith and the whole CTI catalogue mark the range from serious to slick. It was commercially the most successful jazz of the 1970s and, for that reason, the most despised by critics of the period. Its afterlife is disproportionate: hip-hop producers mined these records so thoroughly that a huge share of the sampled jazz in rap comes from a narrow band of 1969–79 jazz-funk, which then fed acid jazz, jazz rap and the whole Brainfeeder orbit.

What to listen for

  • A bass line you'd recognise on its own — it is the tune
  • Clavinet or wah guitar chopping out sixteenth notes
  • The whole band hitting hard on beat one and leaving space after it
  • Horn riffs that repeat as a hook rather than developing
  • Drum breaks where everything drops out except the kit — later sampled to death

How it works

harmony
Vamp-based — usually one or two chords, often minor seventh or dominant with a raised ninth, sustained for the whole track.
rhythm
Sixteenth-note funk with the accent on the one. Syncopated bass lines and drum patterns designed to be looped, whether or not anyone intended that.
form
Groove, horn riff, solos over the groove, breakdown, out. Song forms in the vocal material; open vamps otherwise.
instrumentation
Electric bass, clavinet, Rhodes, ARP and Moog synths, wah guitar, horn section, congas alongside the kit.
improvisation
Present but rationed and blues-pentatonic in orientation. Nobody is running changes.
timbre
Warm analogue production, heavy compression on the drums, synth bass and clavinet as textural signatures.

Key figures

  • Herbie Hancock (keyboards, composition) — Head Hunters put a jazz musician's harmonic mind inside a Sly Stone rhythm section and sold enormously.
  • Donald Byrd (trumpet) — His Mizell brothers records are the most fully realised jazz-funk productions and the most sampled.
  • Roy Ayers (vibraphone, vocals) — Made vibraphone-led jazz-funk with a songwriting sensibility; sampled more than almost anyone in the idiom.
  • Lonnie Liston Smith (keyboards) — Connected the spiritual jazz wing to the dancefloor with a floating, echo-heavy sound.
  • Eddie Harris (tenor saxophone) — Early adopter of the electrified saxophone and a genuine funk writer, not a jazz musician visiting.
  • Grover Washington Jr. (saxophones) — The commercial bridge from jazz-funk into smooth jazz, and a much better player than that lineage suggests.

Labels: CTI/Kudu · Blue Note (1970s) · Polydor · Prestige · Fantasy

Contested

The boundary with fusion, which is a matter of emphasis rather than kind. Also whether the 1990s 'rare groove' reissue framing — which selected records for danceability and sample potential — has distorted which artists are remembered.

Came out of

  • Soul Jazz & Organ Trio — direct descendant, 1969, via rhythm, instrumentation, form. The organ trio's groove-first premise scaled up: horn sections, clavinet, electric bass, and a sixteenth-note funk feel replacing the shuffle. The soloist's role shrinks further, and the bass line becomes the composition.
  • Jazz Fusion — direct descendant, 1972, via technology, instrumentation, harmony. Same electric instruments, different priority. Where fusion uses the groove to support virtuoso soloing, jazz-funk uses the soloing to decorate the groove — Herbie Hancock made both records, two years apart, with overlapping personnel.

Fed into

  • Free Funk & Harmolodics — fusion of, 1975
  • Smooth Jazz — direct descendant, 1975
  • Jazz Rap — direct descendant, 1991
  • Acid Jazz — revival of, 1987
  • M-Base — direct descendant, 1985

What to hear

gateway

Herbie Hancock — Head Hunters gateway

recorded/released 1973 · Columbia KC 32731 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Chameleon”

Herbie Hancock (keys, clavinet, synths) · Bennie Maupin (reeds) · Paul Jackson (elb) · Harvey Mason (d) · Bill Summers (percussion)

Hancock deliberately built a band around a groove rather than around soloing, and it became one of the best-selling jazz albums ever made. Everything in jazz-funk after this is measured against it.

Listen for: 'Chameleon' is one bass line for fifteen minutes. The clavinet and the synth bass do all the harmonic work and nothing modulates.

core

Donald Byrd — Black Byrd core

recorded 1972, released 1973 · Blue Note BN.LA047-F · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Flight Time”

Donald Byrd (tp) · Larry Mizell (prod, arr) · Fonce Mizell (prod) · Joe Sample (keys) · Chuck Rainey (elb) · Harvey Mason (d)

Blue Note's best-selling album of all time at the point of release, produced by the Mizell brothers with a hard bop trumpeter in front. It was treated as a betrayal at the time and has been mined by producers ever since.

Listen for: Byrd's trumpet is one colour among many; the arrangement, the vocal harmonies and the bass line are the composition. That inversion is what upset the critics.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Roy Ayers Ubiquity — Everybody Loves the Sunshine core

recorded/released 1976 · Polydor PD-1-6070 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Everybody Loves the Sunshine”

Roy Ayers (vib, voc, keys) · William Allen (elb) · Justo Almario (ts, fl) · Philip Woo (keys)

The title track is one of the most sampled recordings in existence, and the album around it is a study in doing almost nothing with total conviction.

Listen for: The title track has essentially two chords and a mist of Rhodes and vibraphone. The mood is the content, and it is why hip-hop producers kept returning to it.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Les McCann & Eddie Harris — Swiss Movement core

recorded/released 1969 · Atlantic SD 1537 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Compared to What”

Les McCann (p, voc) · Eddie Harris (ts) · Benny Bailey (tp) · Leroy Vinnegar (b) · Donald Dean (d)

Recorded at Montreux in 1969 by musicians who had barely rehearsed, and 'Compared to What' became a protest anthem. It is jazz-funk before the studio production arrived.

Listen for: Eddie Harris is playing an electrified tenor through a Varitone attachment. The horn's processed tone is a deliberate technological choice, not a studio effect.

Lonnie Liston Smith and the Cosmic Echoes — Expansions core

recorded 1974, released 1975 · Flying Dutchman BDL1-0934 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Expansions”

Lonnie Liston Smith (keys) · Donald Smith (voc, fl) · Cecil McBee (b) · David Hubbard (ts) · Art Gore (d)

The bridge between spiritual jazz and the dancefloor: modal, echoing, mystical, and built on a bass line that London DJs were still playing fifteen years later.

Listen for: The title track's bass figure and the electric piano wash. This is the specific sound that acid jazz set out to recreate.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jimmy Smith — Root Down core

recorded/released 1972 · Verve V6-8806 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Root Down (And Get It)”

Jimmy Smith (org) · Arthur Adams (g) · Wilton Felder (elb) · Paul Humphrey (d) · Buck Clarke (congas)

The organ pioneer, live in Los Angeles in 1972, playing over a hard funk rhythm section rather than a swing one. The Beastie Boys built a track around it two decades later.

Listen for: Smith's vocabulary has not changed since 1958; the rhythm section underneath it has changed completely. That is the whole soul-jazz-to-jazz-funk transition in one record.

deep

Bob James — Two deep

recorded/released 1975 · CTI CTI 6057 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Take Me to the Mardi Gras”

Bob James (keys, arr) · Grover Washington Jr. (ss) · Eric Gale (g) · Gary King (elb) · Idris Muhammad (d)

Historically important for a reason James did not intend: 'Take Me to the Mardi Gras' contains a bell-and-drum break that became one of hip-hop's foundational samples.

Listen for: The break at the start of 'Take Me to the Mardi Gras' — sleigh bells and drums — turns up in dozens of rap records. Here it is, in context, doing nothing much.

Gary Bartz NTU Troop — Harlem Bush Music – Taifa deep

recorded 1970, released 1971 · Milestone MSP 9032 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Uhuru Sasa”

Gary Bartz (as, ss, voc) · Andy Bey (voc, p) · Ron Carter (b) · Harold White (d) · Juni Booth (b)

Bartz had just left Miles Davis and made a record with chanted Swahili, political text and a groove. It sits precisely between spiritual jazz's intent and jazz-funk's body.

Listen for: Andy Bey's voice is used as an instrument in unison with the alto, and the material is explicitly about Black self-determination rather than about transcendence.

Idris Muhammad — Power of Soul deep

recorded/released 1974 · Kudu KU-17 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Piece of Mind”

Idris Muhammad (d) · Grover Washington Jr. (ts, ss) · Bob James (keys, arr) · Joe Beck (g) · Gary King (elb)

A New Orleans drummer leading a CTI/Kudu session, which means the funk has a second-line looseness that the New York studio records lack.

Listen for: Muhammad's snare placement is slightly behind the grid throughout — the New Orleans feel inside a mid-1970s studio production.

ECM & Nordic Chamber Jazz 1969–present

Jazz built around space, silence and sustained sound — recorded so precisely the room becomes an instrument.

Munich / Oslo, Germany / Norway · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called European chamber jazz, Nordic tone, The ECM sound

Manfred Eicher founded ECM in Munich in 1969 and produced almost everything on it himself, with engineer Jan Erik Kongshaug at Talent Studio in Oslo for a large part of the catalogue. The recording aesthetic — long natural reverb, extreme dynamic range, every instrument separated and audible, piano and cymbals given room to decay — is so consistent that it constitutes a compositional element, and the phrase 'the most beautiful sound next to silence' came from a review and stuck as the label's own slogan. Musically the identifying features are slow harmonic rhythm, folk-derived melody, restraint, and a relationship to European classical music that is unembarrassed rather than programmatic. Jan Garbarek's saxophone tone — cold, pure, enormous — became the sound people mean by 'Nordic'. Keith Jarrett's solo concerts, especially Köln in 1975, made the label commercially significant. It is contested whether this is a genre or a producer's taste, and the argument is real: ECM also released Art Ensemble of Chicago, Steve Reich and Meredith Monk. But the Nordic chamber wing is coherent enough to name.

What to listen for

  • Enormous natural-sounding reverb — the room is clearly part of the record
  • Very quiet passages that demand you turn it up, then sudden fullness
  • Melodies that sound like folk songs rather than jazz heads
  • Drums played with mallets and cymbals left to ring out
  • Almost no swing feel, and often no steady pulse at all

How it works

harmony
Slow-moving, modal, often built on suspended and open voicings that avoid clear resolution. Folk modes from Scandinavian and Eastern European sources.
rhythm
Rubato and free-time passages alongside straight eighths; swing appears rarely. Drummers play colour and texture as much as pulse.
form
Long, patient, often through-composed. Pieces develop by accumulation and change of density rather than by chorus structure.
instrumentation
Piano, saxophone, double bass, drums, but also guitar, oud, tabla, string quartet and voice; unusual pairings are routine.
improvisation
Restrained and thematic. Melodic development matters more than harmonic navigation or rhythmic display.
timbre
The defining parameter. Long natural reverb, wide dynamics, the decay of notes treated as content.

Key figures

  • Manfred Eicher (production) — Produced the overwhelming majority of the catalogue; the label's aesthetic is a single person's editorial ear applied for fifty years.
  • Jan Garbarek (saxophones) — His tone and his use of Norwegian folk material define what 'Nordic jazz' means to most listeners.
  • Keith Jarrett (piano) — The solo concerts made improvised piano a concert-hall event and gave ECM a commercial base.
  • Eberhard Weber (electric upright bass, composition) — Built an entirely personal instrument and a body of chamber-jazz writing around it.
  • Terje Rypdal (guitar) — Brought sustained, distorted, orchestral electric guitar into the label's otherwise acoustic world.
  • Tomasz Stańko (trumpet) — The Polish wing — darker, more Miles-derived, and connected to a Central European free-jazz history the Nordic players did not share.

Labels: ECM · Jazzland (Norway) · Winter & Winter

Contested

Whether 'ECM jazz' names a genre or a label's house taste. The catalogue includes free jazz, contemporary classical, Renaissance music and Art Ensemble of Chicago records, which no stylistic definition covers. This guide names the Nordic chamber wing specifically and treats the label as its institutional home rather than as a synonym. There is also a persistent criticism that the aesthetic prizes surface beauty over risk.

Came out of

  • Third Stream — direct descendant, 1970, via form, instrumentation, harmony. George Russell lived and taught in Scandinavia from 1964, and Jan Garbarek, Terje Rypdal and Jon Christensen all played in his groups there. The label's comfort with composed structure, chamber instrumentation and European concert-music values comes down this line directly.
  • Modal Jazz — direct descendant, 1971, via harmony, form. Slow harmonic rhythm, pedal points and suspended voicings are modal jazz's inheritance, applied to folk-derived rather than blues-derived melody. Keith Jarrett's solo concerts are extended modal improvisation with the jazz repertoire removed.
  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1970, via improvisation, timbre. Garbarek, Rypdal and Christensen came out of Norwegian free jazz, and the label's early catalogue is closer to the avant-garde than its later reputation suggests. What survived the shift to lyricism is the freedom from song form and the treatment of timbre as content.
  • West Coast Jazz — parallel influence, 1972, via timbre, instrumentation. Interpretation: the piano-less chamber group, the soft attack and the arranger's ear are West Coast preoccupations arriving in Europe two decades later. Direct influence is hard to document; the family resemblance between Giuffre's trios and early ECM is strong enough to note.

Fed into

  • Nu Jazz & Electronic Jazz — fusion of, 1997
  • Contemporary Creative Jazz — parallel influence, 2005

What to hear

gateway

Keith Jarrett — The Köln Concert gateway

recorded/released 1975 · ECM ECM 1064/65 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Part I”

Keith Jarrett (p)

The best-selling solo piano album ever made, improvised in one sitting on a badly out-of-tune house piano that Jarrett nearly refused to play. He worked around the instrument's faults and the record is shaped by them.

Listen for: He avoids the piano's weak upper register and dead pedals by staying in the middle and using ostinatos. The limitation produced the style.

core

Jan Garbarek Quartet — Afric Pepperbird core

recorded/released 1970 · ECM ECM 1007 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Afric Pepperbird”

Jan Garbarek (ts, bs, fl) · Terje Rypdal (g) · Arild Andersen (b) · Jon Christensen (d)

ECM's Nordic wing at its most aggressive, before the label's reputation for beauty set in. This is free jazz played by Norwegians who had studied with George Russell, and it is closer to Ayler than to Garbarek's later sound.

Listen for: The tone is already unmistakable — cold, pure, huge — but the playing is far more violent than what Garbarek became known for a decade later.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Keith Jarrett — Belonging core

recorded/released 1974 · ECM ECM 1050 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Long as You Know You're Living Yours”

Keith Jarrett (p) · Jan Garbarek (ts, ss) · Palle Danielsson (b) · Jon Christensen (d)

Jarrett's 'European quartet', with tunes that are folk-simple and playing that is anything but. It is the most perfectly balanced record either Jarrett or Garbarek made.

Listen for: 'Long as You Know You're Living Yours' is a two-chord vamp with a melody a child could sing, and the band builds twenty minutes of tension out of refusing to resolve it.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Eberhard Weber — The Colours of Chloë core

recorded 1973, released 1974 · ECM ECM 1042 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “The Colours of Chloë”

Eberhard Weber (b, cello, ocarina) · Rainer Brüninghaus (p) · Ack van Rooyen (flugelhorn) · cello ensemble

Weber built his own five-string electric upright bass and wrote chamber music for it. The record's sound — enormous, singing, sustained bass over cellos — has no antecedent.

Listen for: The bass carries the melody for long stretches with a sustain and pitch clarity that an acoustic bass cannot produce. The instrument is the composition's premise.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Pat Metheny — Bright Size Life core

recorded 1975, released 1976 · ECM ECM 1073 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Bright Size Life”

Pat Metheny (g) · Jaco Pastorius (elb) · Bob Moses (d)

Metheny at twenty-one with Jaco Pastorius, in a trio with no chordal instrument other than the guitar. It brought an American, country-inflected lyricism into ECM's world.

Listen for: The guitar tone is clean and reverberant with almost no attack, which became one of the most imitated sounds in jazz. Pastorius is playing melody as much as bass.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Tomasz Stańko — Litania: The Music of Krzysztof Komeda core

recorded/released 1997 · ECM ECM 1636 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Svantetic”

Tomasz Stańko (tp) · Bernt Rosengren (ts) · Joakim Milder (ts) · Bobo Stenson (p) · Palle Danielsson (b) · Jon Christensen (d)

Stańko playing the music of Krzysztof Komeda, the Polish composer and Polanski's film scorer, in whose band he had played as a young man. It is a distinctly Central European strand of the label's aesthetic.

Listen for: Komeda's melodies are dark and song-like in a way that Nordic material is not — the harmonic language comes from Polish film music, not Norwegian folk.

deep

Terje Rypdal — Odyssey deep

recorded/released 1975 · ECM ECM 1067/68 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Darkness Falls”

Terje Rypdal (g) · Torbjørn Sunde (tb) · Brynjulf Blix (org) · Sveinung Hovensjø (elb) · Svein Christiansen (d)

Sustained, distorted, orchestral electric guitar inside ECM's acoustic world. Rypdal plays long held notes rather than lines, closer to a string section than to a rock guitarist.

Listen for: He almost never plays fast. The guitar produces slowly evolving sustained tones, and the volume pedal removes every attack.

Gary Peacock (b) · Keith Jarrett (p) · Jack DeJohnette (d)

Nominally Peacock's record, this is the first meeting of the trio that Jarrett later led for thirty years. The interplay is already fully formed and the writing is Peacock's, which changes the character.

Listen for: Peacock's compositions are more angular than Jarrett's, and the trio treats them more freely than it treated standards later. A different band under the same name.

Bobo Stenson Trio — Goodbye deep

recorded/released 2005 · ECM ECM 1904 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Race Face”

Bobo Stenson (p) · Anders Jormin (b) · Paul Motian (d)

The Swedish pianist with Paul Motian, playing material from Ornette Coleman to Cuban song to Alban Berg. It is the label's chamber aesthetic at its most patient and least sentimental.

Listen for: Motian's drumming has no timekeeping function at all — he is playing colour and shape, and the trio finds the pulse collectively or not at all.
