Loft Jazz 1970–1982

The second generation of the avant-garde, playing in their own apartments because no club would book them.

New York (SoHo / NoHo / Lower East Side), USA · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called New York loft scene, The Wildflowers generation

By the early 1970s the New York jazz club economy had collapsed for anyone playing anything challenging, and Manhattan was full of cheap industrial loft space. Musicians rented lofts, lived in them, and put on concerts in them. Sam Rivers and Beatrice Rivers ran Studio Rivbea on Bond Street from 1970 until around 1980; Rashied Ali ran Ali's Alley; there was also Environ, the Ladies' Fort and Studio We. What resulted was less a style than a scene with a shared condition: no owner taking a cut, no set length, no drinks minimum, an audience that came specifically for this. The music sat between free jazz and composition — the AACM and BAG diaspora had arrived from Chicago and St. Louis, and their compositional discipline mixed with New York energy playing. Duos and unusual small formats flourished because they were cheap. The five-LP Wildflowers set, recorded at Studio Rivbea over ten days in May 1976, is the scene's document. Gentrification ended it: the same loft space that had been affordable in 1972 was not by 1982.

What to listen for

  • Recordings that sound like a room, not a studio — you can hear the space and the audience
  • Unusual small line-ups: two saxophones and nothing else, or saxophone and drums alone
  • Written composed sections bracketing long free improvisations
  • Grooves and repeated figures returning, which 1960s free jazz mostly avoided
  • Sets that clearly ran as long as the musicians wanted

How it works

harmony
Wide open — freely atonal, modal, and occasionally straightforwardly tonal within one set. No single harmonic system.
rhythm
Free time predominates, but the second generation reintroduced grooves, ostinatos and explicit metric structures more readily than the 1960s players.
form
Composed frames around free sections. Duo and trio formats where structure has to be negotiated live.
instrumentation
Saxophone-heavy, with the all-saxophone ensemble emerging here. Cello, tuba, bass clarinet and multiple percussionists are common.
improvisation
Collective, long-form and highly interactive, typically in small groups where every player is audible at all times.
timbre
Dry, close, unglamorous — most of these recordings were made in the rooms themselves, and the acoustics are part of the document.

Key figures

  • Sam Rivers (tenor and soprano saxophone, flute, piano) — Ran Studio Rivbea, the scene's centre, and played long-form free music with unusual structural clarity.
  • David Murray (tenor saxophone, bass clarinet) — Arrived from California as a very young man and became the scene's most prolific figure; his tone reaches back to Ayler and Hawkins at once.
  • Julius Hemphill (alto saxophone, composition) — Brought BAG's compositional approach from St. Louis and formed the World Saxophone Quartet out of the scene.
  • Anthony Braxton (reeds, composition) — A constant presence, bringing AACM structural thinking into the New York environment.
  • Rashied Ali (drums) — Coltrane's last drummer, who ran Ali's Alley and issued the scene's records on his own Survival label.
  • Oliver Lake (alto saxophone, composition) — BAG co-founder and WSQ member; among the scene's most consistent composer-bandleaders.

Labels: Douglas · India Navigation · Black Saint/Soul Note · Survival · hatHUT

Contested

Whether loft jazz is a style or a real-estate situation. Critics at the time used it as a catch-all for New York avant-garde music in the 1970s, and the musicians involved played very differently from each other. This guide treats it as a scene-genre — defined by its conditions of production, which genuinely shaped the music that came out.

Came out of

  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1970, via improvisation, social-context. Same music, different economics. When the club circuit closed to free players, they rented lofts and became their own promoters — which removed set lengths, drink minimums and owner veto, and produced longer, more patient performances than any club would have allowed.
  • AACM & Great Black Music — direct descendant, 1973, via form, instrumentation, social-context. The AACM and St. Louis's BAG both sent members to New York in the early 1970s, and they brought the self-organising model and the compositional discipline with them. The all-saxophone ensemble and the duo format both flourish here for the same reason: they are cheap and they demand structure.

Fed into

  • Downtown & Punk Jazz — direct descendant, 1980

What to hear

gateway

Various Artists — Wildflowers: The New York Loft Jazz Sessions gateway

recorded 1976, released 1977 · Douglas NBLP 7045-7049 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Ismell (Sam Rivers Trio)”

Sam Rivers (ts, fl) · David Murray (ts) · Anthony Braxton (reeds) · Julius Hemphill (as) · Randy Weston (p) · Andrew Cyrille (d) · Oliver Lake (as)

Five LPs recorded at Studio Rivbea over ten days in May 1976, capturing almost a hundred musicians in a room that musicians owned. It is the scene's only comprehensive document.

Listen for: The sound of the room and the audience is left in. These are not concert recordings or studio dates — they are a loft, on a weeknight, with about forty people present.

Sam Rivers (ts, ss, fl, p) · Cecil McBee (b) · Norman Connors (d)

A single continuous performance from the Montreux festival where Rivers moves through four instruments without a break, structuring forty minutes of free playing entirely by ear.

Listen for: Nothing is composed and it never sounds shapeless. Rivers builds sections, closes them and starts new ones — free improvisation with an architect's discipline.

Murray at twenty-one, newly arrived in New York, with a tone that reaches back past free jazz to Ben Webster and Coleman Hawkins. The title track is an elegy for Albert Ayler.

Listen for: His vibrato and his enormous low register are pre-bebop devices used inside free playing. The avant-garde reconnecting to the tenor tradition happens here.

Recorded in St. Louis for Hemphill's own label, with a cello instead of a bass and a rhythmic figure derived from Dogon material. It is one of the great records almost nobody heard until its reissue.

Listen for: The title track's groove is in an odd meter and repeats hypnotically for over a quarter of an hour. Abdul Wadud's cello is playing bass, harmony and counter-melody at once.

Arthur Blythe — Lenox Avenue Breakdown core

recorded/released 1979 · Columbia JC 35638 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Down San Diego Way”

Arthur Blythe (as) · James Newton (fl) · Bob Stewart (tuba) · James 'Blood' Ulmer (g) · Cecil McBee (b) · Jack DeJohnette (d)

A major label signed a loft-scene altoist and he made a record with tuba, flute and harmolodic guitar that grooves hard. It is the moment the scene briefly touched the mainstream.

Listen for: Bob Stewart's tuba is the bass instrument and the hook. Forty years later the London scene built a whole aesthetic on that choice.

recorded 1977, released 1978 · Nessa N-12 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Keep Right on Playing Thru the Mirror Over the Water”

Henry Threadgill (as, ts, fl, hubkaphone) · Fred Hopkins (b) · Steve McCall (d)

The AACM trio working in New York's loft environment, with Threadgill's homemade 'hubkaphone' built from car hubcaps. Composition and free playing are indistinguishable here.

Listen for: Fred Hopkins plays bass as a lead voice throughout. In a trio with no chordal instrument, he is carrying both the bottom and a large part of the melodic content.

recorded/released 1976 · Black Saint BSR 0006 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Holding Together” · medium confidence

Oliver Lake (as, fl) · Michael Gregory Jackson (g) · Fred Hopkins (b) · Paul Maddox (d)

BAG's approach applied in New York, with an electric guitar in a free context years before that became normal. Lake's writing is tighter and more angular than most of the scene's.

Listen for: Michael Gregory Jackson's guitar is clean and rock-derived rather than jazz-toned, which puts this closer to what downtown would sound like a few years later.

Braxton's first major-label album, made in New York during the loft years and including a saxophone quartet piece that anticipates the World Saxophone Quartet by two years.

Listen for: The diagram titles are not affectation — each piece has a specified structure, and the quartet track's density comes from four horns executing written counterpoint.

Frank Lowe — Black Beings deep

recorded/released 1973 · ESP-Disk ESP 3013 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “In Trane's Name” · medium confidence

Frank Lowe (ts) · Joseph Jarman (as) · Raymond Lee Cheng (vln) · William Parker (b) · Rashid Sinan (d)

Loft-era energy playing at full intensity, with William Parker's first recorded appearance. It is uncompromising in a way that even the loft scene's other records are not.

Listen for: The violin is used as a screaming upper voice rather than a lyrical one, and the whole record operates at a single very high level of intensity for its duration.

Free Funk & Harmolodics 1975–1998

Free jazz over a hard funk backbeat, with everybody playing a lead line at once.

New York, USA · Electric & Crossover · also called Harmolodic funk, Punk jazz (early)

In 1975 Ornette Coleman plugged in. Prime Time paired two electric guitars, two electric basses and two drummers with his alto, playing his harmolodic theory in an idiom built on funk backbeats. Harmolodics is Coleman's own concept and he explained it inconsistently, but the working version is: harmony, movement and melody have equal status, no instrument is subordinate, and players can transpose a line to whatever register or key suits their instrument while it remains 'the same' line. In practice that produces a thick, deliberately unresolved texture where four or five people are all playing melody over a groove that stays put. Ronald Shannon Jackson's Decoding Society and James Blood Ulmer took it in harder, more rock-facing directions, and it became a direct input to the downtown New York scene and to the whole idea that free improvisation could coexist with a heavy beat. Coleman's own Prime Time records remain the clearest statement, and Song X with Pat Metheny in 1985 dragged the approach onto a much larger stage.

What to listen for

  • A rock-solid funk beat underneath music that is otherwise completely unresolved
  • Two guitars and two basses all playing different lines at once
  • Melodies that sound simple and singable, dropped into total harmonic chaos
  • No obvious soloist — several people appear to be leading simultaneously
  • Everything sounding slightly out of tune with everything else, on purpose

How it works

harmony
Harmolodic — no fixed key centre, instruments playing lines in different keys simultaneously, consonance and dissonance treated as equally usable.
rhythm
Hard funk and second-line backbeats, often with two drummers producing overlapping rather than unified patterns.
form
Themes stated in unison then abandoned; the groove continues while the melodic content roams free.
instrumentation
Doubled everything — two guitars, two basses, two drummers — plus alto saxophone or trumpet on top.
improvisation
Simultaneous and non-hierarchical. There is often no soloist because everyone is soloing.
timbre
Bright, trebly, deliberately cluttered. Guitars are clean-toned and busy rather than distorted and sustaining.

Key figures

  • Ornette Coleman (alto saxophone, composition) — Invented harmolodics and Prime Time; the entire idiom is his theory made audible.
  • Ronald Shannon Jackson (drums, composition) — The Decoding Society took harmolodic principles into denser, more composed and harder-hitting territory.
  • James Blood Ulmer (guitar, vocals) — Applied harmolodics to blues and rock forms; his guitar tuning and attack have no real precedent.
  • Jamaaladeen Tacuma (electric bass) — Prime Time's bassist, who treated the bass as a lead melodic voice rather than a foundation.
  • Bern Nix (guitar) — Prime Time's clean-toned guitarist, whose refusal to play rock guitar clichés is central to the band's sound.

Labels: Artists House · Antilles · Island/Antilles · Caravan of Dreams · DIW

Contested

Whether harmolodics is a coherent theory or an idiosyncratic vocabulary. Coleman's explanations varied and musicians who played the music describe it in incompatible ways. This guide treats free funk as a genre and harmolodics as one leader's method for producing it — which is an interpretive choice, not a settled position.

Came out of

  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1975, via harmony, improvisation. Ornette Coleman's harmolodic principle — every instrument carrying melody, no fixed key centre, no accompanist role — is carried unchanged from the 1959 quartet into Prime Time. Only the rhythmic floor and the electricity are new.
  • Jazz-Funk — fusion of, 1975, via rhythm, instrumentation, technology. The backbeat, the doubled electric instruments and the repeating groove come from funk as it stood in the mid-1970s. Placing a hard, danceable floor under fully free melodic playing is the whole proposition, and nobody had done it seriously before.

Fed into

  • M-Base — direct descendant, 1985
  • Downtown & Punk Jazz — direct descendant, 1981

What to hear

gateway

Ornette Coleman — Dancing in Your Head gateway

recorded 1975, released 1977 · A&M Horizon SP-722 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Theme from a Symphony (Variation One)”

Ornette Coleman (as) · Bern Nix (g) · Charles Ellerbee (g) · Rudy McDaniel [Jamaaladeen Tacuma] (elb) · Ronald Shannon Jackson (d)

Prime Time's arrival. Two guitars, electric bass and drums playing a hard groove while a simple, repeated melody is bent in every direction on top. Also includes Coleman playing with Moroccan musicians in Joujouka.

Listen for: The tune is one short phrase, repeated for over twenty minutes. Everything else — five people playing melody simultaneously — happens around and against it.

Ornette Coleman and Prime Time — Of Human Feelings core

recorded 1979, released 1982 · Antilles AN 2001 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Sleep Talk”

Ornette Coleman (as) · Bern Nix (g) · Charles Ellerbee (g) · Jamaaladeen Tacuma (elb) · Calvin Weston (d) · Denardo Coleman (d)

Prime Time fully formed: doubled guitars, doubled basses, doubled drummers, and one of the first jazz records recorded digitally. Harmolodics as a working band language rather than a theory.

Listen for: There is no accompanist. Every instrument is playing a melodic line, and the groove emerges from six independent melodies happening to coincide rhythmically.

James Blood Ulmer — Tales of Captain Black core

recorded 1978, released 1979 · Artists House AH 7 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Theme from Captain Black”

James Blood Ulmer (g) · Ornette Coleman (as) · Jamaaladeen Tacuma (elb) · Denardo Coleman (d)

Ulmer's debut with Coleman as sideman, and the clearest recording of harmolodic guitar. His tuning and attack have no obvious ancestor in either jazz or blues guitar.

Listen for: Ulmer tunes his guitar so that all the strings are related to one pitch, which makes chords ambiguous by design. Nothing sounds like it is in a key.

Ronald Shannon Jackson and the Decoding Society — Eye on You core

recorded/released 1980 · About Time AT 1001 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “When Souls Speak” · medium confidence

Ronald Shannon Jackson (d, comp) · Vernon Reid (g) · Byard Lancaster (as) · Melvin Gibbs (elb)

Jackson had played with Ornette Coleman, Cecil Taylor and Albert Ayler, and the Decoding Society is what he built out of all three plus Texas funk. Vernon Reid is on guitar, years before Living Colour.

Listen for: Jackson's drumming is a second-line New Orleans feel underneath free playing. He grew up in Fort Worth and it shows in every bar.

recorded 1985, released 1986 · Geffen GHS 24096 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Song X”

Ornette Coleman (as, vln) · Pat Metheny (g, guitar synth) · Charlie Haden (b) · Jack DeJohnette (d) · Denardo Coleman (d)

A guitarist with a huge mainstream audience made a Coleman record on a rock label and did not soften it. It confused Metheny's listeners and introduced harmolodics to a lot of them anyway.

Listen for: Metheny's guitar synth is used for noise and texture rather than for melody. He is not visiting the idiom politely.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jamaaladeen Tacuma — Show Stopper core

recorded 1982, released 1983 · Gramavision GR 8301 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Dancing in Your Head” · medium confidence

Jamaaladeen Tacuma (elb) · Cornell Rochester (d) · Bill Bruford (d) · Julius Hemphill (as) · Olu Dara (tp)

Prime Time's bassist as a leader, treating electric bass as a lead melodic voice with a bright, trebly tone that nobody else used. The writing is funkier and more song-shaped than Coleman's.

Listen for: The bass is mixed as the lead instrument and plays in the upper register throughout. The bottom end is being handled by the kick drum alone.

deep

Last Exit — Last Exit deep

recorded/released 1986 · Enemy EMY 101 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Discharge” · medium confidence

European free jazz, harmolodic drums, noise guitar and a producer's bass, playing at metal volume with no material prepared at all. It is the loudest music in this guide.

Listen for: Nothing is composed. Four musicians with completely different backgrounds improvising at maximum volume, and it coheres — barely, and on purpose.

Jackson's compositions at their most intricate, with written horn lines over grooves in unusual meters. It is more composed than most free funk and consequently harder to play.

recorded/released 1991 · Axiom 314-512 070-2 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Promises Kept”

Sonny Sharrock (g) · Pharoah Sanders (ts) · Charnett Moffett (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

Sharrock's last major record, produced by Bill Laswell, with Pharoah Sanders and Elvin Jones. It is a Coltrane-quartet record with an electric guitarist in Coltrane's chair, and it works.

Listen for: Sharrock plays with sustained feedback and slide as melodic devices. Elvin Jones plays exactly as he did in 1965 and the two languages fit.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Smooth Jazz 1975–present

Jazz-funk sanded down for radio: melody in front, solos short, nothing that would make a listener flinch.

Los Angeles, USA · Electric & Crossover · also called Contemporary jazz (radio format), Quiet storm jazz

Smooth jazz is a radio format that became a genre, and it should be described that way rather than sneered at. Its ancestry is real — Grover Washington Jr., Wes Montgomery's late CTI records, Bob James, the Crusaders — and its ingredients are consistent: a strong singable melody carried by soprano saxophone or guitar, a mid-tempo funk or quiet-storm groove, glossy production with a lot of reverb, and improvisation kept short and consonant. Kenny G's Breathless in 1992 sold in the millions and became the reference point for everything wrong with the format for jazz critics, which is a lot of weight to put on one record. The format's radio dominance from the late 1980s through the early 2000s meant that for a large number of people this was what 'jazz' meant, which is part of why the hostility was so intense. Musically the interesting question is what got removed: harmonic risk, rhythmic disruption, and the possibility that a solo might fail. Nothing is at stake, deliberately. Some of the players — Washington, Larry Carlton, Lee Ritenour — could do far more than the format asked.

What to listen for

  • A soprano saxophone playing a melody you could hum after one listen
  • Everything mixed clean, wide and glossy, with a lot of reverb
  • Solos that last eight bars and never go outside the chord
  • A steady mid-tempo groove that stays exactly the same throughout
  • Tracks that end in a fade at about four and a half minutes

How it works

harmony
Consonant and stable — major sevenths, gentle ii–V motion, no altered dominants. Modulations are for lift, not tension.
rhythm
Mid-tempo funk, quiet-storm ballad feels, and later programmed drums and quantised grids.
form
Pop song form. Melody, short solo, melody, fade. Tracks run to radio length.
instrumentation
Soprano and alto saxophone, clean electric guitar, synthesizers and DX7-era electric pianos, fretless bass, programmed and live drums.
improvisation
Brief and melodic, staying inside the harmony. Solos are usually written or worked out in advance.
timbre
Highly produced — chorus, digital reverb, gated drums. The 1980s studio sound is inseparable from the genre.

Key figures

  • Grover Washington Jr. (saxophones) — The genuine link between jazz-funk and the format; a serious improviser whose commercial records set the template.
  • Bob James (keyboards, arranging) — CTI arranger whose 1970s records supplied both the format's sound and a great many hip-hop samples.
  • David Sanborn (alto saxophone) — His piercing, blues-derived alto tone became the most imitated sound in the idiom, though his own playing has more bite than the copies.
  • Kenny G (soprano saxophone) — The best-selling instrumentalist of the era and the lightning rod for every argument about the format's legitimacy.
  • The Crusaders (group) — Went from hard bop as the Jazz Crusaders to a groove-based crossover sound that prefigured the whole format.

Labels: GRP · Warner Bros. · Arista · Verve Forecast

Contested

Whether it counts as jazz. The critical consensus has been dismissive for forty years, and the argument that it removes improvisational risk has force. The counter-argument is that dismissing it wholesale erases the Black radio and quiet-storm context it grew out of, and flattens genuine players into one caricature. This guide includes it as a real branch with a real lineage, and does not pretend to be neutral about the ceiling.

Came out of

  • Jazz-Funk — direct descendant, 1975, via rhythm, instrumentation, timbre. Smooth jazz is jazz-funk with the risk removed: the same groove, the same instruments, but consonant harmony, short solos and a melody engineered for radio recall. Grover Washington Jr. is on both sides of the line and the transition is audible across his 1970s records.
  • Jazz Fusion — direct descendant, 1979, via technology, timbre. The studio apparatus transferred wholesale — synthesizers, gated drums, digital reverb, multitrack polish — while the odd meters and extended solos that justified it were dropped. Several fusion players moved into the format when the fusion market collapsed.

What to hear

gateway

Grover Washington Jr. — Winelight gateway

recorded/released 1980 · Elektra 6E-305 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Winelight”

Grover Washington Jr. (ss, as, ts) · Bill Withers (voc) · Marcus Miller (elb) · Eric Gale (g) · Ralph MacDonald (percussion)

The record that defined the format, made by a musician who could genuinely play. 'Just the Two of Us' with Bill Withers was a top-three single and the album won two Grammys.

Listen for: Marcus Miller's bass playing is far more interesting than the format requires. Underneath the gloss there is a serious rhythm section doing careful work.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

core

The Crusaders — Street Life core

recorded/released 1979 · MCA MCA-3094 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Street Life”

Joe Sample (keys) · Wilton Felder (ts, elb) · Stix Hooper (d) · Randy Crawford (voc) · Barry Finnerty (g)

The Jazz Crusaders were a hard bop band in 1961; by 1979 they had dropped the 'Jazz' and had a hit single with a vocalist. The whole crossover arc is visible in one group's discography.

Listen for: Wilton Felder's tenor still phrases like a hard bop player. The arrangement around him has changed completely and he has not.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

George Benson — Breezin' core

recorded/released 1976 · Warner Bros. BS 2919 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Breezin'”

George Benson (g, voc) · Ronnie Foster (keys) · Jorge Dalto (keys) · Phil Upchurch (g) · Stanley Banks (elb) · Harvey Mason (d)

The first jazz album to be certified platinum, made by a guitarist who had been Jack McDuff's sideman at nineteen. It is where a formidable bebop player chose the crossover route deliberately.

Listen for: Benson sings in unison with his guitar solos, an octave apart. It became a signature and it is technically much harder than it sounds.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

David Sanborn — Voyeur core

recorded 1980, released 1981 · Warner Bros. BSK 3546 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Let's Just Say Goodbye”

David Sanborn (as) · Marcus Miller (elb) · Buzz Feiten (g) · Steve Gadd (d)

Sanborn's alto tone — piercing, blues-derived, instantly identifiable — became the most imitated sound in the format. This is the record where it locked into place.

Listen for: The tone comes from playing rhythm and blues behind singers, not from Charlie Parker. It is a different lineage and the intensity in it is real.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Spyro Gyra — Morning Dance core

recorded/released 1979 · Infinity INF 9004 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Morning Dance”

Jay Beckenstein (as, ss) · Jeremy Wall (keys) · Tom Schuman (keys) · Chet Catallo (g)

The commercial template in its purest form: a steel-drum hook, an unvarying mid-tempo groove and a saxophone melody you cannot dislodge. It sold over a million copies.

Listen for: Nothing is at stake at any point, by design. Hearing what has been engineered out is more informative than hearing what is there.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Kenny G — Breathless core

recorded/released 1992 · Arista 18646 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Forever in Love”

Kenny G (ss, as, ts) · Walter Afanasieff (prod, keys)

One of the best-selling instrumental albums in history and the record that made 'smooth jazz' an insult. It belongs here because pretending it does not exist explains nothing about how the genre worked.

Listen for: The melodies are written to be memorable on one hearing and the improvisation is minimal. Whatever else it is, it is expertly built for its purpose.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Wes Montgomery — A Day in the Life deep

recorded/released 1967 · A&M/CTI SP-3001 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “A Day in the Life”

Wes Montgomery (g) · Don Sebesky (arr) · Herbie Hancock (p) · Ron Carter (b) · Grady Tate (d)

Creed Taylor put one of the great jazz guitarists in front of a string orchestra playing Beatles covers, and it sold. This is the ancestral form of the smooth jazz proposition.

Listen for: Montgomery's octave technique and thumb-picked tone are entirely intact; the setting around them is pop. The argument about whether that is a compromise starts here.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Bob James & Earl Klugh — One on One deep

recorded/released 1979 · Tappan Zee/Columbia JC 36241 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Kari”

Bob James (keys) · Earl Klugh (g) · Gary King (elb) · Harvey Mason (d)

Nylon-string guitar and electric piano as a duo-led format, which is the most chamber-like the genre gets. It won a Grammy and set the acoustic-guitar wing of the format going.

Listen for: Klugh plays fingerstyle nylon-string, a classical technique, in a pop-funk setting. The combination is more unusual than it sounds now.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Pat Metheny Group — Still Life (Talking) deep

recorded/released 1987 · Geffen GHS 24145 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Last Train Home”

Pat Metheny (g, guitar synth) · Lyle Mays (keys) · Steve Rodby (b) · Paul Wertico (d) · Armando Marçal (percussion) · Mark Ledford (voc)

The most musically ambitious record that regularly gets shelved next to smooth jazz, which says something about how blunt the category is. Brazilian rhythm, wordless vocals and genuinely complex writing.

Listen for: 'Last Train Home' has an ostinato that never stops and a melody that arrives late and leaves early. It is doing what smooth jazz does and doing it with real compositional care.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Downtown & Punk Jazz 1978–present

Free jazz colliding with hardcore punk, surf, klezmer and cartoon music, usually at high speed and without warning.

New York (Lower East Side), USA · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called Knitting Factory scene, No wave jazz, Radical Jewish Culture (adjacent)

Lower Manhattan in the late 1970s and 80s had a scene where the boundary between avant-garde jazz, no wave, punk and contemporary composition simply was not enforced. John Zorn is the organising figure. His game pieces — Cobra above all, from 1984 — are structured improvisations run by a prompter using cue cards, where the rules govern who plays and when but not what. Naked City compressed hardcore punk, film noir soundtracks, country and free jazz into pieces lasting under a minute, switching genre every few seconds. The Lounge Lizards played what Zorn's contemporary John Lurie called 'fake jazz', deadpan and hard-edged. Bill Frisell, Wayne Horvitz, Elliott Sharp, Marc Ribot and Tim Berne all worked across the same rooms — the Knitting Factory from 1987, and before it a network of lofts and galleries. Zorn's Tzadik label from 1995 institutionalised it. The Jewish identity strand — Masada, the Radical Jewish Culture series — brought klezmer modes into an Ornette Coleman quartet format. What holds the scene together is a compositional attitude: rapid juxtaposition, genre as material, and structure imposed on improvisation from outside.

What to listen for

  • The style changing completely every few seconds without transition
  • Hardcore punk speed and screaming next to something that swings
  • Pieces that last thirty seconds and then stop
  • Klezmer or surf melodies played by a free jazz rhythm section
  • A group that is clearly following rules you can't hear

How it works

harmony
Anything, and often several things per minute. Klezmer modes, surf progressions, free atonality and hardcore power chords used as blocks.
rhythm
Extreme contrast — blast beats next to free time next to a shuffle. Metric stability is a choice, not a default.
form
Game pieces, file-card compositions, and blocks of material spliced with hard cuts. Duration ranges from 20 seconds to an hour.
instrumentation
Alto saxophone, guitar, bass, drums, plus turntables, samplers, voice, and whatever else. Electric and amplified by default.
improvisation
Central but governed. The composer sets rules of engagement rather than notes; players improvise inside a designed system.
timbre
Loud, abrasive, and stylistically promiscuous. Studio and live production are aggressive rather than transparent.

Key figures

  • John Zorn (alto saxophone, composition) — The scene's organiser, composer and label head; the game pieces and Masada are the two poles of his output.
  • Bill Frisell (guitar) — Developed a volume-pedal, delay-heavy Americana-inflected guitar language that was equally at home downtown and on ECM.
  • Marc Ribot (guitar) — The scene's most abrasive and versatile guitarist, moving between Zorn, Tom Waits and Cuban repertoire.
  • Tim Berne (alto saxophone, composition) — Wrote long, intricate compositions for working bands, connecting Julius Hemphill's lineage to the downtown environment.
  • John Lurie (saxophone) — The Lounge Lizards' deadpan 'fake jazz' set the scene's tone of ironic seriousness.
  • Elliott Sharp (guitar, reeds, composition) — Brought algorithmic and mathematical structures into an otherwise intuitive scene.

Labels: Tzadik · Knitting Factory Works · Nonesuch · JMT · Elektra Nonesuch

Contested

Whether it is jazz. A large part of the output has no swing, no blues and no improvisation on changes, and 'downtown' names a geography and a social network rather than a style. Its inclusion here rests on personnel and lineage — most of these players came through free jazz — and on the fact that the scene fed contemporary creative music directly.

Came out of

  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 1978, via improvisation, timbre, instrumentation. The extended techniques, the abandonment of changes and the tolerance for noise are free jazz's, and most of the downtown players came up through it. What downtown adds is a composer imposing structure from outside — the freedom is scheduled.
  • Loft Jazz — direct descendant, 1980, via social-context, instrumentation, form. Physical and institutional continuity: the same Lower Manhattan rooms, the same self-organising economics, and in several cases the same musicians. The Knitting Factory in 1987 is the loft model with a liquor licence.
  • Free Funk & Harmolodics — direct descendant, 1981, via rhythm, instrumentation, technology. Harmolodic funk demonstrated that a rock rhythm section and free playing could coexist, which is the precondition for punk jazz. Ronald Shannon Jackson, James Blood Ulmer and Bill Laswell moved between both scenes constantly.
  • European Free Improvisation — parallel influence, 1982, via form, timbre, improvisation. John Zorn played in Derek Bailey's Company gatherings, and the game pieces owe their premise — structure that governs interaction rather than pitch — to that encounter. The European emphasis on timbre over line is audible throughout downtown playing.

Fed into

  • Contemporary Creative Jazz — direct descendant, 2000

What to hear

gateway

John Zorn — Cobra gateway

recorded 1985, released 1987 · hatART hatART 2034 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Cobra (Studio Version)”

John Zorn (prompter) · Bill Frisell (g) · Wayne Horvitz (keys) · Elliott Sharp (g) · Anthony Coleman (keys) · Christian Marclay (turntables) · Bobby Previte (d)

A game piece: no notes are written, only rules governing who plays, when, and in what relationship. A prompter holds up cards and the ensemble responds. It is the downtown method at its purest.

Listen for: The music changes direction abruptly and completely, over and over. Those are the cues. Once you realise you are hearing a system operating, it stops sounding arbitrary.

core

Naked City — Naked City core

recorded 1989, released 1990 · Elektra Nonesuch 9 79238-2 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Batman”

John Zorn (as) · Bill Frisell (g) · Wayne Horvitz (keys) · Fred Frith (b) · Joey Baron (d) · Yamatsuka Eye (voc)

Twenty-six tracks including several under thirty seconds, cutting between hardcore punk, surf, film noir jazz and country with no transitions. The band could execute the cuts at speed, which is why it works.

Listen for: 'Speedfreaks' compresses about eight genres into forty seconds. The changes are written and rehearsed, not improvised — the precision is the joke's delivery.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

The Lounge Lizards — The Lounge Lizards core

recorded 1980, released 1981 · EG EGS 108 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Incident on South Street”

John Lurie (as) · Evan Lurie (p) · Arto Lindsay (g) · Steve Piccolo (b) · Anton Fier (d)

'Fake jazz', as Lurie called it: noir themes played deadpan by musicians from the no wave scene, with Arto Lindsay's untuned guitar noise cutting across. It set the downtown tone of ironic seriousness.

Listen for: Arto Lindsay is playing a twelve-string guitar he does not tune, purely as noise, over what is otherwise a jazz quintet. Neither element concedes.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Masada — Alef core

recorded 1993, released 1994 · DIW DIW-888 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Jair”

John Zorn (as) · Dave Douglas (tp) · Greg Cohen (b) · Joey Baron (d)

Zorn's Ornette Coleman quartet playing Jewish scales and modes — the Radical Jewish Culture project in its most focused form, and the most straightforwardly beautiful thing he has made.

Listen for: The instrumentation and the pulse-keeping freedom are lifted from Ornette Coleman's 1959 quartet exactly. The melodic material is klezmer and Middle Eastern modes.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Bill Frisell — Have a Little Faith core

recorded 1992, released 1993 · Elektra Nonesuch 9 79301-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Billy the Kid”

Bill Frisell (g) · Don Byron (cl) · Guy Klucevsek (accordion) · Kermit Driscoll (b) · Joey Baron (d)

Copland, Sousa, Madonna, Muddy Waters and Bob Dylan played by a downtown quintet with an accordion. The American repertoire treated as one continuous body of material.

Listen for: The Copland 'Billy the Kid' arrangement is played with total sincerity by a band that also covers 'Live to Tell'. Frisell never signals which one is the joke, because neither is.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Tim Berne — Fractured Fairy Tales core

recorded/released 1989 · JMT JMT 834 431-2 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Fractured Fairy Tales” · medium confidence

Tim Berne (as) · Herb Robertson (tp) · Mark Dresser (b) · Hank Roberts (cello) · Joey Baron (d)

Berne writes long, intricate, multi-section pieces for working bands and this is his most tightly organised record. He came out of Julius Hemphill's tutelage and it shows in the horn writing.

Listen for: Pieces run fifteen minutes and never repeat a section. The structures are through-composed with improvisation slotted in, which is closer to the AACM than to downtown's cut-ups.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Marc Ribot — Rootless Cosmopolitans deep

recorded/released 1990 · Island/Antilles 422-848 361-2 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “The Wind Cries Mary” · medium confidence

Marc Ribot (g, voc) · Don Byron (cl) · Anthony Coleman (keys) · Melvin Gibbs (elb) · Roy Nathanson (as)

Ribot's debut as leader, covering Hendrix and Ellington with equal deliberate awkwardness. His guitar playing is the scene's most abrasive and least virtuosic-sounding, on purpose.

Listen for: He plays like someone who cannot quite reach the note and does not care. It is a technique, not a limitation, and it is the opposite of every guitar aesthetic around it.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

James White and the Blacks — Off White deep

recorded/released 1979 · ZE ZEA 33-002 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Contort Yourself”

James Chance (as, voc) · Pat Place (g) · Robert Quine (g) · Don Christensen (d)

The no wave prehistory of punk jazz: Albert Ayler's saxophone screaming over a James Brown rhythm section, played by people from the New York art-rock scene rather than the jazz one.

Listen for: The saxophone playing is genuinely free-jazz-derived and the band is playing disco-funk. Neither is a parody of the other, which is what makes it uncomfortable.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Elliott Sharp / Carbon — Tocsin deep

recorded 1989, released 1990 · SST SST 246 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Tocsin” · low confidence

Elliott Sharp (g, reeds) · David Weinstein (keys) · Zeena Parkins (harp) · Samm Bennett (percussion)

Sharp built structures from Fibonacci sequences and fractal geometry and then had a band improvise inside them, on a hardcore punk label. It is downtown's most rigorously systematic wing.

Listen for: Zeena Parkins' electric harp is processed beyond recognition. The harp as a noise instrument is one of the scene's genuinely original contributions.

Note: Elliott Sharp's late-1980s Carbon discography is spread across several small labels and is inconsistently documented.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Neo-Bop & the Young Lions 1980–present

A deliberate return to acoustic hard bop and swing, made by conservatory-trained players who thought fusion had been a wrong turn.

New York / New Orleans, USA · Traditional & Mainstream · also called Neoclassical jazz, The Young Lions, Neo-traditionalism

Wynton Marsalis signed to Columbia in 1981 at nineteen, won Grammys in both jazz and classical categories in 1983, and became the public face of an argument: that jazz had a tradition, that the tradition was acoustic and blues-based and ran from New Orleans through Ellington and Parker to the mid-1960s, and that fusion and free jazz had taken the music away from it. The music that followed is technically formidable and largely acoustic — quintets and sextets, standards and originals in a post-bop language, sharp suits, no synthesizers. It coincided with the institutionalisation of jazz: university programmes, Jazz at Lincoln Center from 1987 with Marsalis as artistic director, the Ken Burns documentary in 2001, repertory orchestras, NEA Jazz Masters. The criticism, made forcefully by Nicholas Payton, Greg Osby and others, is that this froze the music into a museum canon at exactly the moment it should have been moving. The defence is that a generation of musicians got a rigorous grounding they would not otherwise have had. Both are true and the argument has not settled.

What to listen for

  • Entirely acoustic instruments, with a swing feel front and centre
  • Playing that is audibly virtuosic and audibly schooled
  • Deliberate references to earlier styles — a New Orleans strut, an Ellington voicing
  • Standards played straight rather than deconstructed
  • A clean, warm, modern recording of a determinedly old-fashioned band

How it works

harmony
Bebop and post-bop harmony played with conservatory precision — full changes, extended forms, and a deliberate refusal of modal shortcuts.
rhythm
Acoustic swing restored as the default, with New Orleans second-line and Latin feels used as compositional colour.
form
Standards and originals in traditional forms, plus extended suites, especially in Marsalis's later large-scale works.
instrumentation
Acoustic quintet and sextet, big band, no electric instruments as a matter of policy in the strict wing.
improvisation
Highly schooled, harmonically precise, and consciously in dialogue with recorded history — quotation and stylistic allusion are common.
timbre
Warm, controlled, well-recorded acoustic sound. Digital-era production without digital-era instruments.

Key figures

  • Wynton Marsalis (trumpet, composition) — The movement's musician, spokesman and institution-builder; his 1997 Pulitzer for Blood on the Fields was the first awarded to a jazz work.
  • Branford Marsalis (saxophones) — Took the same training in a looser direction, including work with Sting and a hip-hop project his brother would not have made.
  • Terence Blanchard (trumpet, composition) — Came through the Jazz Messengers, then built a major second career in film scoring and opera.
  • Joshua Redman (tenor saxophone) — Won the Monk competition in 1991 and became the most visible of the second Young Lions wave.
  • Roy Hargrove (trumpet) — The one who most obviously refused the boundaries — hard bop, Afro-Cuban, and the RH Factor's neo-soul, all seriously.
  • Marcus Roberts (piano) — Went furthest back, rebuilding stride and ragtime technique inside a modern practice.

Labels: Columbia · Blue Note · Warner Bros. · Verve · Nonesuch

Contested

This genre is an argument as much as a style. Critics including Nicholas Payton and Greg Osby have argued it produced a canon-policing orthodoxy that damaged the music's forward motion and marginalised the avant-garde. Defenders point to the training, the archives and the institutions that resulted. The dispute over who gets to define the tradition is the actual content of the category.

Came out of

  • Jazz Fusion — reaction against, 1981, via technology, instrumentation, social-context. The Young Lions defined themselves by what they refused: electric instruments, rock rhythm, crossover marketing, and the idea that jazz needed to chase a pop audience. Wynton Marsalis's public argument that fusion was a wrong turn is inseparable from the music he made instead.
  • Hard Bop — revival of, 1980, via harmony, form, improvisation. The Jazz Messengers were still running, and Wynton and Branford Marsalis, Terence Blanchard and Donald Harrison all passed through Blakey's band before leading their own. The revival is literal — the same institution, twenty-five years on.
  • Post-Bop — direct descendant, 1982, via harmony, form, improvisation. The actual template for the Marsalis quintet is the 1964–68 Miles Davis group: same instrumentation, same elastic time, same harmonically ambiguous writing. Herbie Hancock, Ron Carter and Tony Williams appear on Wynton Marsalis's early records in person.
  • Swing & Big Band — revival of, 1986, via instrumentation, repertoire, form. The second phase of the movement reached back past bebop to Ellington, Morton and the blues, and built repertory orchestras to play that material. Jazz at Lincoln Center from 1987 institutionalised the claim that this history is the tradition.
  • New Orleans Jazz — revival of, 1989, via repertoire, rhythm, social-context. Marsalis's own turn toward New Orleans — second-line rhythm, collective polyphony, plunger-muted brass — reframed the movement as a claim on the music's whole lineage rather than on hard bop alone. It is also a claim about who owns that lineage.

Fed into

  • Contemporary Creative Jazz — parallel influence, 2002

What to hear

gateway

Wynton Marsalis — Black Codes (From the Underground) gateway

recorded/released 1985 · Columbia FC 40009 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Black Codes”

Wynton Marsalis (tp) · Branford Marsalis (ts, ss) · Kenny Kirkland (p) · Charnett Moffett (b) · Jeff 'Tain' Watts (d)

The strongest record of the movement's first phase, made by a working band of contemporaries rather than by a young leader with veteran sidemen. The writing is genuinely difficult and the playing is ferocious.

Listen for: Jeff 'Tain' Watts plays with Tony Williams' elasticity but hits considerably harder. The Miles quintet is the model and the temperature is completely different.

core

Wynton Marsalis — Wynton Marsalis core

recorded 1981, released 1982 · Columbia FC 37574 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “RJ”

Wynton Marsalis (tp) · Branford Marsalis (ts) · Herbie Hancock (p) · Ron Carter (b) · Tony Williams (d) · Clarence Seay (b) · Jeff Watts (d)

A nineteen-year-old's debut with three-quarters of Miles Davis's second quintet as his rhythm section. The endorsement was the point, and it launched the entire Young Lions marketing apparatus.

Listen for: Hancock, Carter and Williams play exactly as they did in 1966, and the trumpet in front is the one thing that has changed. The lineage claim is made explicit by personnel.

Joshua Redman Quartet — MoodSwing core

The second Young Lions wave, and a band of four musicians who all became major figures. Mehldau and Blade in particular sound nothing like traditionalists here.

Listen for: Brian Blade's drumming is quieter and more elastic than the 1980s neo-bop model, and Mehldau's comping already refuses to state the harmony plainly.

Roy Hargrove's Crisol — Habana core

recorded/released 1997 · Verve 537 563-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “O My Seh Yeh”

Roy Hargrove (tp) · Chucho Valdés (p) · David Sánchez (ts) · Russell Malone (g) · Horacio 'El Negro' Hernández (d) · Miguel 'Angá' Díaz (congas)

Hargrove was the Young Lion least interested in policing boundaries, and this Cuban project won a Grammy while being a genuinely collaborative record rather than a tourist's.

Listen for: The Cuban rhythm section is not simplified for the American horns. Hargrove had to learn to phrase against clave and you can hear him doing it.

Terence Blanchard — The Malcolm X Jazz Suite core

recorded/released 1993 · Columbia CK 53599 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Fear” · medium confidence

Terence Blanchard (tp) · Sam Newsome (ts) · Bruce Barth (p) · Tarus Mateen (b) · Troy Davis (d)

Blanchard reworking his own film score into a jazz suite, which is where the neo-bop generation's compositional ambitions actually landed — in extended form and in film.

Listen for: The writing is programmatic and cinematic, using recurring themes across movements rather than head-solos-head. It is a different project from the quintet records.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Marcus Roberts — Deep in the Shed core

recorded 1989, released 1990 · Novus 3078-2-N · difficulty 2/5 · start with “The Governor”

Marcus Roberts (p) · Wynton Marsalis (tp) · Todd Williams (ts) · Wessell Anderson (as) · Reginald Veal (b) · Herlin Riley (d)

Roberts went furthest back of anyone in the movement, rebuilding stride and blues technique as working equipment. The compositions here are all blues-based and none of them are simple.

Listen for: Every piece is a blues in some form, and Roberts keeps finding structural variations — different lengths, different harmonic detours — inside that constraint.

deep

Wynton Marsalis — Blood on the Fields deep

recorded 1995, released 1997 · Columbia C3K 57694 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Calling the Indians Out”

Wynton Marsalis (tp, comp) · Jon Hendricks (voc) · Cassandra Wilson (voc) · Miles Griffith (voc) · Lincoln Center Jazz Orchestra

A three-hour oratorio about slavery for big band and three singers, and the first jazz work to win the Pulitzer Prize for Music, in 1997. Whatever you think of the movement, this is its most serious artefact.

Listen for: The writing moves through the whole history — field holler, New Orleans polyphony, Ellington, bebop — as narrative rather than as pastiche.

Lincoln Center Jazz Orchestra — Big Train deep

recorded 1998, released 1999 · Columbia CK 69860 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Union Pacific Big Boy” · medium confidence

Wynton Marsalis (tp, comp) · Wycliffe Gordon (tb) · Victor Goines (reeds) · Herlin Riley (d) · Marcus Printup (tp)

The repertory orchestra playing new material rather than the archive, which is the institution's strongest justification. It is also the clearest sample of what Jazz at Lincoln Center sounds like as a band.

Listen for: The train imagery is worked out through orchestration — brass imitating whistles, drums imitating wheels. It is an Ellington device applied by a student of Ellington.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Brad Mehldau Trio — The Art of the Trio, Volume Three: Songs deep

recorded/released 1998 · Warner Bros. 9 47051-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Song-Song”

Brad Mehldau (p) · Larry Grenadier (b) · Jorge Rossy (d)

The Young Lions generation's most harmonically searching pianist, playing standards, Radiohead and his own material with a contrapuntal left hand that has more to do with Brahms than with bebop.

Listen for: The left hand plays independent melodic lines rather than comping chords, so the trio often has three simultaneous voices with no one stating the harmony.

M-Base 1984–present

Improvisation over interlocking, cyclic rhythmic structures drawn from West African and diasporic music — jazz thinking in loops rather than choruses.

New York (Brooklyn), USA · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called Macro-Basic Array of Structured Extemporization

A group of young musicians in Brooklyn in the mid-1980s — Steve Coleman, Greg Osby, Cassandra Wilson, Geri Allen, Robin Eubanks, Graham Haynes, Marvin 'Smitty' Smith — worked out an approach that was neither neo-bop nor free. The rhythmic basis is nested cycles: several repeating patterns of different lengths running simultaneously, so the downbeat is a matter of which layer you follow. That idea comes from West African drumming traditions and their diasporic descendants, and it makes the music feel simultaneously funky and disorienting. Over it, the melodic language is angular, chromatic, and phrased against the grid rather than with it. Coleman coined the acronym, and he has consistently insisted that M-Base names a philosophy of creative growth and shared language rather than a style of music — a distinction most journalism has ignored. That correction matters: the players involved sound quite different from each other, and treating M-Base as a sound rather than a working method misrepresents its own self-description. The influence is enormous and mostly indirect — Vijay Iyer, Steve Lehman, Ambrose Akinmusire and much of what is now called contemporary creative music runs through it.

What to listen for

  • A groove that is clearly funky but that you cannot easily count
  • Several repeating patterns of different lengths running at once
  • Melodic lines that cut across the beat rather than sitting on it
  • Voice used as another horn, singing wordless lines in unison
  • A dry, clipped band sound with almost no reverb

How it works

harmony
Chromatic and non-functional, often built from symmetrical structures and pitch cells rather than chord progressions.
rhythm
The centre of the concept. Nested, interlocking cycles of unequal length, odd groupings, and a funk feel that keeps displacing.
form
Cyclic rather than chorus-based. Sections repeat at different rates and phase against one another.
instrumentation
Alto saxophone, trumpet, trombone, guitar, electric bass, drums; voice used as an instrumental line. Electric and acoustic freely mixed.
improvisation
Improvising across a shifting grid — the challenge is orientation as much as invention. Collective interaction is expected.
timbre
Dry, tight, unromantic. Little reverb, hard articulation, and a deliberately unlush ensemble sound.

Key figures

  • Steve Coleman (alto saxophone, composition) — Coined the term and has pursued the rhythmic research for forty years, including fieldwork in Ghana, Cuba and India.
  • Greg Osby (alto saxophone) — The other central altoist; later a Blue Note bandleader and a persistent critic of neo-traditionalist orthodoxy.
  • Cassandra Wilson (voice) — Came out of the collective and then built a singular body of work applying its rhythmic thinking to blues and folk repertoire.
  • Geri Allen (piano, composition) — Connected M-Base to the AACM lineage and to the mainstream, with a harmonic imagination distinct from anyone else in the group.
  • Robin Eubanks (trombone) — Made the trombone work in a rhythmically demanding idiom that gives it no natural place.
  • Marvin 'Smitty' Smith (drums) — Executed the interlocking-cycle concept on the kit and made it feel like a groove rather than an exercise.

Labels: JMT · Novus/RCA · Blue Note · Pi Recordings · Label Bleu

Contested

Whether M-Base is a genre at all. Steve Coleman has stated repeatedly that it is a concept and a way of working, not a style, and that critics who describe an 'M-Base sound' have misunderstood it. This guide includes it as a node because the influence is traceable and the rhythmic approach is identifiable — while noting that the person who named it would object to the framing.

Came out of

  • Post-Bop — direct descendant, 1984, via harmony, improvisation. The chromatic, non-functional melodic writing and the expectation that a rhythm section improvises collectively come from the post-bop world these players trained in. Steve Coleman worked in Sam Rivers' and Cecil Taylor's large ensembles before forming Five Elements.
  • Jazz-Funk — direct descendant, 1985, via rhythm, instrumentation. The rhythmic floor is funk — electric bass, backbeat, sixteenth-note subdivision — and the group sound is deliberately dry and unswung. What M-Base does to it is stack cycles of unequal length so the groove keeps displacing under you.
  • Free Funk & Harmolodics — direct descendant, 1985, via harmony, instrumentation, improvisation. Ornette Coleman's harmolodic groups had already established that free melodic playing and a hard electric groove could occupy the same bar, and that no instrument need be subordinate. Coleman and Steve Coleman recorded together, and the alto lineage is audible.
  • Afro-Cuban & Latin Jazz — parallel influence, 1993, via rhythm, social-context. Steve Coleman's rhythmic research took him to Ghana, Cuba, Brazil and India, and he recorded in Havana with AfroCuba de Matanzas in 1996. The nested-cycle model is drawn from West African and Afro-Caribbean drumming practice rather than derived from jazz.

Fed into

  • Contemporary Creative Jazz — direct descendant, 2000

What to hear

gateway

Steve Coleman and Five Elements — Motherland Pulse gateway

recorded/released 1985 · JMT JMT 850 001 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Motherland Pulse”

Steve Coleman (as) · Graham Haynes (cor) · Geri Allen (p) · Kelvyn Bell (g) · Lonnie Plaxico (b) · Marvin 'Smitty' Smith (d) · Cassandra Wilson (voc)

The first Five Elements record and the collective's founding document, with Geri Allen and Cassandra Wilson both present. The rhythmic concept is already fully in place.

Listen for: The groove is unmistakably funk and you cannot count it in four. Several patterns of different lengths are running at once and the downbeat keeps moving.

core

Steve Coleman and Five Elements — Black Science core

recorded 1990, released 1991 · Novus 3119-2-N · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Black Science”

Steve Coleman (as) · Robin Eubanks (tb) · David Gilmore (g) · Reggie Washington (b) · Marvin 'Smitty' Smith (d) · Kokayi (voc)

The concept at full strength, with rapping, hard funk and rhythmic structures that require the band to count in several ways at once.

Listen for: The horns phrase across the bar lines constantly, landing on beats that the bass line has already redefined. Nothing lines up where you expect it to.

Greg Osby — Season of Renewal core

recorded 1989, released 1990 · JMT JMT 834 435-2 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Season of Renewal” · medium confidence

Greg Osby (as, ss) · Michele Rosewoman (keys) · Kevin Bruce Harris (b) · Paul Samuels (d) · Cassandra Wilson (voc)

Osby's version of the concept is more song-shaped than Coleman's and more openly indebted to funk and go-go. It shows how differently two people could apply the same working method.

Listen for: The alto tone is drier and more nasal than Coleman's, and the lines are shorter and more fragmented. Same idea, different instrument.

Geri Allen — Twylight core

recorded/released 1989 · Minor Music MM 801 013 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Twylight” · medium confidence

Geri Allen (p, keys) · Robin Eubanks (tb) · Marcus Belgrave (tp) · Lonnie Plaxico (b) · Pheeroan akLaff (d)

Allen was the collective's most harmonically sophisticated member and the one most connected to the AACM lineage. Her writing here is denser and stranger than the group's usual funk framework.

Listen for: The piano voicings owe more to Herbie Nichols and Muhal Richard Abrams than to funk. She is applying the rhythmic concept to a very different harmonic language.

Steve Coleman and Five Elements — Rhythm People (The Resurrection of Creative Black Civilization) core

recorded/released 1990 · Novus 3092-2-N · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Little Girl I'll Miss You”

Steve Coleman (as) · Graham Haynes (cor) · David Gilmore (g) · Kevin Bruce Harris (b) · Marvin 'Smitty' Smith (d)

The record where the rhythmic project is stated most explicitly, title included. It is dense, dry and completely uninterested in charming anyone.

Listen for: The bass and drums are locked to different cycle lengths, so the pattern only realigns every several bars. Count one instrument at a time.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Robin Eubanks — Mental Images core

recorded/released 1994 · JMT JMT 514 013-2 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Mental Images” · medium confidence

Robin Eubanks (tb) · Steve Coleman (as) · Kevin Eubanks (g) · Michael Cain (keys) · Dave Holland (b) · Mark Mondesir (d)

Trombone-led M-Base, which is technically brutal — the instrument has no natural facility for this rhythmic language and Eubanks makes it work anyway.

Listen for: Trombone playing sixteenth-note lines across displaced cycles. The slide makes this far harder than it would be on a valved instrument.

deep

Steve Coleman and Five Elements — Curves of Life deep

recorded/released 1995 · RCA Victor 09026 68556-2 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Cross-Fade”

Steve Coleman (as) · Andy Milne (p) · David Gilmore (g) · Reggie Washington (b) · Gene Lake (d) · Kokayi (voc) · Sub-Zero (voc)

Recorded live in Paris across several nights, which is where this music makes most sense — the cycles have room to establish themselves and the band relaxes into them.

Listen for: Over ten minutes a pattern that seemed impossible to follow becomes obvious. Duration is part of how the rhythmic concept communicates.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Steve Coleman and Five Elements — The Sonic Language of Myth deep

recorded 1998, released 1999 · RCA Victor 09026 63316-2 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Precession”

Steve Coleman (as) · Ravi Coltrane (ts) · Andy Milne (p) · Anthony Tidd (b) · Sean Rickman (d)

Coleman's structural thinking applied to Egyptian and Greek mythological schemes, with the rhythmic cycles derived from astronomical and calendrical ratios. It is where the project becomes fully systematic.

Listen for: The cycle lengths are derived from numbers Coleman took from mythological sources. Whether you believe that matters, the resulting rhythms are unlike anyone else's.

Steve Coleman and the Mystic Rhythm Society with AfroCuba de Matanzas — The Sign and the Seal deep

recorded 1996, released 1997 · RCA Victor 09026 68807-2 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Yorubaland”

Steve Coleman (as) · AfroCuba de Matanzas (percussion, voc) · Ravi Coltrane (ts) · Anthony Tidd (b)

Recorded in Matanzas with a folkloric Cuban ensemble whose repertoire comes from Yoruba and Congolese religious practice. It is fieldwork and collaboration rather than a fusion record.

Listen for: The Cuban ensemble is not adapting to Coleman. He is finding places inside their cycles, which is exactly the relationship his whole rhythmic project describes.

Acid Jazz 1987–2004

DJs rediscovering 1970s jazz-funk on the dancefloor, then hiring bands to play new music that sounded like it.

London, UK · Electric & Crossover · also called Jazz dance, Rare groove revival

This one starts with record collecting, not with musicians. London club DJs in the mid-1980s — Gilles Peterson and Chris Bangs among them — were playing forgotten 1960s and 70s jazz-funk, soul jazz and Latin records to dancers, and the name 'acid jazz' began as a joke on acid house. From there it went two ways: reissues and compilations that resurrected a whole layer of neglected recordings, and new bands playing original material in that idiom — Brand New Heavies, Incognito, Jamiroquai, the James Taylor Quartet, Galliano — often with hip-hop production techniques, live rhythm sections and rap or soul vocals over the top. The Acid Jazz label was founded in 1987 by Peterson and Eddie Piller; Peterson later left to start Talkin' Loud. Musically it is more a curatorial position than a technique: the groove and the crate-digger's ear are the constants. Its importance is downstream — it built the UK infrastructure of clubs, labels and radio that later produced broken beat, the 2010s London jazz scene, and much of the audience for both.

What to listen for

  • Hammond organ and horn riffs that sound like 1971 but were recorded in 1993
  • A rock-steady club tempo designed for a DJ to mix in and out of
  • Rapping or soul singing over what is otherwise a jazz-funk band
  • Sampled drum breaks sitting alongside a live rhythm section
  • Very short solos, if any

How it works

harmony
Borrowed wholesale from soul jazz and jazz-funk — modal vamps, minor sevenths, gospel cadences. Rarely harmonically ambitious on its own terms.
rhythm
Danceable funk and boogaloo at club tempos, later incorporating breakbeats and programmed drums alongside live players.
form
Song and groove structures built for DJ use — long intros, breakdowns, clean beat-matchable sections.
instrumentation
Hammond organ, Rhodes, electric bass, horn section, turntables and samplers, plus soul and rap vocals.
improvisation
Short and functional. The idiom is about feel, not solo development.
timbre
Deliberately retro — analogue keyboards, warm bass, drum sounds modelled on 1970s records even when programmed.

Key figures

  • Gilles Peterson (DJ, label founder, broadcaster) — Co-named the movement, co-founded Acid Jazz then Talkin' Loud, and has kept building the UK's jazz infrastructure ever since.
  • Brand New Heavies (band) — The most successful of the live acid jazz bands and the closest to a functioning 1970s jazz-funk group.
  • Incognito (band) — Jean-Paul 'Bluey' Maunick's group provided the idiom's most durable songwriting.
  • James Taylor Quartet (band) — Hammond-driven and closest to the soul jazz source material, minus the improvisation.
  • Roy Ayers (vibraphone) — The most important living ancestor, adopted by the scene and actively recording with it.

Labels: Acid Jazz · Talkin' Loud · Blue Note (Blue Break Beats) · Dorado

Contested

Whether it is a genre or a DJ marketing category. Musicians in it frequently rejected the label. It also has an uneasy relationship with the music it revived — the acid jazz audience discovered soul jazz and jazz-funk via compilations that often stripped the original context away.

Came out of

  • Jazz-Funk — revival of, 1987, via rhythm, instrumentation, timbre. Acid jazz began as London DJs playing forgotten 1970s jazz-funk to dancers, and the bands that followed set out to make new records that sounded like the old ones — same instruments, same tempos, same production references, fifteen years later.
  • Soul Jazz & Organ Trio — revival of, 1988, via instrumentation, rhythm, repertoire. The Hammond organ, the boogaloo feel and a good deal of the actual repertoire came from 1960s soul jazz records that had been out of print for twenty years. The James Taylor Quartet's early sides are close to reconstruction.
  • Jazz Rap — parallel influence, 1992, via technology, rhythm. The two scenes were digging the same crates on opposite sides of the Atlantic. Sampling technique, breakbeat programming and rapping over live jazz bands crossed into acid jazz from hip-hop, most obviously in Galliano and Us3.

Fed into

  • Nu Jazz & Electronic Jazz — fusion of, 1996
  • UK Jazz Revival — direct descendant, 2012

What to hear

gateway

The Brand New Heavies — The Brand New Heavies gateway

recorded/released 1990 · Acid Jazz JAZID LP 013 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Never Stop”

Simon Bartholomew (g) · Andrew Levy (elb) · Jan Kincaid (d) · N'Dea Davenport (voc)

The scene's best band playing original material rather than pastiche, with a rhythm section tight enough to pass for 1973. It is the most listenable entry point to a movement that was often more about curation than composition.

Listen for: The drums and bass are recorded dry and up-front in the 1970s manner, with no 1990s reverb. The production is as much a period reference as the playing.

core

Incognito — Positivity core

recorded/released 1993 · Talkin' Loud 518 214-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Still a Friend of Mine”

Jean-Paul 'Bluey' Maunick (g, prod) · Maysa Leak (voc) · Peter Hinds (keys) · Richard Bull (d)

Bluey Maunick's writing is the most durable in the idiom — actual songs rather than grooves with a topline — and this is the record where the band's own material outgrew its influences.

Listen for: The horn arrangements and the layered backing vocals are doing the work of a much bigger band. It is a studio construction with live playing at its core.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Us3 — Hand on the Torch core

recorded 1992, released 1993 · Blue Note CDP 7 80883 2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Cantaloop (Flip Fantasia)”

Geoff Wilkinson (prod) · Mel Simpson (prod) · Rahsaan Kelly (MC) · Gerard Presencer (tp)

Blue Note gave a British production duo unrestricted access to its master tapes, and the result was the label's first platinum record. It is the clearest case of the sampled and the sampling generations sharing a contract.

Listen for: 'Cantaloop' is built on Herbie Hancock's 'Cantaloupe Island' from Empyrean Isles, with a live trumpet playing over the loop. Both records are in this guide.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

The James Taylor Quartet — Mission Impossible core

recorded 1986, released 1987 · Re-Elect the President TAYLOR 1 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Blow Up” · medium confidence

James Taylor (Hammond org) · David Taylor (elb) · Allan Crockford (g) · Simon Howard (d)

Hammond organ instrumentals covering TV themes, released before 'acid jazz' had a name. It is the scene's rawest and most literal reconstruction of 1960s soul jazz.

Listen for: There is essentially no improvisation. It is a groove band playing arrangements, which is what most of acid jazz actually was.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jamiroquai — The Return of the Space Cowboy core

recorded/released 1994 · Sony Soho Square 477813 2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Space Cowboy”

Jay Kay (voc) · Toby Smith (keys) · Stuart Zender (elb) · Wallis Buchanan (didgeridoo)

The most commercially enormous thing acid jazz produced, and the second album is where the band's playing caught up with its ambition. Stuart Zender's bass is the reason it holds up.

Listen for: The bass lines are genuinely difficult and carry the harmonic content. Strip the vocals and this is a jazz-funk instrumental band.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Galliano — A Joyful Noise Unto the Creator core

recorded/released 1992 · Talkin' Loud 512 197-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Prince of Peace” · medium confidence

Rob Gallagher (voc) · Constantine Weir (voc) · Crispin Taylor (d) · Ernie McKone (elb)

The first act signed to Talkin' Loud, combining spoken-word and rap with live jazz-funk and an explicitly spiritual bent. It is closer to the Last Poets than to a dance record.

Listen for: The rapping is British, unhurried and consciously not American in cadence — one of the earliest examples of a UK voice in this space.

deep

Various Artists — Rebirth of Cool Volume One deep

recorded/released 1991 · 4th & Broadway BRCD 573 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Various” · medium confidence

compiled by Gilles Peterson and Norman Jay

The compilation series that defined the sensibility, mixing hip-hop, jazz, dub and soul with no regard for genre boundaries. It shows acid jazz as a listening position rather than a sound.

Listen for: The sequencing is the argument. Records that had no relationship to each other are placed side by side until a common thread becomes audible.

Corduroy — Out of Here deep

recorded/released 1994 · Acid Jazz JAZID CD 90 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Mo' Chuvva” · low confidence

Ben Addison (d, voc) · Scott Addison (org, voc) · Simon Nelson-Smith (g) · Richard Searle (elb)

The mod end of the scene — Hammond-driven, sharp-suited, and closer to 1960s British R&B than to American soul jazz. It documents acid jazz as a British subculture rather than a revival.

Listen for: The organ sound and the arrangements come from Georgie Fame and Brian Auger rather than from Jimmy Smith. The lineage is local.

Note: Acid Jazz label catalogue numbering from this period is inconsistently documented; details should be verified.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Roy Ayers — Wake Up deep

recorded/released 1989 · Ichiban ICH 1064 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Wake Up” · low confidence

Roy Ayers (vib, voc, keys)

The living ancestor recording during the acid jazz years and being adopted by the scene that had rediscovered him. Ayers toured with these bands and the traffic was genuinely two-way.

Listen for: The production is late-1980s and the playing is unchanged from 1976. Hearing the mismatch clarifies what the revival was and was not reproducing.

Note: Ayers' late-1980s discography on small labels is patchily documented; verify before citing.

Jazz Rap 1988–present

Hip-hop producers building beats from jazz records, and then jazz musicians answering back.

New York, USA · Electric & Crossover · also called Hip-hop jazz, Jazzy hip-hop

It starts with sampling. Producers in the late 1980s went looking for breaks in their parents' jazz collections and found that upright bass, Rhodes and horn stabs sat beautifully under rapping. A Tribe Called Quest's The Low End Theory in 1991 is the pivot: sparse, bass-forward, built on jazz samples, with Ron Carter playing upright bass on one track. Gang Starr, Digable Planets, Pete Rock, Us3 and the Native Tongues collective make up the rest of the first wave. Then it goes both ways. Guru's Jazzmatazz in 1993 put live jazz musicians in the studio with a rapper; Miles Davis's last album, Doo-Bop, was a hip-hop collaboration; Branford Marsalis's Buckshot LeFonque and Steve Coleman's projects came from the jazz side. The lasting influence is rhythmic: the boom-bap feel — a heavy, slightly-behind, sampled-sounding backbeat — became a legitimate jazz groove, and by the 2010s drummers like Chris Dave, Karriem Riggins and Marcus Gilmore had absorbed it as a first language. That is the line running into the LA beat scene and the London revival.

What to listen for

  • A short loop of an old jazz record repeating under the whole track
  • Upright bass, which almost no other hip-hop uses
  • A drum feel that's slightly behind and slightly loose rather than machine-tight
  • Muted trumpet or Rhodes chords used as a hook
  • Vinyl crackle and filtering left audible in the mix

How it works

harmony
Sample-derived. Loops of jazz records supply the harmony wholesale, often two or four bars repeated with no development.
rhythm
Boom-bap — a hard, slightly loose backbeat around 85–95 BPM, with swung sixteenths and deliberate timing imperfections.
form
Verse–chorus rap structure over looped material, with the loop as the harmonic and formal constant.
instrumentation
Sampler and turntables first; upright bass, Rhodes, saxophone and trumpet added live in the second wave.
improvisation
Initially the rapping is the improvisation-descended element. Live solos appear from Jazzmatazz onward.
timbre
Warm, dusty, filtered — the sound of vinyl sampled at low bit rates is an aesthetic choice, not a limitation.

Key figures

  • A Tribe Called Quest (group) — The Low End Theory established the sparse, bass-forward jazz-sample aesthetic that everything else worked from.
  • Guru (MC) — Gang Starr's rapper and the Jazzmatazz series' architect — the first sustained attempt to put rappers and live jazz players in one room.
  • DJ Premier (production) — Gang Starr's producer and the definitive boom-bap sound designer; his chopping technique is a compositional method.
  • Pete Rock (production) — Built the lushest horn-sample production of the era, working from soul jazz and hard bop records.
  • Digable Planets (group) — Took the aesthetic furthest into explicit jazz reference, right down to the bebop-cool persona.
  • J Dilla (production) — His deliberately unquantised drum programming changed how a generation of jazz drummers thinks about time.

Labels: Jive · Chrysalis/EMI · Blue Note · Elektra · Pendulum

Contested

Whether it belongs in a jazz lineage or a hip-hop one. It is unambiguously hip-hop as music; its inclusion here is on the grounds of what it sent back — the boom-bap feel is now standard jazz drumming vocabulary. Filing it as a jazz genre risks the old habit of claiming Black popular music for jazz whenever it becomes respectable.

Came out of

  • Jazz-Funk — direct descendant, 1991, via technology, harmony, rhythm. The descent is physical: producers took the actual recorded material — two bars of Rhodes, a bass figure, a drum break — and looped it. Bob James, Donald Byrd and Roy Ayers records supply a disproportionate share of the harmony in early 1990s hip-hop.
  • Hard Bop — direct descendant, 1991, via harmony, timbre, repertoire. The other sample source is the Blue Note and Prestige catalogue: upright bass, muted trumpet and hard bop piano voicings give jazz rap the sonic signature that separates it from other hip-hop. Us3's 1993 record licensed Blue Note directly rather than clearing samples one at a time.

Fed into

  • Acid Jazz — parallel influence, 1992
  • LA Beat-Scene Jazz — direct descendant, 2008
  • Contemporary Creative Jazz — parallel influence, 2008

What to hear

gateway

A Tribe Called Quest — The Low End Theory gateway

recorded/released 1991 · Jive 1418-2-J · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Excursions”

Q-Tip (MC, prod) · Phife Dawg (MC) · Ali Shaheed Muhammad (DJ) · Ron Carter (b)

The record that proved jazz samples and rapping were not a novelty pairing. Sparse, bass-forward, and with Ron Carter playing upright on one track at Q-Tip's request.

Listen for: There is almost nothing in the mix — a bass line, drums, one sample, two voices. The restraint is what makes the low end sound so enormous.

Gang Starr — Daily Operation core

recorded/released 1992 · Chrysalis F2 21910 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Take It Personal”

Guru (MC) · DJ Premier (prod, DJ)

DJ Premier's chopping technique at its sharpest — samples cut into fragments and reassembled rather than looped whole. Guru's flat, unhurried delivery is a rhythmic instrument in itself.

Listen for: Premier takes half a second of a jazz record and repitches it across a bar. The source is often unrecognisable, which is the point: he is composing, not quoting.

Digable Planets — Reachin' (A New Refutation of Time and Space) core

recorded 1992, released 1993 · Pendulum 61414-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Rebirth of Slick (Cool Like Dat)”

Butterfly (MC, prod) · Ladybug Mecca (MC) · Doodlebug (MC)

The most explicitly jazz-referencing rap record of the era, down to the personas and the Art Blakey samples. It won a Grammy and made the aesthetic briefly mainstream.

Listen for: 'Rebirth of Slick' is built on a loop from Art Blakey's 'Stretchin''. The upright bass and the brushed drums are doing the same job they did in 1958.

Guru — Jazzmatazz, Volume 1 core

recorded 1992, released 1993 · Chrysalis F2 21998 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Loungin'”

Guru (MC) · Donald Byrd (tp) · Roy Ayers (vib) · Lonnie Liston Smith (keys) · Branford Marsalis (ts) · N'Dea Davenport (voc)

The first sustained attempt to put live jazz musicians and a rapper in a studio together rather than sampling them. Donald Byrd, Roy Ayers and Lonnie Liston Smith — the men whose records had been sampled — playing in person.

Listen for: The difference between a sampled loop and a live rhythm section is immediately audible, and not always in the live section's favour. That tension is the record's subject.

Pete Rock & CL Smooth — Mecca and the Soul Brother core

recorded/released 1992 · Elektra 60948-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “They Reminisce Over You (T.R.O.Y.)”

Pete Rock (prod, DJ) · CL Smooth (MC)

The richest horn-sample production of the era, built from soul jazz and hard bop records layered several deep. 'They Reminisce Over You' is an elegy built from a Tom Scott saxophone figure.

Listen for: Pete Rock stacks multiple horn samples so the result sounds like a section that never existed. It is arranging by other means.

Robert Glasper Experiment — Black Radio core

recorded 2011, released 2012 · Blue Note 509996 06777 2 3 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Afro Blue”

Robert Glasper (keys) · Casey Benjamin (as, vocoder) · Derrick Hodge (elb) · Chris Dave (d) · Erykah Badu (voc) · Bilal (voc)

A jazz pianist made an R&B and hip-hop record with a jazz band's interactivity, and it won a Grammy. It established the template that most of the current jazz-adjacent mainstream works from.

Listen for: Chris Dave's drumming is the whole argument — an acoustic kit playing with the timing displacement of a programmed Dilla beat, live.

deep

Madlib — Shades of Blue deep

recorded 2002, released 2003 · Blue Note 7243 5 39387 2 8 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Slim's Return”

Madlib (prod, multi-instruments)

Blue Note handed a producer its vaults for a second time, and Madlib both remixed the originals and re-recorded material himself playing every instrument. It is a producer's reading of a catalogue.

Listen for: Some tracks are remixes and some are Madlib playing all the parts under a false band name. Working out which is which is genuinely difficult.
