Miles Davis — Doo-Bop deep

recorded 1991, released 1992 · Warner Bros. 9 26938-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “The Doo-Bop Song”

Miles Davis (tp) · Easy Mo Bee (prod, MC) · J.R. (MC)

Davis's last album, unfinished at his death and completed by producer Easy Mo Bee from existing trumpet takes. It is not good, and it is historically unavoidable — the last thing he chose to chase was hip-hop.

Listen for: The trumpet playing is recognisably Davis and the production around it was assembled without him. The seam is audible and worth hearing.

The Roots — Do You Want More?!!!??! deep

recorded 1994, released 1995 · DGC DGCD-24708 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Proceed”

Black Thought (MC) · Questlove (d) · Hub (elb) · Scott Storch (keys) · Rahzel (beatbox) · Cassandra Wilson (voc)

A rap group with no samples at all, playing everything live, with Cassandra Wilson and Steve Coleman guesting. It inverts the whole jazz rap premise — instead of sampling jazz, they play it.

Listen for: Questlove's drumming reproduces the feel of a sampled loop on an acoustic kit, deliberately imperfect. This is where the technique that Chris Dave later refined begins.

Nu Jazz & Electronic Jazz 1995–present

Jazz improvisation over programmed electronic rhythm — the drum machine as bandmate rather than backing track.

London / Oslo / Cologne / Paris, UK / Norway / Germany / France · Electric & Crossover · also called Electro-jazz, Future jazz, Broken beat (adjacent)

Where acid jazz revived old records and jazz rap sampled them, nu jazz starts from electronic dance music and adds improvisers. The rhythmic base is drum and bass, house, broken beat or downtempo — programmed, quantised or deliberately un-quantised — and horns, keyboards and bass improvise across it. There were several parallel scenes. In Norway, Bugge Wesseltoft's New Conception of Jazz and Nils Petter Molvær's Khmer in 1997 put ECM-style trumpet lyricism over dub and electronica, and Jaga Jazzist built a large band around programming. In London, the broken beat scene around West London and labels like Talkin' Loud extended acid jazz's clubbing into more complex rhythmic territory. In Germany, Jazzanova and the Compost/Sonar Kollektiv orbit did it as production. St Germain in Paris put house beats under blues and jazz samples and sold two million records. The best of it treats the beat as a partner — something to play against — and the weakest of it is a lounge compilation. Its influence runs directly into the current London scene.

What to listen for

  • Programmed drums that are clearly electronic sitting under acoustic solos
  • Trumpet or saxophone soaked in delay and reverb, played sparsely
  • Deep sub-bass that no acoustic instrument could produce
  • A track that builds and drops like dance music rather than stating a head
  • Harmony that barely moves across five or six minutes

How it works

harmony
Modal and vamp-based, with a strong debt to ECM's floating consonance and to house music's static two-chord loops.
rhythm
Programmed — drum and bass at 170 BPM, house at 125, broken beat's displaced syncopations, downtempo at 90. Live drums often play with or against the grid.
form
Track-based rather than song-based: build, drop, breakdown. Solos happen inside a producer's arrangement.
instrumentation
Laptop, sampler and drum machine alongside trumpet, saxophone, Rhodes and double or electric bass.
improvisation
Real but constrained. The soloist works over a fixed grid and a fixed harmony, closer to a dub toaster's role than a bebop soloist's.
timbre
Processed heavily — delay and reverb on horns, filtered pads, sub-bass. Molvær's echo-drenched trumpet is the signature sound.

Key figures

  • Nils Petter Molvær (trumpet, production) — Khmer in 1997 defined the ECM-meets-electronica sound and remains the idiom's clearest statement.
  • Bugge Wesseltoft (keyboards, production) — New Conception of Jazz and the Jazzland label built the Norwegian scene as an ongoing project.
  • Jazzanova (production collective) — The Berlin production team that connected broken beat, Brazilian material and jazz into a coherent European sound.
  • St Germain (production) — Ludovic Navarre's Tourist sold to an audience far outside jazz and proved the format could carry a mass market.
  • Jaga Jazzist (band) — A large Norwegian ensemble writing for horns, strings and electronics as one machine.
  • Cinematic Orchestra (band) — Jason Swinscoe's group moved from sample-based production to a fully live ensemble without changing the aesthetic.

Labels: Jazzland · Ninja Tune · Compost · Sonar Kollektiv · Talkin' Loud · F Communications

Contested

The name. 'Nu jazz' was largely a retail category and few of the musicians used it. It has also been criticised as jazz's least improvisational branch, where the improviser is a texture inside someone else's production. Both charges are fair for a lot of the catalogue and unfair for the best of it.

Came out of

  • Acid Jazz — fusion of, 1996, via rhythm, technology, social-context. Acid jazz established the club as jazz's venue and the DJ as its curator; nu jazz kept both and replaced the retro live band with contemporary electronic production. Gilles Peterson's move from Acid Jazz to Talkin' Loud tracks the shift.
  • ECM & Nordic Chamber Jazz — fusion of, 1997, via timbre, harmony, improvisation. Nils Petter Molvær and Bugge Wesseltoft were ECM musicians, and Khmer is the label's reverb-drenched trumpet lyricism and static harmony placed over drum programming. The Nordic tone is what makes nu jazz sound like jazz at all.

Fed into

  • UK Jazz Revival — direct descendant, 2014

What to hear

gateway

Nils Petter Molvær — Khmer gateway

recorded 1996, released 1997 · ECM ECM 1560 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Khmer”

Nils Petter Molvær (tp, programming) · Eivind Aarset (g) · Rune Arnesen (d) · Ulf Holand (prod)

ECM's trumpet lyricism placed over drum programming and dub bass, and the record that made electronic jazz a European proposition rather than a novelty. It sold far beyond the label's usual reach.

Listen for: The trumpet is played sparsely and drenched in delay, so a single note becomes a texture. The electronics are not accompaniment — they are the composition.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

core

Bugge Wesseltoft — New Conception of Jazz core

recorded/released 1996 · Jazzland JAZZCD 001 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Existence”

Bugge Wesseltoft (keys, programming) · Anders Engen (d) · Ingebrigt Håker Flaten (b)

The record that named the Norwegian project and launched the Jazzland label. Wesseltoft treats the sequencer as a bandmate rather than a backing track, which is the whole difference.

Listen for: The programmed elements are deliberately allowed to drift and the live players adjust to them. Nothing is locked to a click.

St Germain — Tourist core

recorded 1999, released 2000 · Blue Note 7243 5 25114 2 0 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Rose Rouge”

Ludovic Navarre (prod) · Edouard Labor (ts, fl) · Pascal Ohsé (tp) · Alexandre Destrez (keys)

Deep house grooves with live jazz soloing and blues samples, released on Blue Note and selling over two million copies. It is the commercial peak of the whole idea.

Listen for: 'Rose Rouge' loops a Marlena Shaw vocal phrase over a house beat while a live trumpet solos across it. Three eras of Black American music, stacked.

The Cinematic Orchestra — Motion core

recorded/released 1999 · Ninja Tune ZENCD 45 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Burn Out”

Jason Swinscoe (prod) · T. Daniel Howard (d) · Phil France (b) · Tom Chant (ts)

Sampled and looped material with live improvisation on top, made for a label built on hip-hop production. It is the British answer to the Norwegian project, arrived at independently.

Listen for: The loops are treated as chord changes to solo over. The saxophone is doing exactly what a jazz soloist does, just over a producer's structure.

Jaga Jazzist — A Livingroom Hush core

recorded/released 2001 · Smalltown Supersound STS084 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Airborne”

Lars Horntveth (multi-instruments, comp) · Martin Horntveth (d) · Line Horntveth (tuba) · Andreas Mjøs (vib)

A ten-piece Norwegian band writing for horns, vibraphone, tuba and electronics as one integrated machine. It is the most compositionally ambitious thing nu jazz produced.

Listen for: The arrangements are intricate and largely written, with electronics woven into the ensemble rather than layered on top. This is a big band that grew up on Autechre.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Jazzanova — In Between core

recorded 2001, released 2002 · JCR/Sonar Kollektiv JCR 019-2 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “That Night”

Jazzanova (production collective) · Vikter Duplaix (voc) · Ursula Rucker (voc) · Clara Hill (voc)

The Berlin production team's first album, connecting broken beat, Brazilian material, soul vocals and jazz harmony into something coherent. It is the European production wing at its most assured.

Listen for: The rhythms are broken beat — displaced, syncopated, never quite settling — and the harmony underneath is straightforwardly jazz. That combination is the genre's core.

deep

Eivind Aarset — Électronique Noire deep

recorded 1997, released 1998 · Jazzland 537 933-2 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Dark Moisture”

Eivind Aarset (g, programming) · Nils Petter Molvær (tp) · Bugge Wesseltoft (keys) · Wetle Holte (d)

Molvær's guitarist as a leader, using the guitar as a sound-design instrument rather than a solo voice. It is more abstract than Khmer and better for it.

Listen for: There are almost no identifiable guitar sounds. Everything is processed into pads, textures and rhythmic pulses, and the improvisation happens at the level of timbre.

Dego McFarlane (prod) · Marc Mac (prod) · Ursula Rucker (voc) · Carol Crosby (voc)

Drum and bass producers making a double album with a string orchestra, jazz harmony and spoken word. It is the record where the London electronic scene stopped pretending it was not making jazz.

Listen for: The strings are arranged, not sampled, and the drum programming runs at 170 BPM underneath ballad-tempo melodies. Two time-feels at once.

Koop — Waltz for Koop deep

Oscar Simonsson (prod) · Magnus Zingmark (prod) · Cecilia Stalin (voc) · Yukimi Nagano (voc)

A Swedish duo who built tracks entirely from tiny samples of acoustic jazz records — a single note at a time — and reassembled them into new arrangements. The method is extreme and inaudible.

Listen for: It sounds like a live jazz band and almost none of it is. Every note was sampled separately from old records and sequenced.

Contemporary Creative Jazz 1998–present

The current avant-garde mainstream: rhythmically intricate, harmonically dense, conservatory-trained, and drawing on everything at once.

New York / Chicago / Berlin, USA / Germany · Avant-Garde & Composed · also called Modern creative, Post-genre jazz, Creative music (current)

This is where the M-Base rhythmic project, the AACM's compositional discipline, downtown's genre promiscuity and neo-bop's technical training all end up in the same room. The identifying features are rhythmic: odd meters treated as ordinary, metric modulation, polyrhythm as a structural device rather than an effect, and a comfort with hip-hop and electronic feels. Harmonically it draws on spectral and post-tonal composition as readily as on jazz. Vijay Iyer works with rhythmic ratios and cognitive research on rhythm; Steve Lehman applies spectral harmony derived from Tristan Murail; Mary Halvorson has built a guitar language out of pitch-bending and unexpected form; Tyshawn Sorey writes long-form composition that has largely left jazz behind while remaining in dialogue with it; Ambrose Akinmusire, Jason Moran, Craig Taborn, Matana Roberts and Henry Threadgill's later ensembles fill in the range. Institutional support — grants, Pulitzers, MacArthur fellowships, university positions — has replaced the club economy that supported earlier generations, which shapes the music in ways worth noticing: it is composed more and jammed less.

What to listen for

  • Meters that shift constantly and never settle into four
  • Written material so intricate it is hard to tell what was improvised
  • Harmony that sounds like contemporary classical music, not like jazz chords
  • Hip-hop-derived beats played by an acoustic drummer with total precision
  • Long album-scale structures rather than a set of tunes

How it works

harmony
Post-tonal, spectral and cluster-based alongside jazz extensions. Harmony is often generated by a system rather than inherited from song form.
rhythm
The defining domain — metric modulation, nested tuplets, polymeter, and grooves borrowed from hip-hop, West African and South Indian traditions.
form
Through-composed, modular, or generated by rules. Extended suites and album-length structures are common.
instrumentation
Standard jazz groups plus strings, electronics, laptop processing, and idiosyncratic fixed instrumentations built for a single band.
improvisation
Deeply integrated with composition — the line between written and improvised is often deliberately hard to hear.
timbre
Precisely engineered. Electronics and acoustic instruments blended rather than contrasted.

Key figures

  • Vijay Iyer (piano, composition) — Combines rhythmic research, M-Base's cyclic thinking and a physical piano attack; also the most articulate writer among the group.
  • Mary Halvorson (guitar, composition) — Built an immediately recognisable guitar vocabulary from pitch-shifting and lurching form, out of Anthony Braxton's tutelage.
  • Tyshawn Sorey (drums, composition) — Works across improvised music and contemporary composition without treating them as different jobs; won the Pulitzer in 2024.
  • Steve Lehman (alto saxophone, composition) — Imported spectral harmony into an improvising octet, a genuinely new harmonic resource for the music.
  • Jason Moran (piano, composition) — Connects stride and Fats Waller to hip-hop and conceptual art, often within one piece.
  • Ambrose Akinmusire (trumpet, composition) — Writes long-form records about American violence and race with a trumpet language full of cracks and half-valves.
  • Craig Taborn (piano, electronics) — Moves between acoustic trio, solo piano and modular electronics with the same structural mind.

Labels: Pi Recordings · ECM · Firehouse 12 · Intakt · Blue Note · International Anthem

Contested

Whether this names anything at all, or is just 'jazz now'. The category is a critics' and grant-committees' convenience for music too composed for neo-bop and too structured for free jazz. Several of the musicians in it reject 'jazz' as a description of their work outright — a position with a long history in this music, from Ellington to Nicholas Payton's #BAM argument.

Came out of

  • M-Base — direct descendant, 2000, via rhythm, harmony, improvisation. The rhythmic research is the direct inheritance: nested cycles, odd groupings and improvisation across a shifting grid are now standard equipment. Vijay Iyer, Steve Lehman and Dafnis Prieto all worked with or studied Steve Coleman's approach closely.
  • AACM & Great Black Music — direct descendant, 1998, via form, improvisation, instrumentation. Anthony Braxton taught at Wesleyan for over twenty years and Mary Halvorson, Taylor Ho Bynum and others came directly out of that. The habit of designing a bespoke ensemble and a bespoke structure for each project is the AACM's, not the club scene's.
  • Downtown & Punk Jazz — direct descendant, 2000, via form, instrumentation, technology. Genre as material to be cut and juxtaposed, structure imposed by rule rather than by song form, and a label-and-venue economy built by musicians — all carried forward. Tzadik and the Stone remained the connective infrastructure into the 2010s.
  • Neo-Bop & the Young Lions — parallel influence, 2002, via improvisation, social-context. An uncomfortable inheritance: the conservatory programmes, festival circuits and funding structures the Young Lions built are what train and support this generation, even where the music rejects neo-traditionalist premises entirely. The technical fluency is a direct product of that institutionalisation.
  • ECM & Nordic Chamber Jazz — parallel influence, 2005, via timbre, form. ECM became a principal outlet for this generation — Iyer, Sorey, Halvorson and Moran have all recorded for it — and the label's premium on space, dynamic range and composed structure shapes what the records sound like.
  • Jazz Rap — parallel influence, 2008, via rhythm, technology. The boom-bap feel entered acoustic jazz drumming as a first language rather than a borrowing, and producers' techniques — looping, layering, sound design — became compositional tools for musicians who never used a sampler.

What to hear

gateway

Vijay Iyer Trio — Historicity gateway

recorded/released 2009 · ACT ACT 9489-2 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Historicity”

Vijay Iyer (p) · Stephan Crump (b) · Marcus Gilmore (d)

A piano trio playing M.I.A., Stevie Wonder, Andrew Hill and Ronnie Foster with rhythmic thinking derived from South Indian and West African practice. It made contemporary creative music briefly commercially visible.

Listen for: The trio keeps subdividing the beat into groupings that do not fit the meter, then resolving them. Marcus Gilmore's hi-hat is usually where the real time is.

core

Steve Lehman Octet — Travail, Transformation and Flow core

recorded/released 2009 · Pi Recordings PI33 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Echoes”

Steve Lehman (as) · Jonathan Finlayson (tp) · Tim Albright (tb) · Chris Dingman (vib) · Jose Davila (tuba) · Drew Gress (b) · Tyshawn Sorey (d)

Spectral harmony — derived from analysing the overtone content of sounds, a technique from French contemporary composition — applied to an improvising octet. It is a genuinely new harmonic resource for jazz.

Listen for: The vibraphone and tuba voicings produce chords that sound metallic and slightly out of tune. They are tuned to overtone relationships rather than to equal temperament.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Mary Halvorson Septet — Illusionary Sea core

recorded/released 2013 · Firehouse 12 FH12-04-01-018 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Smiles of Great Men”

Mary Halvorson (g) · Jonathan Finlayson (tp) · Jon Irabagon (as) · Ingrid Laubrock (ts) · Jacob Garchik (tb) · John Hébert (b) · Ches Smith (d)

Halvorson's writing for larger groups, with a guitar language built from pitch-bending and delay that is instantly identifiable and has no obvious ancestor.

Listen for: She uses a pedal to drop the pitch of individual notes mid-decay, so lines sag and warp. It sounds like tape damage and it is entirely deliberate.

Jason Moran — Black Stars core

recorded/released 2001 · Blue Note 7243 5 32922 2 5 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Out Front”

Jason Moran (p) · Sam Rivers (ts, ss, fl) · Tarus Mateen (b) · Nasheet Waits (d)

Moran's trio with Sam Rivers, then in his late seventies, as guest — the loft generation and the current one in the same room. Moran's stride references sit alongside completely free playing.

Listen for: Moran will drop into a Fats Waller left hand in the middle of an abstract passage. He treats the piano's entire history as available at any moment.

Tyshawn Sorey — Verisimilitude core

recorded 2016, released 2017 · Pi Recordings PI68 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Contemplating Tranquility”

Tyshawn Sorey (d, percussion) · Cory Smythe (p) · Chris Tordini (b)

A trio record where almost nothing happens quickly and the dynamic range runs from silence to barely audible. Sorey works between improvised music and contemporary composition without treating them as separate jobs.

Listen for: The patience is the point. Events are separated by long stretches of near-silence, and the tension comes from waiting rather than from density.

recorded 2013, released 2014 · Blue Note 509990 27871 2 8 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Our Basement (Ed)”

Ambrose Akinmusire (tp) · Walter Smith III (ts) · Sam Harris (p) · Harish Raghavan (b) · Justin Brown (d) · OSSO String Quartet · Becca Stevens (voc)

A long-form album with a string quartet, three vocalists and a piece named after Trayvon Martin's shooting. It is contemporary jazz taking on subject matter directly rather than through implication.

Craig Taborn (p)

Solo piano improvisation with the structural discipline of composition and none of the romanticism ECM solo piano usually carries. Taborn builds each piece from a single cell.

Listen for: Most tracks start with one small figure and expand outward without ever introducing unrelated material. It is improvisation working like a compositional process.

The first instalment of a projected twelve-part work tracing Roberts' own ancestry through American slavery, using AACM structural methods, spoken narrative and a large ensemble.

Henry Threadgill Zooid — In for a Penny, In for a Pound deep

recorded 2014, released 2015 · Pi Recordings PI60 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “In for a Penny, In for a Pound (Opening)”

Henry Threadgill (as, fl, bass fl) · Liberty Ellman (g) · Jose Davila (tb, tuba) · Christopher Hoffman (cello) · Elliot Humberto Kavee (d)

The record that won the 2016 Pulitzer Prize for Music, written for a band with tuba, cello and acoustic guitar and using a harmonic system Threadgill devised himself.

Listen for: Threadgill assigns each player a set of intervals rather than chords, so the harmony is generated by what they choose within those sets. Nothing is stated vertically.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

LA Beat-Scene Jazz 2008–present

Jazz musicians who grew up on J Dilla and Flying Lotus, playing with a producer's ear and a beatmaker's sense of time.

Los Angeles, USA · Electric & Crossover · also called Brainfeeder jazz, West Coast Get Down, Beat music

Two Los Angeles streams converged. One was the beat scene around Low End Theory, the club night that ran from 2006, and Flying Lotus's Brainfeeder label — instrumental hip-hop built on sample manipulation, sub-bass and deliberately wonky, off-grid timing inherited from J Dilla. The other was the West Coast Get Down, a group of musicians who had come up together in Los Angeles schools and played together for a decade before anyone noticed: Kamasi Washington, Thundercat, Terrace Martin, Ronald Bruner Jr., Cameron Graves, Miles Mosley. Thundercat is the physical link, playing bass on Brainfeeder records and in jazz contexts simultaneously. Kendrick Lamar's To Pimp a Butterfly in 2015 put nearly all of them on a mainstream rap record; Washington's three-hour The Epic the same year presented the jazz side with choir, strings and unembarrassed spiritual-jazz ambition. The music combines Coltrane-quartet modal intensity, 1970s spiritual jazz orchestration, and rhythm sections that think in terms of feel and pocket rather than swing.

What to listen for

  • A beat that sounds slightly drunk — snare landing late — but never actually loses time
  • Sub-bass and synthesizers sitting under acoustic saxophone and drums
  • Choir or massed vocals used as an orchestral section
  • Long modal solos that build toward ecstasy in the Coltrane manner
  • Production values from hip-hop: everything loud, wide and compressed

How it works

harmony
Modal and pentatonic, McCoy Tyner-derived, over pedal points, with gospel and soul progressions and lush orchestral or choral layering.
rhythm
Dilla-descended: deliberately off-grid, with the snare pushed or dragged so the beat feels drunk but stays locked. Hip-hop tempos alongside fast swing.
form
Long-form and suite-like on the jazz side; loop-and-transform on the beat side. Both prefer accumulation to development.
instrumentation
Six-string electric bass, synthesizers, saxophone, keys, two drummers on occasion, plus choir and strings.
improvisation
Extended and intensity-driven, closer to spiritual jazz than to post-bop. Solos are long and go for transcendence rather than cleverness.
timbre
Producer-shaped: heavy sub-bass, saturated synths, vocal layering, and a mix aesthetic drawn from hip-hop rather than jazz engineering.

Key figures

  • Kamasi Washington (tenor saxophone, composition) — The Epic put large-scale spiritual jazz in front of an audience that had not been listening to jazz.
  • Thundercat (electric bass, vocals) — The scene's connective tissue, moving between Brainfeeder, Kendrick Lamar and jazz contexts as one practice.
  • Flying Lotus (production) — Founded Brainfeeder and built the beat aesthetic; also Alice Coltrane's great-nephew, which is not incidental to the music.
  • Terrace Martin (alto saxophone, keyboards, production) — The producer-musician who brokered the jazz players onto To Pimp a Butterfly.
  • Robert Glasper (piano, production) — Not LA-based, but Black Radio in 2012 established the template of jazz pianist as R&B and hip-hop bandleader.
  • Chris Dave (drums) — The drummer who most fully translated Dilla's programmed feel back onto an acoustic kit.

Labels: Brainfeeder · Young Turks/XL · Blue Note · Stones Throw

Contested

Whether it is a genre or a network. Kamasi Washington's large-ensemble spiritual jazz and Flying Lotus's electronic production have little in common technically; what links them is a city, a label, and a set of collaborations. Some critics argue the 'jazz renaissance' framing around 2015 oversold a scene that had been working for a decade already.

Came out of

  • Jazz Rap — direct descendant, 2008, via rhythm, technology, harmony. J Dilla's deliberately unquantised drum programming is the LA beat scene's founding grammar, and Flying Lotus built Brainfeeder on it. The musicians who then played acoustically had internalised that feel as their natural time.
  • Spiritual Jazz — revival of, 2015, via form, harmony, social-context. Kamasi Washington's three-hour record with choir and strings is 1970s spiritual jazz reconstructed without irony, and Flying Lotus is Alice Coltrane's great-nephew, which is a lineage rather than a coincidence. The Impulse! and Strata-East aesthetic returns intact.
  • Jazz Fusion — direct descendant, 2013, via instrumentation, technology, harmony. Thundercat's six-string electric bass, his chord-melody approach and his falsetto singing descend from Stanley Clarke, Jaco Pastorius and George Duke directly, and he has said so. The synthesizer palette is 1970s fusion's, reactivated.

Fed into

  • UK Jazz Revival — parallel influence, 2016

What to hear

gateway

Kamasi Washington — The Epic gateway

recorded 2011, released 2015 · Brainfeeder BF050 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Change of the Guard”

Kamasi Washington (ts) · Cameron Graves (p) · Miles Mosley (b) · Thundercat (elb) · Ronald Bruner Jr. (d) · Tony Austin (d) · Patrice Quinn (voc) · 32-piece orchestra · 20-voice choir

Three hours, seventeen tracks, choir and strings, released on an electronic label and reaching an audience that had not been listening to jazz. The 1970s spiritual jazz ambition returns without a hint of irony.

Listen for: Two drummers play throughout, panned left and right, playing different parts. The choir functions as an orchestral section, not as a garnish.

Flying Lotus (prod) · Thundercat (elb) · Ravi Coltrane (ts) · Thom Yorke (voc) · Rebekah Raff (harp)

The record where the beat scene reached explicitly for jazz — harp, Ravi Coltrane on saxophone, and a structure closer to a suite than to a beat tape. Flying Lotus is Alice Coltrane's great-nephew and the reference is deliberate.

Listen for: The harp is a direct citation of Alice Coltrane, and the way tracks bleed into one another without pause is a suite structure imported from 1970s Impulse! records.

Twenty-three tracks, most under two minutes, with bass playing of real virtuosity underneath deadpan songs about cats and video games. The fusion lineage is worn openly, including hiring Michael McDonald.

Listen for: The bass playing is technically extraordinary and mixed like a pop record. Stanley Clarke and Jaco Pastorius are the ancestors and Thundercat has said so directly.

A mainstream rap album made with a live jazz band, and the record that put the whole West Coast Get Down in front of a global audience. It is where the scene became visible.

Listen for: 'For Free?' is a jazz interlude at bebop tempo with rapping over the top. The band is playing changes and Lamar is phrasing against them.

The producer-musician who assembled the To Pimp a Butterfly sessions making his own record, which is more explicitly jazz than anything he produced for others.

Listen for: The vocoder is used as a horn section, layered in harmony. It is a G-funk device deployed with jazz voicing.

Makaya McCraven — Universal Beings core

Makaya McCraven (d, prod) · Jeff Parker (g) · Shabaka Hutchings (ts) · Tomeka Reid (cello) · Brandee Younger (harp) · Nubya Garcia (ts)

McCraven records live improvisation and then edits it into composed tracks in the manner of a hip-hop producer. Four sessions in four cities, including London with Shabaka Hutchings and Nubya Garcia.

Listen for: The tracks are cut from long improvisations, so a groove will be interrupted and resumed in a way live playing never does. The edit is the composition.

deep

Miguel Atwood-Ferguson & Carlos Niño — Suite for Ma Dukes: The Music of James Yancey deep

J Dilla's beats orchestrated for a forty-piece ensemble, which forced someone to work out what the harmony in those tracks actually was. It is the clearest evidence that the beats were compositions.

Listen for: The orchestration reveals harmonic movement that the original short loops only implied. Dilla's chord choices turn out to be far from simple.

Listen for: Parker samples himself, loops it, and then plays over the loop. The 'band' on much of this is one person assembled in layers.
