Kamaal Williams (keys) · Pete Martin (elb) · MckNasty (d)

The London counterpart to the LA scene, built on broken beat and jazz-funk keyboards rather than on spiritual jazz. It shows the two cities working the same territory from different directions.

Listen for: The drums have the same off-grid feel as the LA records but derive it from UK garage and broken beat rather than from Dilla directly.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

UK Jazz Revival 2012–present

London jazz built on Caribbean, West African and soundsystem rhythm, made by musicians who came up together and play for dancers.

London, UK · Global Currents · also called New London jazz, The London scene, We Out Here generation

The most striking thing about the current London scene is the rhythm, and where it comes from. These musicians grew up with grime, jungle, Afrobeats, dub, calypso and broken beat as native languages, and the jazz they make is organised around those feels rather than around swing. Sons of Kemet built a band from tuba, two drummers and Shabaka Hutchings' saxophone, playing what amounts to a Caribbean carnival band's rhythmic logic. Ezra Collective run Afrobeat and reggae under jazz soloing. Nubya Garcia, Moses Boyd, Theon Cross, Emma-Jean Thackray and Yussef Dayes fill out the range. The infrastructure is as important as the music: Tomorrow's Warriors, the free youth programme co-founded in 1991 by bassist Gary Crosby and Janine Irons, trained a large proportion of the scene and did so with an explicit commitment to reaching Black and female musicians who the conservatory route was not reaching. Gilles Peterson's Brownswood released the We Out Here compilation in 2018, which is when the rest of the world noticed. The audience is young, and it dances, which has not been true of jazz in Britain for a very long time.

What to listen for

  • Tuba playing the bass line instead of a double bass or electric bass
  • Two drummers, playing complementary rather than identical patterns
  • Grooves that come from Afrobeat, dub or jungle rather than from swing
  • Dub-style echo thrown across horns and drums
  • Music clearly made for a crowd that is standing up

How it works

harmony
Modal, vamp-based and often minor-key, with spiritual jazz's pedal points and Afrobeat's two-chord frames. Harmonic complexity is not the priority.
rhythm
The centre of everything: Afrobeat, dub, jungle, grime, calypso and second-line feels, frequently with two drummers or a drummer plus percussion.
form
Groove-driven and often long, with slow-building structures aimed at a live, standing audience rather than a seated one.
instrumentation
Tuba as a bass instrument, tenor and soprano saxophone, keys, twin drum kits, electronics, and MCs or singers as guests.
improvisation
Present and extended, but subordinate to the groove — solos build collective intensity rather than displaying harmonic navigation.
timbre
Soundsystem-informed: heavy low end, dub-style delay and reverb, and mixes made for large speakers.

Key figures

  • Shabaka Hutchings (tenor saxophone, clarinet, flutes) — The scene's most visible figure, running Sons of Kemet, The Comet Is Coming and Shabaka and the Ancestors simultaneously, then abandoning the saxophone for flutes in 2023.
  • Nubya Garcia (tenor saxophone) — The clearest link back to spiritual jazz and to Caribbean rhythm, with a big, unhurried tone.
  • Moses Boyd (drums, production) — Works as both jazz drummer and electronic producer, which is exactly the scene's premise.
  • Theon Cross (tuba) — Made the tuba a lead bass instrument capable of sub-bass weight and dancehall articulation.
  • Gary Crosby (bass, education) — Co-founded Tomorrow's Warriors in 1991; the scene's existence is substantially attributable to that programme.
  • Ezra Collective (band) — Won the Mercury Prize in 2023, the first jazz act to do so, playing Afrobeat and reggae-driven jazz.

Labels: Brownswood · Gearbox · Impulse! · Native Rebel · Concord Jazz · Total Refreshment Centre (studio/scene hub)

Contested

Whether 'revival' is the right word, since British jazz did not stop — the Jazz Warriors in the 1980s, Courtney Pine, and a continuous free improvisation scene were all active. Critics also note the media framing conflated a genuinely diverse set of bands into one marketable story. Several of the musicians have said they do not consider themselves a scene.

Came out of

  • Acid Jazz — direct descendant, 2012, via social-context, rhythm, technology. The infrastructure is inherited whole: Gilles Peterson's radio show and Brownswood label, a club culture that treats jazz as dance music, and an audience trained by twenty-five years of crate-digging. We Out Here in 2018 came out on Peterson's label.
  • Spiritual Jazz — revival of, 2016, via harmony, form, social-context. Modal vamps, pedal points, long ecstatic builds and an explicitly political and spiritual framing are taken up directly — Nubya Garcia and Shabaka Hutchings both cite the Impulse! and Strata-East catalogue. Shabaka and the Ancestors is the most literal continuation.
  • Free Jazz — direct descendant, 2016, via improvisation, timbre. Sons of Kemet and The Comet Is Coming both use collective free playing and overblown saxophone as climactic devices, and the London scene overlaps with a continuous British free improvisation tradition that never went away.
  • Nu Jazz & Electronic Jazz — direct descendant, 2014, via technology, rhythm. Broken beat and its West London scene are the immediate rhythmic ancestor, and several of the current players — Moses Boyd above all — work as electronic producers as readily as instrumentalists.
  • LA Beat-Scene Jazz — parallel influence, 2016, via rhythm, technology, social-context. The two scenes emerged in parallel and recognised each other quickly, sharing bills, remixes and a shared debt to Dilla-derived timing. Neither is a parent of the other; the traffic is lateral and continuous.
  • Ethio-Jazz — parallel influence, 2016, via harmony, rhythm. Interpretation: the Éthiopiques reissues became common currency in London crate-digging culture, and Mulatu Astatke recorded with the UK's Heliocentrics in 2009. Pentatonic modal vamps over a loping groove are a recognisable ingredient in the current scene, though rarely acknowledged as a specific source.
  • J-Jazz — parallel influence, 2018, via timbre, form. Interpretation, and a weak claim: the BBE J Jazz reissue series from 2018 landed in the same London DJ and record-shop ecosystem that supports the current scene, and the deep-modal, long-form Japanese records are part of its listening. Direct musical influence is unproven.

What to hear

gateway

Various Artists — We Out Here gateway

recorded 2017, released 2018 · Brownswood BWOOD0180 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Black Skin, Black Masks (Theon Cross)”

Shabaka Hutchings (curator, ts) · Nubya Garcia (ts) · Moses Boyd (d) · Theon Cross (tuba) · Ezra Collective · Joe Armon-Jones (keys)

Nine tracks recorded in three days with a shared pool of musicians, assembled by Shabaka Hutchings for Gilles Peterson's label. It is the scene introducing itself, deliberately, as a scene.

Listen for: The same players recur across tracks in different combinations. That overlap is the point — this is a community with a shared rhythmic language, not a compilation of unrelated acts.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

core

Sons of Kemet — Your Queen Is a Reptile core

recorded 2017, released 2018 · Impulse! 00602567289654 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “My Queen Is Ada Eastman”

Shabaka Hutchings (ts, cl) · Theon Cross (tuba) · Tom Skinner (d) · Eddie Hick (d) · Congo Natty (voc)

Tuba, two drummers and a saxophone, with every track named for a Black woman the band offers as an alternative monarch. The politics are stated in the titles and carried in the rhythm.

Listen for: Theon Cross's tuba is playing bass lines with dancehall articulation and sub-bass weight. Two drummers play complementary Caribbean patterns rather than doubling each other.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Nubya Garcia — Source core

recorded 2019, released 2020 · Concord Jazz CJA00505 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Source”

Nubya Garcia (ts, fl) · Joe Armon-Jones (keys) · Daniel Casimir (b) · Sam Jones (d) · La Perla (voc)

Garcia's debut album for a major label, drawing on her Guyanese and Trinidadian background, dub, cumbia and spiritual jazz without any of it feeling like a checklist.

Listen for: The title track is over twelve minutes and built on a dub structure — heavy delay, dropouts, and a bass line that anchors everything while the horn floats.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Ezra Collective — Where I'm Meant to Be core

recorded/released 2022 · Partisan PTKF2249 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Life Goes On”

Femi Koleoso (d) · TJ Koleoso (elb) · Joe Armon-Jones (keys) · James Mollison (ts) · Ife Ogunjobi (tp) · Sampa the Great (voc)

The record that won the Mercury Prize in 2023, the first jazz album to do so. Afrobeat and reggae rhythms under jazz soloing, made explicitly for people to dance to.

Listen for: Femi Koleoso's drumming is Afrobeat-derived — the kick and the hi-hat carry the groove and the snare is used sparingly. It is not a jazz drummer playing Afrobeat.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Moses Boyd — Dark Matter core

recorded 2019, released 2020 · Exodus EXODUS001 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Stranger Than Fiction”

Moses Boyd (d, prod) · Theon Cross (tuba) · Nonku Phiri (voc) · Obongjayar (voc) · Joe Armon-Jones (keys)

Boyd works as a producer and a drummer simultaneously, and this record refuses to choose — grime, Afrobeat, jazz and electronic production, with the drums as the connecting logic.

Listen for: 'Stranger Than Fiction' has an acoustic kit and programmed elements interlocking so tightly that separating them is difficult. That integration is the scene's technical signature.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

The Comet Is Coming — Trust in the Lifeforce of the Deep Mystery core

recorded 2018, released 2019 · Impulse! 00602567981633 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Summon the Fire”

Shabaka Hutchings (ts) · Danalogue (synths) · Betamax (d) · Kate Tempest (voc)

Saxophone, analogue synthesizers and drums playing what amounts to psychedelic dance music, signed to Impulse! — the label that released Ascension. The lineage is claimed openly.

Listen for: The synthesizers do what an organ would have done in 1970 spiritual jazz, and the saxophone builds by overblowing rather than by running changes.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

deep

Theon Cross — Fyah deep

recorded 2018, released 2019 · Gearbox GB1546 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Activate”

Theon Cross (tuba) · Nubya Garcia (ts) · Moses Boyd (d)

A tuba-led trio record, which should not work and does. Cross treats the instrument as a bass, a rhythm section and a lead voice, with dancehall and grime phrasing.

Listen for: The tuba is amplified and processed to produce sub-bass frequencies a brass instrument does not naturally have. That is a soundsystem decision, not a jazz one.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Emma-Jean Thackray — Yellow deep

recorded 2020, released 2021 · Movementt MOVE003 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Say Something”

Emma-Jean Thackray (tp, voc, keys, prod) · Lyle Barton (keys) · Dougal Taylor (d) · Soweto Kinch (as)

Written, played, produced and mixed largely by one person, drawing on Alice Coltrane, P-Funk and brass band tradition. It is the scene's most complete single-authored statement.

Listen for: Thackray comes from a Yorkshire brass band background and the horn voicings show it — thick, hymn-like block harmony under a funk rhythm section.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Yussef Kamaal — Black Focus deep

recorded/released 2016 · Brownswood BWOOD0157 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Strings of Light”

Yussef Dayes (d) · Kamaal Williams (keys) · Tom Driessler (b) · Mansur Brown (g)

The record that connected the London scene to broken beat and West London's electronic history most directly. The duo split shortly afterwards, which makes it a single complete statement.

Listen for: Yussef Dayes' drumming is a live drum-and-bass and broken beat feel executed on an acoustic kit at extreme speed, with the hi-hat carrying most of the information.

Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Listening paths

Start Here 12 records

Twelve records, chronological, nothing difficult

One easy record from each stage of the music, in order. Nothing here is demanding; the point is to hear the ground shifting between 1921 and today without anyone shouting at you.

  1. Begin with one man and a piano, because that is where the machinery is most visible: a marching left hand and a right hand refusing to land with it.

    James P. Johnson — Carolina Shout gateway

    recorded 1921, released 1991 · Biograph BCD 105 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Carolina Shout”

    James P. Johnson (p)

    The founding stride record and the test piece every Harlem pianist had to learn — Ellington taught himself by slowing down Johnson's piano roll and copying the fingering off the keys.

    Listen for: The left hand jumps a tenth or more between the bass note and the chord, on every beat, at speed. That leap is the style's name and its physical difficulty.

    Note: 'Carolina Shout' was cut as a piano roll in 1918 and as a disc in October 1921; Biograph collections gather both.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  2. Johnson had to be his own band; Armstrong had one, and then discovered he did not need it either — this is where the soloist takes over jazz.

    Louis Armstrong — The Complete Hot Five and Hot Seven Recordings gateway

    recorded 1925, released 2000 · Columbia/Legacy C4K 63527 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “West End Blues”

    Louis Armstrong (cor, tp, voc) · Kid Ory (tb) · Johnny Dodds (cl) · Lil Hardin Armstrong (p) · Johnny St. Cyr (bj)

    The single most consequential body of recordings in jazz. Across 1925–28 you can hear the music stop being an ensemble art and become a soloist's art, in real time, in one man's playing.

    Listen for: On 'West End Blues' the unaccompanied opening cadenza announces that a soloist can now command a record on his own. Nothing before 1928 sounds like that.

    Note: Recorded 1925–1928; the Columbia/Legacy four-CD set collecting them was issued in 2000. Earlier equivalent collections exist.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  3. Armstrong showed that a singer could phrase like a horn; Holiday shows what happens when someone takes that permission and uses it on the words.

    Billie Holiday — The Quintessential Billie Holiday, Volume 3 (1936–1937) gateway

    recorded 1936, released 1987 · Columbia CK 44048 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “This Year's Kisses”

    Billie Holiday (voc) · Lester Young (ts) · Teddy Wilson (p) · Buck Clayton (tp) · Freddie Green (g) · Jo Jones (d)

    The Teddy Wilson small-group sides are the most perfect singer-and-band records in jazz. Holiday and Lester Young answer each other phrase by phrase, and the material is often disposable pop that they simply transform.

    Listen for: Follow the conversation between voice and tenor. Young plays around her line rather than under it, and neither of them ever raises their voice.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  4. Same decade, opposite scale: instead of five players listening to each other, fifteen executing a composer's design, with the improvising rationed.

    Duke Ellington — The Blanton–Webster Band gateway

    recorded 1940, released 1986 · RCA Bluebird 5659-2-RB · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Ko-Ko”

    Duke Ellington (p, comp) · Billy Strayhorn (comp, arr) · Jimmy Blanton (b) · Ben Webster (ts) · Johnny Hodges (as) · Cootie Williams (tp) · Tricky Sam Nanton (tb)

    The 1940–42 band is the peak of orchestral jazz writing. Ellington had Strayhorn, a bassist who reinvented the instrument's role, and a saxophone section he had been writing for individually for fifteen years.

    Listen for: 'Ko-Ko' is built almost entirely from a minor blues and short repeated figures, and it is genuinely dark — closer to Bartók's sense of orchestration than to dance music.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  5. The big bands have collapsed and the small group is back, but now it plays too fast to dance to and the harmony moves twice as often.

    Clifford Brown & Max Roach — Clifford Brown and Max Roach core

    recorded 1954, released 1955 · EmArcy MG 36036 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Joy Spring”

    Clifford Brown (tp) · Max Roach (d) · Harold Land (ts) · Richie Powell (p) · George Morrow (b)

    The quintet that connects bebop to hard bop, with the most fluent trumpet playing anyone had heard. Brown died in a car crash in June 1956 at twenty-five.

    Listen for: Brown's tone never breaks, at any speed or register. Compare the strain audible in most bebop trumpet — this is why every trumpeter after him tried to sound like this.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  6. Bebop had become a specialist's music; this is the correction — the same harmony slowed down with the blues and the church put back into it.

    Art Blakey & The Jazz Messengers — Moanin' gateway

    recorded 1958, released 1959 · Blue Note BLP 4003 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Moanin'”

    Lee Morgan (tp) · Benny Golson (ts) · Bobby Timmons (p) · Jymie Merritt (b) · Art Blakey (d)

    The record that fixed what hard bop sounded like: a gospel head everyone can sing, Golson's arranging, Morgan at twenty playing like he has nothing to lose, and Blakey underneath refusing to be background.

    Listen for: The opening: Timmons plays a churchy piano phrase and the horns answer 'amen'. That call-and-response is lifted from Black church practice and dropped whole into jazz.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  7. Hard bop gives a soloist a chord every two beats. Here Miles Davis takes almost all of them away and finds out what is left to play.

    Miles Davis — Kind of Blue gateway

    recorded/released 1959 · Columbia CL 1355 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “So What”

    Miles Davis (tp) · John Coltrane (ts) · Cannonball Adderley (as) · Bill Evans (p) · Wynton Kelly (p) · Paul Chambers (b) · Jimmy Cobb (d)

    The best-selling jazz album ever made, and it earns it. Davis brought sketches rather than charts to two sessions and the band read them nearly cold, which is why nothing sounds worked over.

    Listen for: 'So What' gives the soloist two chords in thirty-two bars. Listen to how Coltrane and Adderley respond completely differently to the same emptiness — one fills it, one plays around it.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  8. Coltrane was on the previous record. Five years later he takes the same reduced harmony and aims it at something other than music.

    John Coltrane — A Love Supreme gateway

    recorded 1964, released 1965 · Impulse! A-77 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Acknowledgement”

    John Coltrane (ts, voc) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Jimmy Garrison (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

    A four-part suite offered explicitly as a religious statement, recorded in one session in December 1964. It gave the whole idiom its structure, its intent and its permission.

    Listen for: The four-note bass figure in 'Acknowledgement' is the whole record's seed. Coltrane plays it in every key before finally singing it as words — the melody becomes the mantra.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  9. Davis again, and the instruments have changed: electric piano, electric guitar, and eighth notes that no longer swing.

    Miles Davis — In a Silent Way core

    recorded/released 1969 · Columbia CS 9875 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “In a Silent Way / It's About That Time”

    Miles Davis (tp) · Wayne Shorter (ss) · John McLaughlin (g) · Herbie Hancock (elp) · Chick Corea (elp) · Joe Zawinul (org) · Dave Holland (b) · Tony Williams (d)

    Quieter and stranger than Bitches Brew, and the first place the electric approach fully works. Two side-long pieces built by Macero from short takes, with Tony Williams playing only the hi-hat for long stretches.

    Listen for: The opening and closing sections of the title suite are the same take, used twice. Macero built the record's shape in the edit, and Davis approved it.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  10. Hancock played electric piano on that record. Four years later he builds a band where the groove is the composition and the solo is a passenger.

    Herbie Hancock — Head Hunters gateway

    recorded/released 1973 · Columbia KC 32731 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Chameleon”

    Herbie Hancock (keys, clavinet, synths) · Bennie Maupin (reeds) · Paul Jackson (elb) · Harvey Mason (d) · Bill Summers (percussion)

    Hancock deliberately built a band around a groove rather than around soloing, and it became one of the best-selling jazz albums ever made. Everything in jazz-funk after this is measured against it.

    Listen for: 'Chameleon' is one bass line for fifteen minutes. The clavinet and the synth bass do all the harmonic work and nothing modulates.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  11. A generation decides electricity was a wrong turn, goes back to acoustic instruments, and plays them with conservatory precision and considerable force.

    Wynton Marsalis — Black Codes (From the Underground) gateway

    recorded/released 1985 · Columbia FC 40009 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Black Codes”

    Wynton Marsalis (tp) · Branford Marsalis (ts, ss) · Kenny Kirkland (p) · Charnett Moffett (b) · Jeff 'Tain' Watts (d)

    The strongest record of the movement's first phase, made by a working band of contemporaries rather than by a young leader with veteran sidemen. The writing is genuinely difficult and the playing is ferocious.

    Listen for: Jeff 'Tain' Watts plays with Tony Williams' elasticity but hits considerably harder. The Miles quintet is the model and the temperature is completely different.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  12. And a generation after that decides the argument was never worth having, and builds jazz out of Afrobeat, dub and grime instead.

    Various Artists — We Out Here gateway

    recorded 2017, released 2018 · Brownswood BWOOD0180 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Black Skin, Black Masks (Theon Cross)”

    Shabaka Hutchings (curator, ts) · Nubya Garcia (ts) · Moses Boyd (d) · Theon Cross (tuba) · Ezra Collective · Joe Armon-Jones (keys)

    Nine tracks recorded in three days with a shared pool of musicians, assembled by Shabaka Hutchings for Gilles Peterson's label. It is the scene introducing itself, deliberately, as a scene.

    Listen for: The same players recur across tracks in different combinations. That overlap is the point — this is a community with a shared rhythmic language, not a compilation of unrelated acts.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

The Full Arc 30 records

Thirty records, ragtime to now

The historical spine in order. Some of it is hard going and that is deliberate — this is the route that shows you how each thing came out of the last one, including the parts that came out of an argument.

  1. Before jazz: composed piano music with the syncopation written in, no improvising, no blues. Everything downstream inherits the rhythmic habit and discards the rest.

    Joshua Rifkin — Piano Rags by Scott Joplin gateway

    recorded/released 1970 · Nonesuch H-71248 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Maple Leaf Rag”

    Joshua Rifkin (p)

    A musicologist played Joplin slowly, quietly and exactly as written, treating the rags as concert repertoire rather than saloon music — which is what Joplin had asked for and never got. It became one of Nonesuch's best-selling records and started the ragtime revival single-handedly.

    Listen for: The left hand never varies its oom-pah while the right keeps landing between the beats; that friction is the entire idea, and at Rifkin's unhurried tempo you can hear it clearly.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  2. The first jazz record, made by a white New Orleans band who then claimed to have invented the music. Hold both of those facts at once.

    Original Dixieland Jazz Band — The Complete Original Dixieland Jazz Band 1917–1936 core

    recorded 1917, released 1996 · RCA Bluebird ND 82483 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Livery Stable Blues”

    Nick LaRocca (cor) · Larry Shields (cl) · Eddie Edwards (tb) · Henry Ragas (p) · Tony Sbarbaro (d)

    The first jazz record, in February 1917, made by a white band from New Orleans who then claimed to have invented the music. Both the musical and the historical facts here matter, and they point in different directions.

    Listen for: The energy is real and the collective front line works, but compare the rhythmic looseness to Oliver's band six years later — the difference is what the record industry took twelve years to document.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  3. In Harlem, ragtime's left hand gets loosened and swung and turned into a competitive sport at rent parties.

    James P. Johnson — Carolina Shout gateway

    recorded 1921, released 1991 · Biograph BCD 105 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Carolina Shout”

    James P. Johnson (p)

    The founding stride record and the test piece every Harlem pianist had to learn — Ellington taught himself by slowing down Johnson's piano roll and copying the fingering off the keys.

    Listen for: The left hand jumps a tenth or more between the bass note and the chord, on every beat, at speed. That leap is the style's name and its physical difficulty.

    Note: 'Carolina Shout' was cut as a piano roll in 1918 and as a disc in October 1921; Biograph collections gather both.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  4. Back to New Orleans, properly this time: three horns improvising simultaneously, and a second cornet player who will not stay in the background much longer.

    King Oliver's Creole Jazz Band — King Oliver's Jazz Band 1923 core

    recorded 1923, released 1974 · Smithsonian Collection R 001 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Dippermouth Blues”

    King Oliver (cor) · Louis Armstrong (cor) · Johnny Dodds (cl) · Honoré Dutrey (tb) · Lil Hardin (p) · Baby Dodds (d)

    The best documentation of a working New Orleans band with two cornets, recorded before the style was diluted. Armstrong is the second cornet, playing harmony parts, three years before he takes over.

    Listen for: The two cornets play worked-out breaks in perfect unison, apparently improvised. They were not — Oliver signalled them in advance, and the illusion is the point.

    Note: Recorded 1923 for Gennett, OKeh, Columbia and Paramount; multiple later compilations exist. Catalogue given here is the widely cited Smithsonian reissue.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  5. That second cornet player. Across three years the collective front line stops being the point and the soloist becomes it.

    Louis Armstrong — The Complete Hot Five and Hot Seven Recordings gateway

    recorded 1925, released 2000 · Columbia/Legacy C4K 63527 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “West End Blues”

    Louis Armstrong (cor, tp, voc) · Kid Ory (tb) · Johnny Dodds (cl) · Lil Hardin Armstrong (p) · Johnny St. Cyr (bj)

    The single most consequential body of recordings in jazz. Across 1925–28 you can hear the music stop being an ensemble art and become a soloist's art, in real time, in one man's playing.

    Listen for: On 'West End Blues' the unaccompanied opening cadenza announces that a soloist can now command a record on his own. Nothing before 1928 sounds like that.

    Note: Recorded 1925–1928; the Columbia/Legacy four-CD set collecting them was issued in 2000. Earlier equivalent collections exist.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  6. The first evidence that this music could be rebuilt somewhere else: Paris, no drums, no horns, and a guitar language from Romani string tradition.

    Django Reinhardt & Stéphane Grappelli — Djangologie 1936–1937 gateway

    recorded 1936, released 2009 · Frémeaux & Associés FA 303 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Minor Swing” · medium confidence

    Django Reinhardt (g) · Stéphane Grappelli (vln) · Joseph Reinhardt (g) · Roger Chaput (g) · Louis Vola (b)

    The Quintette du Hot Club de France at its peak, with the classic all-string line-up. 'Minor Swing', 'Djangology' and 'Nuages' are here and everything the idiom later became is already fully formed.

    Listen for: There is no drummer. The two rhythm guitars playing 'la pompe' — a hard chop with the chord instantly damped — do the entire job of a kit.

    Note: The Quintette's 1930s sides appear in many reissue configurations; the Frémeaux 'Djangologie' series is the most complete and best documented.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  7. Meanwhile in New York, a singer and a tenor player work out how to have a conversation across a mediocre pop song.

    Billie Holiday — The Quintessential Billie Holiday, Volume 3 (1936–1937) gateway

    recorded 1936, released 1987 · Columbia CK 44048 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “This Year's Kisses”

    Billie Holiday (voc) · Lester Young (ts) · Teddy Wilson (p) · Buck Clayton (tp) · Freddie Green (g) · Jo Jones (d)

    The Teddy Wilson small-group sides are the most perfect singer-and-band records in jazz. Holiday and Lester Young answer each other phrase by phrase, and the material is often disposable pop that they simply transform.

    Listen for: Follow the conversation between voice and tenor. Young plays around her line rather than under it, and neither of them ever raises their voice.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  8. Kansas City arrives and changes the floor everyone is standing on — an even four, timekeeping moved to the cymbals, and enormous space.

    Count Basie — The Original American Decca Recordings gateway

    recorded 1937, released 1992 · GRP/Decca GRD-3-611 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “One O'Clock Jump”

    Count Basie (p) · Lester Young (ts) · Herschel Evans (ts) · Buck Clayton (tp) · Freddie Green (g) · Walter Page (b) · Jo Jones (d) · Jimmy Rushing (voc)

    The 1937–39 Decca sides are the Kansas City rhythm section in its first flush, and they changed how every band in America kept time. Lester Young's solos here are the other half of the reason this matters.

    Listen for: The rhythm section floats rather than pushes. Freddie Green plays quarter notes on guitar and never solos in his fifty years with the band; Jo Jones moves the pulse to the hi-hat.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  9. The peak of the written tradition, by a composer who wrote for specific people rather than for parts, which is why nobody else can play it properly.

    Duke Ellington — The Blanton–Webster Band gateway

    recorded 1940, released 1986 · RCA Bluebird 5659-2-RB · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Ko-Ko”

    Duke Ellington (p, comp) · Billy Strayhorn (comp, arr) · Jimmy Blanton (b) · Ben Webster (ts) · Johnny Hodges (as) · Cootie Williams (tp) · Tricky Sam Nanton (tb)

    The 1940–42 band is the peak of orchestral jazz writing. Ellington had Strayhorn, a bassist who reinvented the instrument's role, and a saxophone section he had been writing for individually for fifteen years.

    Listen for: 'Ko-Ko' is built almost entirely from a minor blues and short repeated figures, and it is genuinely dark — closer to Bartók's sense of orchestration than to dance music.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  10. And the reaction: the big band abandoned, the dance floor abandoned, the tempo doubled, and the harmony made deliberately harder to follow.

    Charlie Parker — The Complete Savoy and Dial Studio Recordings gateway

    recorded 1944, released 2000 · Savoy Jazz 92911-2 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Ko-Ko”

    Charlie Parker (as) · Dizzy Gillespie (tp) · Miles Davis (tp) · Bud Powell (p) · Max Roach (d) · Duke Jordan (p) · Tommy Potter (b)

    The 1945–48 Savoy and Dial sides are bebop's primary text. They are short, most are three minutes, and the alternate takes let you hear a solo being reconsidered from scratch each time.

    Listen for: On 'Ko-Ko' the head is played at a tempo nobody had attempted, and Parker's first chorus lands on the flattened fifth of every chord as though it were consonant. In 1945 it was not.

    Note: Recorded 1944–48 for Savoy and Dial; multiple box-set configurations exist under different catalogue numbers.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  11. Four years later the same harmony is played at half the temperature, with written counterpoint and a French horn, by people including a very young Miles Davis.

    Miles Davis — Birth of the Cool gateway

    recorded 1949, released 1957 · Capitol T 762 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Boplicity”

    Miles Davis (tp) · Gerry Mulligan (bs, arr) · Lee Konitz (as) · J.J. Johnson (tb) · Gunther Schuller (frh) · Bill Barber (tuba) · Gil Evans (arr) · John Lewis (arr) · Max Roach (d) · Kenny Clarke (d)

    Nine players including French horn and tuba, arranged as chamber music, recorded across three sessions in 1949–50. The sides sold poorly and then reorganised a decade of jazz.

    Listen for: 'Boplicity' — the ensemble breathes as one instrument, and the voicings move in ways that no big band section writing had attempted. Gil Evans arranged it.

    Note: Recorded January 1949, April 1949 and March 1950; collected as a Capitol LP under this title in 1957.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  12. A different reaction to bebop entirely: keep the harmony and the soloing, replace the swing feel with clave, and the whole rhythmic basis changes.

    Machito and His Afro-Cubans — Afro-Cuban Jazz Suite gateway

    recorded 1950, released 1952 · Mercury (Clef series) MG C-505 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Afro-Cuban Jazz Suite” · medium confidence

    Machito (maracas, voc) · Mario Bauzá (mus. dir) · Charlie Parker (as) · Flip Phillips (ts) · Buddy Rich (d) · Chico O'Farrill (comp, arr)

    Chico O'Farrill's five-movement suite with Charlie Parker soloing over an authentic Cuban rhythm section. It is the meeting stated at full length rather than as a novelty single.

    Listen for: Parker plays exactly as he would over bebop changes, and the rhythm section does not adjust to him at all. Both languages stay intact and the friction is the record.

    Note: Recorded in New York on 21 December 1950; first issued on Mercury's Clef series as MG C-505 and reissued repeatedly on Clef, Norgran and Verve through the 1950s, so the release year is given variously as 1951-1957.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  13. Back in New York the correction to cool jazz is heat — gospel chords, a backbeat, and tunes you can actually hum.

    Horace Silver and the Jazz Messengers — Horace Silver and the Jazz Messengers core

    recorded 1954, released 1956 · Blue Note BLP 1518 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “The Preacher”

    Horace Silver (p) · Kenny Dorham (tp) · Hank Mobley (ts) · Doug Watkins (b) · Art Blakey (d)

    The band that became the Jazz Messengers, with Silver's tunes — 'The Preacher', 'Doodlin'' — that made gospel and blues content the point rather than a garnish.

    Listen for: 'The Preacher' is built on a sixteen-bar structure closer to a hymn than to a jazz form, and Blue Note's Alfred Lion initially thought it was too old-fashioned to release.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  14. In Rio, musicians listening to the cool jazz records take the restraint, add samba and much harder harmony, and invent something Brazil still argues about.

    João Gilberto — Chega de Saudade core

    recorded 1958, released 1959 · Odeon MOFB 3073 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Chega de Saudade”

    João Gilberto (g, voc) · Antônio Carlos Jobim (arr, cond)

    The record that started it. Gilberto's guitar pattern and vocal delivery arrive fully formed, and Brazilian popular music divides into before and after.

    Listen for: The rhythm is displaced across two bars rather than repeating every bar, which is why it never sounds mechanical. Count two bars, not one.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  15. Davis's answer to hard bop's congestion: take away nearly all the chords and see what a soloist does with the silence.

    Miles Davis — Kind of Blue gateway

    recorded/released 1959 · Columbia CL 1355 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “So What”

    Miles Davis (tp) · John Coltrane (ts) · Cannonball Adderley (as) · Bill Evans (p) · Wynton Kelly (p) · Paul Chambers (b) · Jimmy Cobb (d)

    The best-selling jazz album ever made, and it earns it. Davis brought sketches rather than charts to two sessions and the band read them nearly cold, which is why nothing sounds worked over.

    Listen for: 'So What' gives the soloist two chords in thirty-two bars. Listen to how Coltrane and Adderley respond completely differently to the same emptiness — one fills it, one plays around it.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  16. The same year, in a different city, someone takes away the chords entirely and keeps the tune. The reception was hostile and personal.

    Ornette Coleman — The Shape of Jazz to Come gateway

    recorded/released 1959 · Atlantic SD 1317 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Lonely Woman”

    Ornette Coleman (as) · Don Cherry (cor) · Charlie Haden (b) · Billy Higgins (d)

    The most approachable free jazz record ever made. The melodies are singable, the pulse is steady, and there is simply no chord sequence — which is the only thing that was actually removed.

    Listen for: 'Lonely Woman' has a clear, mournful theme that anyone can follow. Then notice that the bass and horns are not agreeing on a tempo, and no piano is telling you what key you are in.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  17. None of that argument reached the neighbourhood bars, where the Hammond organ was doing perfectly well and the audience answered out loud.

    Jimmy Smith — Back at the Chicken Shack gateway

    recorded 1960, released 1963 · Blue Note BLP 4117 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Back at the Chicken Shack”

    Jimmy Smith (org) · Stanley Turrentine (ts) · Kenny Burrell (g) · Donald Bailey (d)

    The organ trio plus tenor, at its most relaxed. Nothing is being proved and the groove never stops, which is precisely the format's proposition.

    Listen for: There is no bass player. Smith is playing the bass line with his left hand and the pedals while soloing with his right — listen for the walk continuing underneath the solo.

    Note: Recorded April 1960 in the same session block as 'Midnight Special'; Blue Note held it until 1963.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  18. Musicians who heard Ornette Coleman and went partway: forms that will not resolve, harmony that will not declare a key, and a rhythm section that argues back.

    Wayne Shorter — Speak No Evil core

    recorded 1964, released 1966 · Blue Note BLP 4194 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Infant Eyes”

    Wayne Shorter (ts) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Herbie Hancock (p) · Ron Carter (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

    Shorter's compositions at their most perfectly balanced between hard bop's directness and post-bop's ambiguity. Every tune here is still played, and none of them analyse cleanly.

    Listen for: 'Infant Eyes' has a thirteen-bar form — an odd number that you will not notice until you try to count it. The asymmetry is what makes the melody feel suspended.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  19. Coltrane in the same year, using the reduced harmony for something else — a suite structured as a religious offering, with a chant where the head should be.

    John Coltrane — A Love Supreme gateway

    recorded 1964, released 1965 · Impulse! A-77 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Acknowledgement”

    John Coltrane (ts, voc) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Jimmy Garrison (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

    A four-part suite offered explicitly as a religious statement, recorded in one session in December 1964. It gave the whole idiom its structure, its intent and its permission.

    Listen for: The four-note bass figure in 'Acknowledgement' is the whole record's seed. Coltrane plays it in every key before finally singing it as words — the melody becomes the mantra.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  20. Chicago's answer to New York's free jazz: a musicians' collective, composed sections, small instruments, and long stretches where almost nothing happens.

    Roscoe Mitchell Sextet — Sound core

    recorded/released 1966 · Delmark DS-408 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Sound”

    Roscoe Mitchell (as, cl, recorder) · Lester Bowie (tp) · Lester Lashley (tb, cello) · Malachi Favors (b) · Alvin Fielder (d) · Maurice McIntyre (ts)

    The first AACM record, and the first jazz album to treat silence as a compositional material rather than an absence. It sounds nothing like the New York free jazz of the same year.

    Listen for: The title track has stretches where almost nothing happens for a long time. That was a deliberate rejection of the continuous-blowing model, and it defined the Chicago approach.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  21. Davis's second quintet holding a pulse while treating the harmony as negotiable — the last acoustic thing he did before plugging in.

    Miles Davis — Miles Smiles gateway

    recorded 1966, released 1967 · Columbia CL 2601 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Footprints”

    Miles Davis (tp) · Wayne Shorter (ts) · Herbie Hancock (p) · Ron Carter (b) · Tony Williams (d)

    The Second Great Quintet's clearest statement of 'time, no changes' — the pulse holds while the harmony becomes negotiable. Hancock lays out for long stretches so nothing states the chord at all.

    Listen for: On 'Footprints' the bass line implies one harmony and the piano implies another, while Williams shifts between a 6/4 and a 4/4 feel underneath. It never resolves and never falls apart.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  22. By now the music has left the country for good: Tokyo takes the modal template and plays it harder, recorded better than almost anything in America.

    Terumasa Hino — Hi-Nology gateway

    recorded/released 1969 · Columbia (Japan) YS-10022-J · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Hi-Nology”

    Terumasa Hino (tp) · Hiromasa Suzuki (p) · Kunimitsu Inaba (b) · Motohiko Hino (d) · Takeru Muraoka (ts)

    The record that announced Japanese modern jazz as its own thing rather than an import. Modal, hard-driving, and recorded with a punch that American records of the same year rarely had.

    Listen for: The title track sits on a vamp and builds by sheer accumulation. Motohiko Hino's drumming is heavier and more rock-inflected than any Blue Note session of 1969.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  23. Davis plugs in, hires thirteen people, and lets a producer assemble the record out of tape splices afterwards. The argument this started lasted twenty years.

    Miles Davis — Bitches Brew gateway

    recorded 1969, released 1970 · Columbia GP 26 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “Miles Runs the Voodoo Down”

    Miles Davis (tp) · Wayne Shorter (ss) · Bennie Maupin (bcl) · Chick Corea (elp) · Joe Zawinul (elp) · John McLaughlin (g) · Dave Holland (b) · Harvey Brooks (elb) · Jack DeJohnette (d) · Lenny White (d) · Airto Moreira (percussion)

    The record that made fusion a commercial and critical event. Thirteen musicians, three keyboards, two drummers, and Teo Macero assembling the released tracks from tape splices after the fact.

    Listen for: The harmony barely moves for twenty minutes at a time. What changes is density and texture — musicians entering and dropping out — and the editing that Macero imposed afterwards.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  24. One of those thirteen forms a band where the groove is the composition, sells enormously, and gets called a sell-out for it.

    Herbie Hancock — Head Hunters gateway

    recorded/released 1973 · Columbia KC 32731 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Chameleon”

    Herbie Hancock (keys, clavinet, synths) · Bennie Maupin (reeds) · Paul Jackson (elb) · Harvey Mason (d) · Bill Summers (percussion)

    Hancock deliberately built a band around a groove rather than around soloing, and it became one of the best-selling jazz albums ever made. Everything in jazz-funk after this is measured against it.

    Listen for: 'Chameleon' is one bass line for fifteen minutes. The clavinet and the synth bass do all the harmonic work and nothing modulates.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  25. In Europe an entirely different reaction to the same decade: one man, one out-of-tune piano, no plan, and no volume at all.

    Keith Jarrett — The Köln Concert gateway

    recorded/released 1975 · ECM ECM 1064/65 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Part I”

    Keith Jarrett (p)

    The best-selling solo piano album ever made, improvised in one sitting on a badly out-of-tune house piano that Jarrett nearly refused to play. He worked around the instrument's faults and the record is shaped by them.

    Listen for: He avoids the piano's weak upper register and dead pedals by staying in the middle and using ostinatos. The limitation produced the style.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  26. And in New York the avant-garde, priced out of every club, rents lofts and puts on its own concerts. This is what a weeknight sounded like.

    Various Artists — Wildflowers: The New York Loft Jazz Sessions gateway

    recorded 1976, released 1977 · Douglas NBLP 7045-7049 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Ismell (Sam Rivers Trio)”

    Sam Rivers (ts, fl) · David Murray (ts) · Anthony Braxton (reeds) · Julius Hemphill (as) · Randy Weston (p) · Andrew Cyrille (d) · Oliver Lake (as)

    Five LPs recorded at Studio Rivbea over ten days in May 1976, capturing almost a hundred musicians in a room that musicians owned. It is the scene's only comprehensive document.

    Listen for: The sound of the room and the audience is left in. These are not concert recordings or studio dates — they are a loft, on a weeknight, with about forty people present.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  27. The counter-revolution: acoustic instruments, formidable technique, and a public argument that the previous fifteen years had been a mistake.

    Wynton Marsalis — Black Codes (From the Underground) gateway

    recorded/released 1985 · Columbia FC 40009 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Black Codes”

    Wynton Marsalis (tp) · Branford Marsalis (ts, ss) · Kenny Kirkland (p) · Charnett Moffett (b) · Jeff 'Tain' Watts (d)

    The strongest record of the movement's first phase, made by a working band of contemporaries rather than by a young leader with veteran sidemen. The writing is genuinely difficult and the playing is ferocious.

    Listen for: Jeff 'Tain' Watts plays with Tony Williams' elasticity but hits considerably harder. The Miles quintet is the model and the temperature is completely different.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  28. While that argument runs, producers in the same city are lifting two-bar loops off the jazz records nobody was buying and building something else out of them.

    A Tribe Called Quest — The Low End Theory gateway

    recorded/released 1991 · Jive 1418-2-J · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Excursions”

    Q-Tip (MC, prod) · Phife Dawg (MC) · Ali Shaheed Muhammad (DJ) · Ron Carter (b)

    The record that proved jazz samples and rapping were not a novelty pairing. Sparse, bass-forward, and with Ron Carter playing upright on one track at Q-Tip's request.

    Listen for: There is almost nothing in the mix — a bass line, drums, one sample, two voices. The restraint is what makes the low end sound so enormous.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  29. Twenty years on, musicians raised on both sides of that split make a three-hour spiritual jazz record with a choir and put it out on an electronic label.

    Kamasi Washington — The Epic gateway

    recorded 2011, released 2015 · Brainfeeder BF050 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Change of the Guard”

    Kamasi Washington (ts) · Cameron Graves (p) · Miles Mosley (b) · Thundercat (elb) · Ronald Bruner Jr. (d) · Tony Austin (d) · Patrice Quinn (voc) · 32-piece orchestra · 20-voice choir

    Three hours, seventeen tracks, choir and strings, released on an electronic label and reaching an audience that had not been listening to jazz. The 1970s spiritual jazz ambition returns without a hint of irony.

    Listen for: Two drummers play throughout, panned left and right, playing different parts. The choir functions as an orchestral section, not as a garnish.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  30. And in London, a scene that came up through a free youth programme plays jazz for people who are standing up, on rhythms from Lagos, Kingston and Port of Spain.

    Various Artists — We Out Here gateway

    recorded 2017, released 2018 · Brownswood BWOOD0180 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “Black Skin, Black Masks (Theon Cross)”

    Shabaka Hutchings (curator, ts) · Nubya Garcia (ts) · Moses Boyd (d) · Theon Cross (tuba) · Ezra Collective · Joe Armon-Jones (keys)

    Nine tracks recorded in three days with a shared pool of musicians, assembled by Shabaka Hutchings for Gilles Peterson's label. It is the scene introducing itself, deliberately, as a scene.

    Listen for: The same players recur across tracks in different combinations. That overlap is the point — this is a community with a shared rhythmic language, not a compilation of unrelated acts.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

Into the Deep End 15 records

Fifteen records that get progressively harder

Free, spiritual and avant-garde music, sequenced so each record teaches your ear what it needs for the next one. Do not start at the bottom of this list. It will not go well.

  1. Start with a song you already know, stretched to thirteen minutes over two chords. Nothing here is strange yet; you are just learning to wait.

    John Coltrane — My Favorite Things deep

    recorded 1960, released 1961 · Atlantic SD 1361 · difficulty 1/5 · start with “My Favorite Things”

    John Coltrane (ss, ts) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Steve Davis (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

    A Broadway waltz turned into a thirteen-minute modal vamp, and the record that made the soprano saxophone a jazz instrument again after Bechet.

    Listen for: The tune alternates between E minor and E major and Coltrane simply stays in each for as long as he wants. The song's form has become a switch, not a structure.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  2. Now the avant-garde with a bass line and a backbeat, so you can hear that free playing and a groove are not opposites.

    Art Ensemble of Chicago — Les Stances à Sophie gateway

    recorded/released 1970 · Nessa N-4 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Theme de Yoyo”

    Lester Bowie (tp) · Roscoe Mitchell (reeds) · Joseph Jarman (reeds) · Malachi Favors (b) · Don Moye (percussion) · Fontella Bass (voc, p)

    A film soundtrack recorded in Paris, and the most immediately enjoyable thing the Art Ensemble made. 'Theme de Yoyo' has a bass line, a backbeat and Fontella Bass singing, and it is still recognisably them.

    Listen for: 'Theme de Yoyo' is a funk record made by an avant-garde collective, and the free playing sits on top of the groove without disturbing it.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  3. Keep the singable melody and the steady pulse, and remove one thing only: the chord sequence. That single subtraction is what all of this is built on.

    Ornette Coleman — The Shape of Jazz to Come gateway

    recorded/released 1959 · Atlantic SD 1317 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Lonely Woman”

    Ornette Coleman (as) · Don Cherry (cor) · Charlie Haden (b) · Billy Higgins (d)

    The most approachable free jazz record ever made. The melodies are singable, the pulse is steady, and there is simply no chord sequence — which is the only thing that was actually removed.

    Listen for: 'Lonely Woman' has a clear, mournful theme that anyone can follow. Then notice that the bass and horns are not agreeing on a tempo, and no piano is telling you what key you are in.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  4. Same reduction, aimed at devotion rather than at freedom — a four-note figure and a mantra holding forty minutes together.

    John Coltrane — A Love Supreme gateway

    recorded 1964, released 1965 · Impulse! A-77 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Acknowledgement”

    John Coltrane (ts, voc) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Jimmy Garrison (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

    A four-part suite offered explicitly as a religious statement, recorded in one session in December 1964. It gave the whole idiom its structure, its intent and its permission.

    Listen for: The four-note bass figure in 'Acknowledgement' is the whole record's seed. Coltrane plays it in every key before finally singing it as words — the melody becomes the mantra.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  5. Now remove the chord changes entirely and replace them with a drone, and add a harp. Your ear stops looking for movement and starts listening to colour.

    Alice Coltrane — Journey in Satchidananda core

    recorded 1970, released 1971 · Impulse! AS-9203 · difficulty 2/5 · start with “Journey in Satchidananda”

    Alice Coltrane (harp, p) · Pharoah Sanders (ss) · Cecil McBee (b) · Rashied Ali (d) · Tulsi (tanpura) · Majid Shabazz (bells)

    The harp, the tanpura drone and the Hindu devotional framework in one record, made by a musician who was practising what she was playing. Nothing else in jazz sounds like this.

    Listen for: The tanpura drone under everything is not an effect — it is the harmonic foundation, and it means the music has no chord changes at all, only a fixed centre.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  6. A gentle pentatonic melody that goes somewhere violent and comes back. This is your first taste of an ecstatic build, with a safe route home.

    Pharoah Sanders — Karma core

    recorded/released 1969 · Impulse! AS-9181 · difficulty 3/5 · start with “The Creator Has a Master Plan”

    Pharoah Sanders (ts) · Leon Thomas (voc) · Lonnie Liston Smith (p) · Reggie Workman (b) · Julius Watkins (frh) · Billy Hart (d)

    'The Creator Has a Master Plan' runs thirty-two minutes and moves from a serene pentatonic melody to total collective violence and back. It is the idiom's defining shape in one track.

    Listen for: Leon Thomas's yodelling — a technique he adapted from Central African pygmy singing — is doing something no jazz vocalist had attempted.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  7. Back to a composed record, but everything wobbles — no piano stating the harmony, and a drummer playing around the beat rather than on it.

    Eric Dolphy — Out to Lunch! core

    recorded/released 1964 · Blue Note BLP 4163 · difficulty 4/5 · start with “Hat and Beard”

    Eric Dolphy (as, bcl, fl) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Bobby Hutcherson (vib) · Richard Davis (b) · Tony Williams (d)

    Dolphy's last studio album as leader, recorded four months before he died at thirty-six. There is no piano; the vibraphone leaves the harmony deliberately unstated and everything wobbles.

    Listen for: Tony Williams plays around the beat rather than on it for the entire record, and Hutcherson answers with chords that never confirm a key. The instability is composed, not accidental.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  8. Two quartets improvising at once, one in each speaker. Follow one at a time until you can hear both, then decide what you think about it.

    Ornette Coleman Double Quartet — Free Jazz: A Collective Improvisation core

    recorded 1960, released 1961 · Atlantic SD 1364 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Free Jazz”

    Ornette Coleman (as) · Eric Dolphy (bcl) · Don Cherry (pocket tp) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Charlie Haden (b) · Scott LaFaro (b) · Billy Higgins (d) · Ed Blackwell (d)

    Two quartets, one in each stereo channel, improvising together for thirty-seven unbroken minutes with only brief written cues. It gave the movement its name and remains its most extreme statement of collective method.

    Listen for: Play it with headphones. The two quartets are completely separated left and right, and you can follow one band at a time or hear them collide.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  9. Now the melodies get simpler and the playing gets more extreme. It is a hymn and a howl, and the tune always survives.

    Albert Ayler Trio — Spiritual Unity core

    recorded 1964, released 1965 · ESP-Disk ESP 1002 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Ghosts: First Variation”

    Albert Ayler (ts) · Gary Peacock (b) · Sunny Murray (d)

    Thirty minutes, three players, no chords, no meter, and melodies that sound like nursery rhymes or Salvation Army hymns played by someone in distress. It is the most emotionally extreme record in jazz.

    Listen for: 'Ghosts' has a simple, almost childish tune. Ayler states it plainly and then destroys it with a vibrato so wide it becomes a howl, then returns to the tune intact.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  10. Eleven players alternating between collective density and single solos. If you can hear that alternation, the wall becomes a structure.

    John Coltrane — Ascension core

    recorded 1965, released 1966 · Impulse! A-95 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Ascension (Edition II)”

    John Coltrane (ts) · Pharoah Sanders (ts) · Archie Shepp (ts) · Marion Brown (as) · John Tchicai (as) · Freddie Hubbard (tp) · Dewey Johnson (tp) · McCoy Tyner (p) · Jimmy Garrison (b) · Art Davis (b) · Elvin Jones (d)

    Eleven musicians, forty minutes, alternating between full collective improvisation and individual solos. Coltrane's audience followed him here, which is why free jazz got a hearing it would not otherwise have had.

    Listen for: The structure is simple and audible: massed ensemble, then a solo, then massed ensemble again. Once you hear the alternation the wall of sound becomes navigable.

    Note: Two complete takes exist; Impulse! substituted Edition II for Edition I shortly after release.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  11. A completely different route to freedom: atonal, percussive and organised by written units rather than by feel. Nothing about this comes from the blues.

    Cecil Taylor — Unit Structures core

    recorded/released 1966 · Blue Note BLP 4237 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Steps”

    Cecil Taylor (p) · Jimmy Lyons (as) · Ken McIntyre (as, ob, bcl) · Henry Grimes (b) · Alan Silva (b) · Andrew Cyrille (d)

    Taylor's densest and most organised record, on a major label, with his own written notes explaining the structural units. It is atonal, percussive, and completely unlike Coleman's version of freedom.

    Listen for: The piano is being played as a percussion instrument — clusters, forearms, extreme velocity — but the pitch material is organised, not random. Follow one interval and you will hear it return.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  12. Chicago's opposite proposition — silence and space as the material, and a refusal to keep blowing simply because the tape is running.

    Roscoe Mitchell Sextet — Sound core

    recorded/released 1966 · Delmark DS-408 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Sound”

    Roscoe Mitchell (as, cl, recorder) · Lester Bowie (tp) · Lester Lashley (tb, cello) · Malachi Favors (b) · Alvin Fielder (d) · Maurice McIntyre (ts)

    The first AACM record, and the first jazz album to treat silence as a compositional material rather than an absence. It sounds nothing like the New York free jazz of the same year.

    Listen for: The title track has stretches where almost nothing happens for a long time. That was a deliberate rejection of the continuous-blowing model, and it defined the Chicago approach.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  13. Europe rejecting all of the above, including the blues and the pulse, at maximum volume for the entire record. There is a written theme; find it.

    Peter Brötzmann Octet — Machine Gun gateway

    recorded/released 1968 · BRÖ (self-released), later FMP BRÖ 2 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Machine Gun”

    Peter Brötzmann (ts) · Evan Parker (ts) · Willem Breuker (ts) · Fred Van Hove (p) · Peter Kowald (b) · Buschi Niebergall (b) · Han Bennink (d) · Sven-Åke Johansson (d)

    The line in the sand. Eight players at maximum volume for the whole record, self-released because no label would touch it. It is not the gentlest introduction but it is the clearest statement of what European free music was refusing.

    Listen for: There is a written theme — a repeated stabbing figure — that returns at intervals. Once you hear it, the record stops being noise and becomes a structure.

    Note: Originally self-issued on Brötzmann's own BRÖ label in 1968 and later reissued by FMP.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  14. One saxophone, no breathing pauses, producing several simultaneous lines. By now you should be listening to texture rather than for melody.

    Evan Parker — Monoceros core

    recorded/released 1978 · Incus Incus 27 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Monoceros I”

    Evan Parker (ss)

    Solo soprano saxophone, direct to disc, using circular breathing and multiphonics to produce continuous overlapping lines from one horn. There is no jazz antecedent for this technique.

    Listen for: He never stops to breathe, and you will hear two or three simultaneous lines from a monophonic instrument. It is a physical achievement and a compositional one.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase

  15. Finish where there is nothing left to hold: tenor and drums, no bass, no harmony, no meter. Recorded five months before he died.

    John Coltrane — Interstellar Space deep

    recorded 1967, released 1974 · Impulse! ASD-9277 · difficulty 5/5 · start with “Mars”

    John Coltrane (ts) · Rashied Ali (d)

    Duets for tenor saxophone and drums, recorded in February 1967, five months before Coltrane's death. There is nothing left to hold onto — no bass, no harmony, no meter.

    Listen for: Each piece is named for a planet and starts with bells. Ali is not keeping time; he is producing a continuous field that Coltrane moves through.

    Spotify · Apple Music · NetEase
